Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge with Applications to Score-Based Generative Modeling
Valentin De Bortoli, James Thornton, Jeremy Heng, Arnaud Doucet

Abstract
Progressively applying Gaussian noise transforms complex data distributions to approximately Gaussian. Reversing this dynamic defines a generative model. When the forward noising process is given by a Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE), Song et al., 2021 demonstrate how the time inhomogeneous drift of the associated reverse-time SDE may be estimated using score-matching. A limitation of this approach is that the forward-time SDE must be run for a sufficiently long time for the final distribution to be approximately Gaussian while ensuring that the corresponding time-discretization error is controlled. In contrast, solving the Schrödinger Bridge (SB) problem, i.e. an entropy-regularized optimal transport problem on path spaces, yields diffusions which generate samples from the data distribution in finite time. We present Diffusion SB (DSB), an original approximation of the Iterative Proportional Fitting (IPF) procedure to solve the SB problem, and provide theoretical analysis along with generative modeling experiments. The first DSB iteration recovers the methodology proposed by Song et al., 2021 , with the flexibility of using shorter time intervals, as subsequent DSB iterations reduce the discrepancy between the final-time marginal of the forward (resp. backward) SDE with respect to the Gaussian prior (resp. data) distribution. Beyond generative modeling, DSB offers a computational optimal transport tool as the continuous state-space analogue of the popular Sinkhorn algorithm ( Cuturi, 2013 ) .



1
Introduction

Score-Based Generative Modeling
(SGM) is a recently developed
approach to probabilistic generative modeling that exhibits state-of-the-art performance on several audio and image synthesis tasks; see
e.g.

Song and Ermon, 2019
;
Cai et al., 2020
;
Chen et al., 2021a
;
Kong et al., 2021
;
Gao et al., 2020
;
Jolicoeur-Martineau et al., 2021b
;
Ho et al., 2020
;
Song and Ermon, 2020
;
Song et al., 2020
;
Song et al., 2021
;
Niu et al., 2020
;
Durkan and Song, 2021
;
Hoogeboom et al., 2021
;
Saharia et al., 2021
;
Luhman and Luhman, 2021
;
Luhman and Luhman, 2020
;
Nichol and Dhariwal, 2021
;
Popov et al., 2021
;
Dhariwal and Nichol, 2021
.
Existing SGMs generally consist of two parts. Firstly, noise is incrementally
added to the data in order to obtain a perturbed data distribution approximating
an easy-to-sample
prior
distribution
e.g.
Gaussian. Secondly, a neural network
is used to learn the reverse-time denoising dynamics, which when initialized at
this prior distribution, defines a generative model

(
Sohl-Dickstein et al., 2015
;
Ho et al., 2020
;
Song and Ermon, 2019
;
Song et al., 2021
)
.
Song et al., 2021

have shown that one could fruitfully view the noising process as a
Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE) that progressively perturbs the initial data
distribution into an approximately Gaussian one.

Forward
Forward
Backward

t

Generative Model

Figure 1:
The reference forward diffusion initialized from the 2-dimensional data
distribution fails to converge to the Gaussian prior in
T=0.2
diffusion-time (
N=20
discrete time steps),
and the reverse diffusion initialized from the Gaussian prior does not converge to the data distribution. However, convergence does occur after
5
DSB iterations.

The corresponding reverse-time
SDE is an inhomogeneous diffusion whose drift depends on the logarithmic
gradients of the perturbed data distributions,
i.e.
the scores. In practice,
these scores are approximated using neural networks and score-matching
techniques
(
Hyvärinen and Dayan, 2005
;
Vincent, 2011
)
while numerical SDE integrators are
used for the sampling procedure.

Although SGM provides state-of-the-art results
(
Dhariwal and Nichol, 2021
)
,
sample generation is computationally expensive. In order to learn the
reverse-time SDE from the prior,
i.e.
the generative model, the forward noising
SDE must be run for a sufficiently long time to converge to the prior and the step size
must be sufficiently small to obtain a good numerical approximation of this SDE. By reformulating
generative modeling as a Schrödinger bridge (SB) problem, we mitigate this issue and
propose a novel algorithm to solve SB problems. Our detailed contributions are
as follows.

Generative modeling as a Schrödinger bridge problem.
The SB problem is
a famous entropy-regularized Optimal Transport (OT) problem introduced by

Schrödinger, 1932
; see
e.g.

(
Léonard, 2014b
;
Chen et al., 2021b
)
for reviews. Given a reference
diffusion with finite time horizon
T
, a data distribution and a prior
distribution, solving the SB amounts to finding the closest diffusion to the reference
(in terms of Kullback–Leibler divergence on path spaces) which admits the
data distribution as marginal at time
t=0
and the prior at time

t=T
. The reverse-time diffusion solving this SB problem
provides a new SGM algorithm which enables approximate sample generation
from the data distribution using shorter time intervals compared to the
original SGM methods.
Our method differs from the entropy-regularized OT
formulation in
(
Genevay et al., 2018
)
, which deals with discrete distributions and relies on a static formulation of SB, as opposed
to our dynamical approach for continuous distributions which operates on
path spaces. It also differs from
(
Finlay et al., 2020
)
which
approximates the SB solution by a diffusion whose drift is computed
using potentials of the dual formulation of SB. Finally,
Wang et al., 2021
have recently proposed to perform generative modeling by solving not one but two SB problems. Contrary to us, they do not formulate generative modeling as computing the SB between the data and prior distributions.

Solving the Schrödinger bridge problem using score-based diffusions.

The SB problem can be solved using Iterative Proportional
Fitting (IPF)

(
Fortet, 1940
;
Kullback, 1968
;
Chen et al., 2021b
)
. We
propose Diffusion SB (DSB), a novel implementation of IPF using score-based
diffusion techniques. DSB does not
require discretizing the state-space

(
Chen et al., 2016
;
Reich, 2019
)
, approximating potential functions
using regression

(
Bernton et al., 2019
;
Dessein et al., 2017
;
Pavon et al., 2021
)
, nor performing
kernel density estimation
(
Pavon et al., 2021
)
. The first DSB iteration
recovers the method proposed by
Song et al., 2021
, with the flexibility
of using shorter time intervals, as additional DSB iterations reduce the
discrepancy between the final-time marginal of the forward (resp. backward)
SDE w.r.t. the prior (resp. data) distribution; see
Figure

1

for an illustration. An algorithm akin to DSB has been proposed
concurrently and independently by
Vargas et al., 2021
; the main
difference with our algorithm is that they estimate the drifts of the SDEs
using Gaussian processes while we use neural networks and score matching
ideas.

Theoretical results.
We provide the first quantitative convergence results
for the methodology of
Song et al., 2021
. In particular, we show that while we simulate Langevin-type diffusions in potentially extremely high-dimensional spaces, the SGM approach does
not
suffer from poor mixing times. Additionally, we derive novel
quantitative convergence results for IPF in continuous state-space which do
not rely on classical compactness assumptions

(
Chen et al., 2016
;
Ruschendorf et al., 1995
)
and improve on the
recent results of
Léger, 2020
. Finally, we show that DSB may be viewed as the time discretization of a dynamic version
of IPF on path spaces based on forward/backward diffusions.

Experiments.
We validate our methodology by generating image
datasets such as MNIST and CelebA. In particular, we show that using
multiple steps of DSB always improve the generative model. We also show how
DSB can be used to interpolate between two data distributions.

Notation.
In the
continuous-time setting, we set
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{C}(\left[0,T\right],\mathbb{R}^{d})

the space of continuous functions from
\left[0,T\right]
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C})
the Borel sets on
\mathcal{C}
. For any measurable space

(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
, we denote by
\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{E})
the space of probability measures
on
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
. For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathscr{P}_{\ell}=\mathscr{P}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\ell})
. When it is defined, we denote

\mathrm{H}(p)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p(x)\log p(x)\mathrm{d}x
as the entropy of
p
and

\operatorname{KL}(p|q)
as the Kullback–Leibler divergence between
p
and
q
.
When there is no ambiguity, we use the same notation for distributions
and their densities. All proofs are postponed to the supplementary.

2
Denoising Diffusion, Score-Matching and Reverse-Time SDEs

2.1
Discrete-Time: Markov Chains and Time Reversal

Consider a data distribution with positive density
p_{\textup{data}}
1
1

1

In this
presentation, we assume that all distributions admit a density w.r.t. the
Lebesgue measure for simplicity. However, the algorithms presented here
only require having access to samples from
p_{\textup{data}}
and
p_{\textup{prior}}
.
, a
positive prior density
p_{\textup{prior}}
w.r.t. Lebesgue measure both with support on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a Markov chain with initial density
p_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}
on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
evolving according to positive transition densities
p_{k+1|k}
for

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. Hence for any

x_{0:N}=\{x_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N}\in\mathcal{X}=(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N+1}
, the joint density may be expressed as

\textstyle{p(x_{0:N})=p_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}p_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k}).}

(2)

This joint density also admits the backward decomposition

\textstyle{p(x_{0:N})=p_{N}(x_{N})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}p_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1}),\text{with~}p_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})=\frac{p_{k}(x_{k})p_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})}{p_{k+1}(x_{k+1})},}

(3)

where

p_{k}(x_{k})=\int p_{k|k-1}(x_{k}|x_{k-1})p_{k-1}(x_{k-1})\textrm{d}x_{k-1}
is the marginal density at step
k\geq 1
. For the purpose of generative modeling, we will choose transition densities such that

p_{N}(x_{N})=\int p(x_{0:N})\textrm{d}x_{0:N-1}\approx p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})
for
large
N
, where
p_{\textup{prior}}
is an easy-to-sample
prior
density. One
may sample approximately from
p_{\textup{data}}
using ancestral sampling with the
reverse-time decomposition (
3
),
i.e.
first sample

X_{N}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
followed by
X_{k}\sim p_{k|k+1}(\cdot|X_{k+1})
for

k\in\{N-1,\dots,0\}
. This idea is at the core of all recent
SGM methods. The reverse-time transitions in (
3
) cannot be
simulated exactly but may be approximated if we consider a forward
transition density of the form

\textstyle{p_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})=\mathcal{N}(x_{k+1};x_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}f(x_{k}),2\gamma_{k+1}\mathbf{I}),}

(4)

with drift
f:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and stepsize
\gamma_{k+1}>0
. We first make the following approximation from (
3
)

\displaystyle p_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})

\displaystyle=p_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\exp[\log p_{k}(x_{k})-\log p_{k+1}(x_{k+1})]

(5)

\displaystyle\approx\mathcal{N}(x_{k};x_{k+1}-\gamma_{k+1}f(x_{k+1})+2\gamma_{k+1}\nabla\log p_{k+1}(x_{k+1}),2\gamma_{k+1}\mathbf{I}),

(6)

using that
p_{k}\approx p_{k+1}
, a Taylor expansion of
\log p_{k+1}
at

x_{k+1}
and
f(x_{k})\approx f(x_{k+1})
. In practice, the
approximation holds if
\|x_{k+1}-x_{k}\|
is small which is ensured by
choosing
\gamma_{k+1}
small enough.
Although
\nabla\log p_{k+1}
is not available, one may
obtain an approximation using denoising score-matching methods

(
Hyvärinen and Dayan, 2005
;
Vincent, 2011
;
Song et al., 2021
)
.

Assume that the conditional density
p_{k+1|0}(x_{k+1}|x_{0})
is
available analytically as in
(
Ho et al., 2020
;
Song et al., 2021
)
. We
have

p_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=\int p_{0}(x_{0})p_{k+1|0}(x_{k+1}|x_{0})\textrm{d}x_{0}
and elementary
calculations show that

\nabla\log p_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=\mathbb{E}_{p_{0|k+1}}[\nabla_{x_{k+1}}\log p_{k+1|0}(x_{k+1}|X_{0})]
. We can therefore formulate score estimation
as a regression problem and use a flexible class of functions,
e.g.
neural networks, to parametrize an approximation

s_{\theta^{\star}}(k,x_{k})\approx\nabla\log p_{k}(x_{k})
such that

\textstyle{\theta^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{p_{0,k}}[||s_{\theta}(k,X_{k})-\nabla_{x_{k}}\log p_{k|0}(X_{k}|X_{0})||^{2}]},

(7)

where
p_{0,k}(x_{0},x_{k})=p_{0}(x_{0})p_{k|0}(x_{k}|x_{0})
is the joint density
at steps
0
and
k
. If
p_{k|0}
is not available, we use

\theta^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\mathbb{\sum}_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{p_{k-1,k}}[||s_{\theta}(k,X_{k})-\nabla_{x_{k}}\log p_{k|k-1}(X_{k}|X_{k-1})||^{2}]
. In summary, SGM involves first estimating
the score function
s_{\theta^{\star}}
from noisy data, and then
sampling
X_{0}
using
X_{N}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
and the approximation
(
6
),
i.e.

X_{k}=X_{k+1}-\gamma_{k+1}f(X_{k+1})+2\gamma_{k+1}s_{\theta^{\star}}(k+1,X_{k+1})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1},Z_{k+1}\overset{\textup{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}).

(8)

The random variable
X_{0}
is approximately
p_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}
distributed if
p_{N}(x_{N})\approx p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})
. In what
follows, we let
\{Y_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N}=\{X_{N-k}\}_{k=0}^{N}
and remark that

\{Y_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N}
satisfies a forward recursion.

2.2
Continuous-Time: SDEs, Reverse-Time SDEs and Theoretical results

For appropriate transition densities,
Song et al., 2021
showed that the
forward and reverse-time Markov chains may be viewed as discretized
diffusions. We derive the continuous-time limit of the procedure
presented in
Section

2.1
and establish convergence results. The
Markov chain with kernel (
4
) corresponds to an
Euler–Maruyama discretization of
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
, solving
the following SDE

\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=f(\mathbf{X}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},\quad\mathbf{X}_{0}\sim p_{0}=p_{\textup{data}},}

(9)

where
(\mathbf{B}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
is a Brownian motion and

f:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is regular enough so that (strong) solutions
exist. Under conditions on
f
, it is well-known (see

Haussmann and Pardoux, 1986
;
Föllmer, 1985
;
Cattiaux et al., 2021
for
instance) that the reverse-time process

(\mathbf{Y}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}=(\mathbf{X}_{T-t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}

satisfies

\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\left\{-f(\mathbf{Y}_{t})+2\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})\right\}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},}

(10)

with initialization
\mathbf{Y}_{0}\sim p_{T}
, where
p_{t}
denotes the marginal density of
\mathbf{X}_{t}
.

The reverse-time Markov chain
\{Y_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N}
associated with
(
8
) corresponds to an Euler–Maruyama
discretization of (
10
), where the score functions

\nabla\log p_{t}(x)
are approximated by
s_{\theta^{\star}}(t,x)
.

In what follows, we consider
f(x)=-\alpha x
for

\alpha\geq 0
. This framework includes the one of

Song and Ermon, 2019
(
\alpha=0
,

p_{\textup{prior}}(x)=\mathcal{N}(x;0,2T\ \mathbf{I})
) for which

(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
is simply a Brownian motion and

Ho et al., 2020
(
\alpha>0
,

p_{\textup{prior}}(x)=\mathcal{N}(x;0,\mathbf{I}/\alpha)
) for which it is an
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process, see
Section

C.3
for more
details. Contrary to
Song et al., 2021
we consider time homogeneous
diffusions. Both approaches approximate (
8
) using
distinct discretizations but our setting leverages the ergodic
properties of the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process
to establish
Theorem

1
.

Theorem 1
.

Assume that there exists
\mathtt{M}\geq 0
such that for any

t\in\left[0,T\right]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\left\|s_{\theta^{\star}}(t,x)-\nabla\log p_{t}(x)\right\|\leq\mathtt{M},}

(11)

with

s_{\theta^{\star}}\in\mathrm{C}(\left[0,T\right]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Assume that
p_{\textup{data}}\in\mathrm{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
is
bounded and that there exist
d_{1},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\geq 0
,

\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
and

\mathtt{m}_{1}>0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

i\in\{1,2,3\}

\textstyle{\|\nabla^{i}\log p_{\textup{data}}(x)\|\leq A_{i}(1+\|x\|^{\beta_{i}}),\quad\langle\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x),x\rangle\leq-\mathtt{m}_{1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}+d_{1}\left\|x\right\|,}

(12)

with
\beta_{1}=1
. Then for any
\alpha\geq 0
, there exist

B_{\alpha},C_{\alpha},D_{\alpha}\geq 0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}

and
\{\gamma_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}
with
\gamma_{k}>0
for any

k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, the following bounds on the total variation distance hold:

(a)

if
\alpha>0
, we have
\|\mathcal{L}(X_{0})-p_{\textup{data}}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq C_{\alpha}(\mathtt{M}+\bar{\gamma}^{1/2})\exp[D_{\alpha}T]+B_{\alpha}\exp[-\alpha^{1/2}T];

(b)

if
\alpha=0
, we have

\|\mathcal{L}(X_{0})-p_{\textup{data}}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq C_{0}(\mathtt{M}+\bar{\gamma}^{1/2})\exp[D_{0}T]+B_{0}(T^{-1}+T^{-1/2});

where
T=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\gamma_{k}
,
\bar{\gamma}=\sup_{k\in\{1,\dots,N\}}\gamma_{k}
and

\mathcal{L}(X_{0})
is the distribution of
X_{0}
given in (
8
).

Proof.

We provide here a sketch of the proof. The whole proof is detailed in
Section

C.2
. Denote
\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
the path measure associated with (
9
) and
\mathbb{P}^{R}
its time-reversal. Denote
\mathrm{Q}_{N}
the Markov kernel taking us from
Y_{0}
to
Y_{N}
induced by (
8
). We have

\displaystyle\|p_{\textup{prior}}\mathrm{Q}_{N}-p_{\textup{data}}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle=\|p_{\textup{prior}}\mathrm{Q}_{N}-p_{\textup{data}}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

(13)

\displaystyle\leq\|p_{\textup{prior}}\mathrm{Q}_{N}-p_{\textup{prior}}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}+\|p_{\textup{prior}}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}-p_{\textup{data}}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

(14)

\displaystyle\leq\|p_{\textup{prior}}\mathrm{Q}_{N}-p_{\textup{prior}}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}+\|p_{\textup{prior}}-p_{T}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

(15)

We control the first term by bounding the discretization error of
\mathrm{Q}_{N}
when compared to
(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}
via the Girsanov theorem. The second term is controlled using the mixing properties of the forward diffusion process. ∎

Condition (
11
) ensures that the neural network
approximates the score with a given precision
\mathtt{M}\geq 0
. Under
(
11
) and conditions on
p_{\textup{data}}
,

Theorem

1
states how the Markov chain
defined by (
8
) approximates
p_{\textup{data}}
in the
total variation norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
.
The bounds of
Theorem

1
show that
there is a trade-off between the mixing properties of the forward diffusion
which increases with
\alpha
, and the quality of the discrete-time
approximation which deteriorates as
\alpha
and
T
increase, since

B_{\alpha},C_{\alpha}D_{\alpha}\to_{\alpha\to+\infty}+\infty
. Indeed increasing
\alpha
makes the drift steeper and the continuous-time process converges faster but smaller step sizes are required in order to control the error between the discrete and the continuous-time processes.
Theorem

1
is the first theoretical result assessing the convergence of SGM methods. Indeed while
Block et al., 2020
establish convergence results for a
time-homogeneous
Langevin diffusion targeting a density whose score is approximated by a neural network, all SGM methods used in practice rely on
time-inhomogeneous
processes. Contrary to the time-homogeneous case, this approach does not suffer from poor mixing times as the mixing time dependency in the bounds of
Theorem

1
is entirely determined by the mixing time of the
forward
process, given by a simple Brownian motion or an Ornstein–Ulhenbeck process, and is independent of the dimension. Finally, note that (
11
) is a strong assumption. In practice we expect to obtain such bounds in expectation over
X
with high probability w.r.t. the data distribution as in
(
Block et al., 2020
, Proposition 9)
. Our results are also related to

(
Tzen and Raginsky, 2019
, Theorem 3.1)
which establishes the expressiveness of related generative models using tools from stochastic control.

3
Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge and Generative Modeling

3.1
Schrödinger Bridges

The SB problem is a classical problem appearing in applied
mathematics, optimal control and probability; see

e.g.

Föllmer, 1988
;
Léonard, 2014b
;
Chen et al., 2021b
. In the
discrete-time setting, it takes the following (dynamic) form. Consider as

reference
density
p(x_{0:N})
given by (
2
), describing
the process adding noise to the data. We aim to find
\pi^{\star}\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
such
that

\pi^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|p)\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1},\ \pi_{0}=p_{\textup{data}},~\pi_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\}.

(16)

Assuming
\pi^{\star}
is available, a generative model can be obtained by
sampling
X_{N}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
, followed by the reverse-time
dynamics
X_{k}\sim\pi^{\star}_{k|k+1}(\cdot|X_{k+1})
for

k\in\{N-1,\dots,0\}
. Before deriving a method to approximate

\pi^{\star}
in
Section

3.2
, we highlight some desirable
features of Schrödinger bridges.

Static Schrödinger bridge problem.

First, we recall that the dynamic
formulation (
16
) admits a static analogue. Using
e.g.

Léonard, 2014a
, the following decomposition holds for any

\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
,

\operatorname{KL}(\pi|p)=\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{0,N}|p_{0,N})+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,N}}[\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{|0,N}|p_{|0,N})]
,
where for any
\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
we have
\mu=\mu_{0,N}\mu_{|0,N}
with

\mu_{|0,N}
the conditional distribution of
X_{1:N-1}
given
X_{0},X_{N}
2
2

2

See

Section

D.1
for a rigorous presentation using the disintegration theorem
for probability measures.
. Hence we have

\pi^{\star}(x_{0:N})=\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}(x_{0},x_{N})p_{|0,N}(x_{1:N-1}|x_{0},x_{N})

where
\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
with marginals
\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}_{0}
and

\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}_{N}
is the solution of the static SB problem

\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\mathrm{s}}|p_{0,N})\,:\;\pi^{\mathrm{s}}\in\mathscr{P}_{2},\ \pi^{\mathrm{s}}_{0}=p_{\textup{data}},~\pi^{\mathrm{s}}_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\}.}

(17)

Link with optimal transport.

Under mild assumptions, the static SB problem can be seen as an
entropy-regularized optimal transport problem since (
17
) is
equivalent to

\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}=\textstyle{\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\mathrm{s}}}[\log p_{N|0}(X_{N}|X_{0})]-\mathrm{H}(\pi^{\mathrm{s}})\,:\;\pi^{\mathrm{s}}\in\mathscr{P}_{2},\ \pi_{0}^{\mathrm{s}}=p_{\textup{data}},~\pi_{N}^{\mathrm{s}}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\}.}

(18)

If
p_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})=\mathcal{N}(x_{k+1};x_{k},\sigma^{2}_{k+1})
as in

Song and Ermon, 2019
, then
p_{N|0}(x_{N}|x_{0})=\mathcal{N}(x_{N};x_{0},\sigma^{2})

with
\sigma^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}^{2}
which induces a quadratic cost and

\displaystyle\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\mathrm{s}}}[||X_{0}-X_{N}||^{2}]-2\sigma^{2}\mathrm{H}(\pi^{\mathrm{s}})\,:\;\pi^{\mathrm{s}}\in\mathscr{P}_{2},\ \pi_{0}^{\mathrm{s}}=p_{\textup{data}},~\pi_{N}^{\mathrm{s}}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\}.}

(19)

Mikami, 2004
showed that
\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}\to\pi^{\star}_{\mathcal{W}}

weakly and

2\sigma^{2}\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\mathrm{s},\star}|p_{0,N})\to\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(p_{\textup{data}},p_{\textup{prior}})

as
\sigma\to 0
, where
\pi_{\mathcal{W}}^{\star}
is the optimal transport plan
between
p_{\textup{data}}
and
p_{\textup{prior}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is the 2-Wasserstein distance. Note
that the transport cost
c(x,x^{\prime})=-\log p_{N|0}(x^{\prime}|x)
is not necessarily
symmetric.

3.2
Iterative Proportional Fitting and Time
Reversal

In all but trivial cases,
the SB problem does not admit a closed-form solution. However, it can be solved
using Iterative Proportional Fitting (IPF)

(
Fortet, 1940
;
Kullback, 1968
;
Ruschendorf et al., 1995
)

which is defined by the following recursion for
n\in\mathbb{N}
with initialization

\pi^{0}=p
given in (
2
):

\displaystyle\textstyle{\pi^{2n+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1},\ \pi_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\},}

(20)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\pi^{2n+2}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n+1})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1},\ \pi_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}\right\}.}

(21)

This sequence is well-defined if there exists
\tilde{\pi}\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
such
that
\tilde{\pi}_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}
,
\tilde{\pi}_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}
and

\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|p)<+\infty
. A standard representation of
\pi^{n}
is obtained by updating the joint density
p
using potential functions, see
Section

D.2
for details.
However, this representation of the IPF iterates is difficult to approximate
as it requires approximating the potentials. Our methodology builds upon an alternative
representation that is better suited to numerical approximations for generative modeling where one has access to samples of

p_{\textup{data}}
and
p_{\textup{prior}}
.

Proposition 2
.

Assume that
\operatorname{KL}(p_{\textup{data}}\otimes p_{\textup{prior}}|p_{0,N})<+\infty
. Then for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\pi^{2n}
and
\pi^{2n+1}
admit positive densities w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure denoted as
p^{n}
resp.
q^{n}
and for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have
p^{0}(x_{0:N})=p(x_{0:N})
and

\textstyle{q^{n}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}p^{n}_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1}),}\ \textstyle{p^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}q^{n}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k}).}

(22)

In practice we have access to
p^{n}_{k+1|k}
and

q^{n}_{k|k+1}
. Hence, to compute
p^{n}_{k|k+1}
and

q^{n}_{k+1|k}
we use

p^{n}_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})=\frac{p^{n}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})p_{k}^{n}(x_{k})}{p_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})},\ q^{n}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})=\frac{q^{n}_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})q_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})}{q_{k}^{n}(x_{k})}.

(23)

To the best of our knowledge, this representation of the IPF iterates has surprisingly neither been presented nor explored in the literature.
One may interpret these formulas as follows. At iteration
2n
, we have
\pi^{2n}=p^{n}
with
p^{0}=p

given by the noising process (
2
). This forward process initalized with
p^{n}_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}
defines reverse-time transitions
p^{n}_{k|k+1}
, which, when combined with an initialization
p_{\textup{prior}}
at step
N
defines the reverse-time process
\pi^{2n+1}=q^{n}
. The forward transitions
q^{n}_{k+1|k}
associated to
q^{n}
are then used to obtain
\pi^{2n+2}=p^{n+1}
. IPF then iterates this procedure.

3.3
Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge as Iterative Mean-Matching Proportional Fitting

To approximate the IPF recursion defined in
Section

3.2
, we use
similar approximations to
Section

2.1
. If at step
n\in\mathbb{N}

we have

p^{n}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})=\mathcal{N}(x_{k+1};x_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}f^{n}_{k}(x_{k}),2\gamma_{k+1}\mathbf{I})
where
p^{0}=p
and
f^{0}_{k}=f
, then we can
approximate the reverse-time transitions in
Section

3.2
by

\displaystyle q^{n}_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})

\displaystyle=p^{n}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\exp[\log p^{n}_{k}(x_{k})-\log p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})]

(24)

\displaystyle\approx\mathcal{N}(x_{k};x_{k+1}+\gamma_{k+1}b^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1}),2\gamma_{k+1}\mathbf{I}),

(25)

with

b^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=-f^{n}_{k}(x_{k+1})+2\nabla\log p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})
.
We can also approximate the forward transitions in

Section

3.2
by

p^{n+1}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\approx\mathcal{N}(x_{k+1};x_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}f^{n+1}_{k}(x_{k}),2\gamma_{k+1}\mathbf{I})
with

f^{n+1}_{k}(x_{k})=-b^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k})+2\nabla\log q^{n}_{k}(x_{k})
. Hence
we have

f^{n+1}_{k}(x_{k})=f^{n}_{k}(x_{k})-2\nabla\log p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k})+2\nabla\log q^{n}_{k}(x_{k})
. It follows that one could
estimate
f^{n+1}_{k},b^{n+1}_{k}
by using score-matching to approximate
\{\nabla\log p^{i}_{k+1}(x)\}_{i=0}^{n}
,

\{\nabla\log q^{i}_{k}(x)\}_{i=0}^{n}
. This approach is prohibitively costly in terms of
memory and compute, see
Appendix

E
.
We follow an alternative approach
which avoids these difficulties.

Proposition 3
.

Assume that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
,

q_{k|k+1}^{n}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})=\mathcal{N}(x_{k};B_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1}),2\gamma_{k+1}\mathbf{I}),\ p_{k+1|k}^{n}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})=\mathcal{N}(x_{k+1};F_{k}^{n}(x_{k}),2\gamma_{k+1}\mathbf{I}),

(26)

with
B^{n}_{k+1}(x)=x+\gamma_{k+1}b^{n}_{k+1}(x)
,

F^{n}_{k}(x)=x+\gamma_{k+1}f_{k}^{n}(x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then we
have for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\displaystyle\textstyle{B^{n}_{k+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{{p^{n}_{k,k+1}}}[\|\mathrm{B}(X_{k+1})-(X_{k+1}+F^{n}_{k}(X_{k})-F^{n}_{k}(X_{k+1}))\|^{2}]},

(27)

\displaystyle\textstyle{F^{n+1}_{k}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\mathrm{F}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{q^{n}_{k,k+1}}[\|\mathrm{F}(X_{k})-(X_{k}+B^{n}_{k+1}(X_{k+1})-B^{n}_{k+1}(X_{k}))\|^{2}]}.

(28)

Section

3.3
shows how one can recursively approximate

B^{n}_{k+1}
and
F^{n+1}_{k}
. In practice, we use neural networks

B_{\beta^{n}}(k,x)\approx B^{n}_{k}(x)
and
F_{\alpha^{n}}(k,x)\approx F^{n}_{k}(x)
. Note that the networks could also be learned jointly. In this case, at equilibrium, we would obtain a bridge between
p_{\textup{data}}
and
p_{\textup{prior}}
but not necessarily the Schrödinger bridge.

Network parameters
\alpha^{n},\beta^{n}
are learnt through
gradient descent to minimize empirical versions of the sum over
k
of the
loss functions given by (
27
) and (
28
)
computed using
M
samples and denoted as
\hat{\ell}^{b}_{n}(\beta)
and

\hat{\ell}^{f}_{n+1}(\alpha)
. The resulting algorithm approximating
L\in\mathbb{N}
IPF
iterations is called Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge (DSB) and is summarized in

Algorithm

1
with

Z^{j}_{k},\tilde{Z}_{k}^{j}\overset{\textup{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I})
,
see
Figure

1
for an illustration.

Algorithm 1

Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge

1:

for

n\in\{0,\dots,L\}

do

2:

while
not converged
do

3:

Sample
\{X^{j}_{k}\}_{k,j=0}^{N,M}
, where
X^{j}_{0}\sim p_{\textup{data}}
, and
X^{j}_{k+1}=F_{\alpha^{n}}(k,X^{j}_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z^{j}_{k+1}

4:

Compute
\hat{\ell}^{b}_{n}(\beta^{n})
approximating (
27
)

5:

\beta^{n}\leftarrow\textrm{Gradient Step}(\hat{\ell}^{b}_{n}(\beta^{n}))

6:

end

while

7:

while
not
converged
do

8:

Sample
\{X^{j}_{k}\}_{k,j=0}^{N,M}
, where
X^{j}_{N}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
, and
X^{j}_{k-1}=B_{\beta^{n}}(k,X^{j}_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k}}\tilde{Z}^{j}_{k}

9:

Compute
\hat{\ell}^{f}_{n+1}(\alpha^{n+1})
approximating (
28
)

10:

\alpha^{n+1}\leftarrow\textrm{Gradient Step}(\hat{\ell}^{f}_{n+1}(\alpha^{n+1}))

11:

end

while

12:

end

for

13:

Output:

(\alpha^{L+1},\beta^{L})

The DSB algorithm is initialized using the reference dynamics

f_{\alpha^{0}}(k,x)=f(x)
. Once
\beta^{L}
is learnt we can easily
approximately sample from
p_{\textup{data}}
by sampling
X_{N}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
and then
using
X_{k-1}=B_{\beta^{L}}(k,X_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k}}Z_{k}
with

Z_{k}\overset{\textup{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I})
. The resulting
samples
X_{0}
will be approximately distributed from
p_{\textup{data}}
. Although DSB
requires learning a sequence of network parameters,
\alpha^{n},\beta^{n}
,
fewer diffusion steps are needed compared to standard SGM. In addition, as
detailed in
Appendix

I
,
\beta^{0}
may be trained efficiently
in a similar manner to previous SGM methods. Subsequent

\alpha^{n+1},\beta^{n+1}
are refinements of
\alpha^{n},\beta^{n}
, hence
may be fine-tuned from previous iterations.

3.4
Convergence of Iterative Proportional Fitting

In this section, we investigate the theoretical properties of IPF. When the
state-space is discrete and finite

(
Franklin and Lorenz, 1989
;
Peyré and Cuturi, 2019
)
or in the case where

p_{\textup{data}}
and
p_{\textup{prior}}
are compactly supported
(
Chen et al., 2016
)
, IPF
converges at a geometric rate w.r.t. the Hilbert-Birkhoff metric, see

Lemmens and Nussbaum, 2014
for a definition. Other than recent work by
Léger, 2020
, only qualitative results exist in the general case where
p_{\textup{data}}
or
p_{\textup{prior}}
is not compactly
supported
(
Ruschendorf et al., 1995
;
Rüschendorf and Thomsen, 1993
)
.
We establish here quantitative convergence
of IPF in this non-compact setting as well as novel monotonicity results. We
require only the following mild assumption.

A 1
.

p_{N},p_{\textup{prior}}>0
,
|\mathrm{H}(p_{\textup{prior}})|<+\infty
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\log p_{N|0}(x_{N}|x_{0})|p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\mathrm{d}x_{0}\mathrm{d}x_{N}<+\infty
.

Assumption
1
is satisfied in all of our experimental
settings. We recall that for
\mu,\nu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{E})
with
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
a
measurable space, the Jeffrey’s divergence is given by

\operatorname{J}(\mu,\nu)=\operatorname{KL}(\mu|\nu)+\operatorname{KL}(\nu|\mu)
.

Proposition 4
.

Assume
1
. Then
(\pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is well-defined and for any
n\geq 1
we have

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n+1}|\pi^{n})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n-1}|\pi^{n}),\qquad\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}|\pi^{n+1})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}|\pi^{n-1}).

(29)

In addition,
(\|\pi^{n+1}-\pi^{n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\operatorname{J}(\pi^{n+1},\pi^{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are non-increasing.
Finally, we have
\lim_{n\to+\infty}n\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{0}^{n}|p_{\textup{data}})+\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{N}^{n}|p_{\textup{prior}})\right\}=0
.

A more general result with additional monotonicity properties is given in

Appendix

F
. Under similar assumptions,
Léger, 2020
established
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}_{0}|p_{0})\leq C/n

with
C\geq 0
using a Bregman divergence gradient descent perspective. In contrast,
our proof relies only on tools from information geometry. In addition, we
improve the convergence rate and show that
(\pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in total variation
towards
\pi^{\infty}
,
i.e.
we not only obtain convergence of the
marginals but also convergence of the joint distribution. Under restrictive
conditions on
p_{\textup{data}}
and
p_{\textup{prior}}
,
Ruschendorf et al., 1995
showed
that
\pi^{\infty}
is the Schrödinger bridge. In the following proposition, we avoid
this assumption using results on automorphisms of measures

(
Beurling, 1960
)
.

Proposition 5
.

Assume
1
. Then there exists a solution

\pi^{\star}\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
to the SB problem and we have

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|\pi^{n}-\pi^{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=0
with

\pi^{\infty}\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
. Let

h=p_{0,N}/(p_{0}\otimes p_{N})
and assume that

h\in\mathrm{C}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2},\left(0,+\infty\right))
and that there exist

\Phi_{0},\Phi_{N}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
such that

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\left|\log h(x_{0},x_{N})\right|+\left|\log\Phi_{0}(x_{0})\right|+\left|\log\Phi_{N}(x_{N})\right|)p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\mathrm{d}x_{0}\mathrm{d}x_{N}<+\infty,}

(30)

with
h(x_{0},x_{N})\leq\Phi_{0}(x_{0})\Phi_{N}(x_{N})
. If
p
is
absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\pi^{\infty}
then
\pi^{\infty}=\pi^{\star}
.

Section

3.4
extends previous IPF convergence results without the
assumption that the mapping
h
is lower bounded, see

Ruschendorf et al., 1995
;
Chen et al., 2016
. Our assumption on
h
can be
relaxed and replaced by a tighter condition on
\pi^{\infty}
, see

Section

F.2
.
Section

3.4
suggests a
convergence rate of order
o(n)
for the IPF in the non-compact
setting. However, in some situations, we recover geometric convergence rates
with explicit dependency w.r.t. the problem constants, see

Appendix

G
. In practice, we do not run IPF for

p_{\textup{data}},p_{\textup{prior}}
but using empirical versions of these distributions. Recent
results in
Deligiannidis et al., 2021
show that the iterates of IPF
based on empirical distributions remain close to the iterates one
would obtain using the true distributions, uniformly in time. In particular, the
SB computed using the empirical distributions converges to the one computed
using the true distributions as the number of samples goes to infinity.

3.5
Continuous-time IPF

We describe an IPF algorithm for solving SB problems in continuous-time. We show that
DSB proposed in
Algorithm

1
can be seen as a discretization of
this IPF. Given a reference measure
\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
, the
continuous formulation of the SB involves solving the following problem

\textstyle{\Pi^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\Pi|\mathbb{P})\,:\;\Pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}),\ \Pi_{0}=p_{\textup{data}},\ \Pi_{T}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\},\quad T=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\gamma_{k+1}.}

(31)

Similarly to
(
20
), we define the IPF
(\Pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

with
\Pi^{0}=\mathbb{P}
associated with (
9
) and for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\Pi^{2n+1}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\Pi|\Pi^{2n})\,:\;\Pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}),\ \Pi_{T}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\},}

(32)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\Pi^{2n+2}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\Pi|\Pi^{2n+1})\,:\;\Pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}),\ \Pi_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}\right\}.}

(33)

One can show that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Pi^{n}=\pi^{\mathrm{s},n}\mathbb{P}_{|0,T}
,
with
(\pi^{\mathrm{s},n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the IPF for the static SB problem. In
particular,
Section

3.4
and
Section

3.4
extend to
the continuous IPF framework.
In what follows, for any
\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
, we define
\mathbb{P}^{R}
as the
reverse-time measure,
i.e.
for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C})
we have

\mathbb{P}^{R}(\mathsf{A})=\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}^{R})
where

\mathsf{A}^{R}=\{t\mapsto\omega(T-t)\,:\;\omega\in\mathsf{A}\}
.
The following result is the continuous
counterpart of
Section

3.2
and states that each IPF iteration is
associated with a diffusion, showing that DSB can be seen as a
discretization of the continuous IPF.

Proposition 6
.

Assume
1
and that there exist

\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
,
U\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
C\geq 0

such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\operatorname{KL}(\Pi^{n}|\mathbb{M})<+\infty
,

\langle x,\nabla U(x)\rangle\geq-C(1+\|x\|^{2})
and
\mathbb{M}
is
associated with

\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=-\nabla U(\mathbf{X}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},}

(34)

with
\mathbf{X}_{0}
distributed according to the invariant distribution of (
34
). Then,
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have:

(a)

(\Pi^{2n+1})^{R}
is associated with
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{2n+1}=b^{n}_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{2n+1})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
with
\mathbf{Y}_{0}^{2n+1}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
;

(b)

\Pi^{2n+2}
is associated with

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2n+2}=f^{n+1}_{t}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2n+2})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
with
\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2n+2}\sim p_{\textup{data}}
;

where for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in\left[0,T\right]
and

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b^{n}_{t}(x)=-f^{n}_{t}(x)+2\nabla\log p^{n}_{t}(x)
,

f^{n+1}_{t}(x)=-b^{n}_{t}(x)+2\nabla\log q^{n}_{t}(x)
, with
f^{0}_{t}(x)=f(x)
, see (
9
), and
p^{n}_{t}
,
q_{t}^{n}

the densities of
\Pi^{2n}_{t}
and
\Pi_{t}^{2n+1}
.

4
Experiments

Gaussian example.

We first confirm that our algorithm recovers the true SB in a Gaussian setting where the ground truth is available. Let

p_{\textup{prior}}=\mathcal{N}(-a,\mathbf{I})
,
p_{\textup{data}}=\mathcal{N}(a,\mathbf{I})
with

a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and consider a Brownian motion as reference dynamics. The analytic expression for the static SB is
\mathcal{N}((-a,a),\Sigma)
with

\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}
given in
Section

G.2
. We let
a=0.1\times\mathbf{1}
with

d=50
or
d=5
.

Mean
Variance
Covariance

Figure 2:
Convergence of DSB to ground-truth. From left to right: estimated mean, variance and covariance (first component) after each DSB iteration. The ground-truth value is given by the dashed line in each scenario.

In
Figure

2
, we illustrate the convergence of DSB. We train each DSB
with a batch size of
128
,
N=20
and
\gamma=1/40
. We compare two
network configurations: “small” where the network is given by

Figure

9
(30k parameters) whereas “large” corresponds to
the same network but with twice as many latent dimensions (240k
parameters). The small network recovers the statistics of SB in the
low-dimensional setting (
d=5
) but is unable to recover the variance and
covariance for
d=50
. Increasing the size of the network solves this
problem.

Two dimensional toy experiments.

Data distribution
DSB Iteration 1
DSB Iteration 20

Figure 3:
Data distributions
p_{\textup{data}}
vs
distribution at
t=0
for
T=0.2
after
1
and
20
DSB iterations.

We evaluate the validity of our approach on toy two dimensional examples.
Contrary to existing SGM approaches we do
not
require that the
number of steps is large enough for
p_{N}\approx p_{\textup{prior}}
to hold. We use a
fully connected network with positional encoding

(
Vaswani et al., 2017
)
to approximate
B^{n}_{k}
and
F^{n}_{k}
, see

Section

J.1
and our
code
3
3

3

Code is available here
https://github.com/JTT94/diffusion_schrodinger_bridge
for implementation details.
Animated plots of the DSB iterations may be found online on our project

webpage
4
4

4

https://vdeborto.github.io/publication/schrodinger_bridge/
.
In
Figure

3
, we illustrate the benefits of DSB over classical
SGM. We fix
f(x)=-\alpha x
and choose

p_{\textup{prior}}=\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{data}}\mathbf{I})
, hence

\alpha=1/\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{data}}
where
\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{data}}
is the
variance of the dataset. We let
N=20
and
\gamma_{k}=0.01
,
i.e.

T=0.2
. Since
T
is small, we do not have
p_{N}\approx p_{\textup{prior}}
and the
reverse-time process obtained after the first DSB iteration (corresponding
to original SGM methods) does not yield a satisfactory generative
model. However, multiple iterations of DSB improve the quality of the
synthesis.

Generative
modeling.

DSB is the first practical
algorithm for approximating the solution to the SB problem in high
dimension (
d=3072
for CelebA). Whilst our implementation does not yet compete with state-of-the-art methods, we
show promising results with fewer diffusion steps compared to initial
SGMs
(
Song and Ermon, 2019
)
and demonstrate its performance on MNIST
(
LeCun and Cortes, 2010
)
and
CelebA
(
Liu et al., 2015
)
.

. Our method is validated on downscaled CelebA in

, we obtain better results with higher

)

.

Dataset interpolation.

Schrödinger bridges not only reduce the number of steps in SGM
methods but also enable flexibility in the choice of the prior density
p_{\textup{prior}}
. Our approach is still valid for non-Gaussian
p_{\textup{prior}}
, contrary to previous SGM works, and can be set as any other data distribution

p_{\textup{data}}^{\prime}
. In this case DSB converges towards a bridge between
p_{\textup{data}}
and

p_{\textup{data}}^{\prime}
, see
Figure

7
. These experiments pave the way towards
high-dimensional optimal transport between arbitrary data distributions.

Figure 7:
First row: Swiss-roll to S-curve (2D). Iteration 9 of DSB with

T=1
(
N=50
). From left to right:

t=0,0.4,0.6,1
. Second row: EMNIST
(
Cohen et al., 2017
)
to
MNIST. Iteration 10 of DSB with
T=1.5
(
N=30
).
From left to right:
t=0,0.4,1.25,1.5
.

5
Discussion

Score-based generative modeling (SGM) may be viewed as the first stage of
solving a Schrödinger bridge problem. Building on this interpretation, we develop novel methodology, the Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge (DSB), that extends SGM
approaches and allows one to perform generative modeling with fewer diffusion
steps. DSB complements recent techniques to speed up existing SGM methods that
rely on either different noise schedules

(
Nichol and Dhariwal, 2021
;
San-Roman et al., 2021
;
Watson et al., 2021
)
, alternative
discretizations
(
Jolicoeur-Martineau et al., 2021a
)
or knowledge distillation

(
Luhman and Luhman, 2021
)
. Additionally, as the solution of the Schrödinger problem
is a diffusion, it is possible as in
Song et al., 2021
to
obtain an equivalent neural ordinary differential equation that admits the same
marginals as the diffusion but enables exact likelihood computation, see

Section

H.3
. Even though the final time
T>0
within DSB can be
arbitrarily small, we observed that this has limits as choosing
T
too close to

0
decreases the quality of the generative models. One reason for this
behavior is that if the endpoint of the original forward process is too far from
the target distribution
p_{\textup{prior}}
, then learning the score around the support of

p_{\textup{prior}}
is challenging even for DSB. From a theoretical point of view, we have
provided quantitative convergence results for SGM methods and derived new
state-of-the-art convergence bounds for IPF as well as novel monotonicity
results. We have demonstrated DSB on generative modeling and data interpolation
tasks. Finally, although this work was motivated by generative modeling, DSB is
much more widely applicable as it can be thought of as the continuous
state-space counterpart of the celebrated Sinkhorn algorithm

(
Cuturi, 2013
;
Peyré and Cuturi, 2019
)
. For example, DSB could be
used to solve multi-marginal Schrödinger bridges problems

(
Di Marino and Gerolin, 2020
)
, compute Wasserstein barycenters, find the
minimizers of entropy-regularized Gromov–Wasserstein problems

(
Mémoli, 2011
)
or perform domain adaptation in continuous
state-spaces.

Computing resources were provided through the Google Cloud research credits programme. Arnaud Doucet also acknowledges support from the UK Defence Science and Technology Laboratory (DSTL) and EPSRC under grant EP/R013616/1. This is part of the collaboration between US DOD, UK MOD and UK EPSRC under the Multidisciplinary University Research Initiative.

Appendix A
Organization of the supplementary

The supplementary is organized as follows. We define our notation in

Appendix

B
. In
Appendix

C
, we prove

Theorem

1
and draw links between our approach of
SGM and existing works. We recall the classical formulation of IPF, prove

Section

3.2
and draw links with autoencoders in

Appendix

D
. In
Appendix

E
we present
alternative variational formulas for
Algorithm

1
and prove

Section

3.3
. We gather the proofs of our theoretical
study of Schrödinger bridges (
Section

3.4
and

Section

3.4
) in
Appendix

F
. A quantitative
study of IPF with Gaussian targets and reference measure is presented in

Appendix

G
. In particular, we show that the convergence
rate of IPF is geometric in this case. In
Appendix

H
we
study the links between continuous-time and discrete-time IPF and prove

Section

3.5
. We also provide details on the likelihood
computation of generative models obtained with Schrödinger bridges. We
detail training techniques to improve training times in

Appendix

I
then present architecture details and additional
experiments in
Appendix

J
.

Appendix B
Notation

For ease of reading in this section we recall and detail some of the notation
introduced in
Section

1
. For any measurable space
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
, we
denote by
\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{E})
the space of probability measures over
\mathsf{E}
. For any

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we also denote
\mathscr{P}_{\ell}=\mathscr{P}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\ell})
. For any

\pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{E})
and Markov kernel

\mathrm{K}:\mathsf{E}\times\mathcal{F}\to\left[0,1\right]
where
(\mathsf{F},\mathcal{F})
is a measurable
space, we define
\pi\mathrm{K}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{F})
such that for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{F}

we have
\textstyle{\pi\mathrm{K}(\mathsf{A})=\int_{\mathsf{E}}\mathrm{K}(x,\mathsf{A})\mathrm{d}\pi(x)}
.
If
\mathsf{E}=\mathcal{C}
then for any
\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{E})
and

s,t\in\left[0,T\right]
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}_{s,t}
the marginals of
\mathbb{P}
at
time
s
and
t
. In addition, we denote by
\mathbb{P}_{|s,t}
the disintegration
Markov kernel given by the mapping
\omega\mapsto(\omega(s),\omega(t))
, see

Section

D.1
for a definition. In particular, we have

\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}_{s,t}\mathbb{P}_{|s,t}
. All defined mappings are considered to be
measurable unless stated otherwise.

For any
\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
we define
\mathbb{P}^{R}
the reverse-time
measure,
i.e.
for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C})
we have

\mathbb{P}^{R}(\mathsf{A})=\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}^{R})
where

\mathsf{A}^{R}=\{t\mapsto\omega(T-t)\,:\;\omega\in\mathsf{A}\}
. We say that

\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
is
associated with a diffusion
if it solves
the corresponding martingale problem. More precisely,

\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
is associated with

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=b(t,\mathbf{X}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
for

b:\ \left[0,T\right]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
measurable if for any

v\in\mathrm{C}_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
(M_{t}^{v})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
is a

\mathbb{P}
-local martingale, where for any
t\in\left[0,T\right]

\textstyle{M_{t}^{v}=v(\mathbf{X}_{t})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{A}_{s}(v)(\mathbf{X}_{s})\mathrm{d}s}

(35)

with for any
v\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
t\in\left[0,t\right]
and

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\mathcal{A}_{t}(v)(x)=\langle b(t,x),\nabla v(x)\rangle+\Delta v(x).

(36)

We refer to
Revuz and Yor, 1999
for a rigorous treatment of local
martingales. Note that (
35
) uniquely defines
\mathbb{P}_{t|s}

for any
s,t\in\left[0,T\right]
with
t\geq s
. Hence
\mathbb{P}
is uniquely defined
up to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
.

In some cases, we say that
\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
is
associated with
a diffusion
if it solves the corresponding martingale problem with initial
condition. More precisely,
\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
is associated with

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=b(t,\mathbf{X}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
and

\mathbf{X}_{0}\sim\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if it solves the martingale problem and

\mathbb{P}_{0}=\mu_{0}
. Note that in this case
\mathbb{P}
is uniquely defined.

Finally, for
any measurable space
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
and
\mu,\nu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{E})
we recall
that the Jeffrey’s divergence is given by

\operatorname{J}(\mu,\nu)=\operatorname{KL}(\mu|\nu)+\operatorname{KL}(\nu|\mu)
.

Appendix C
Time-reversal and existing work

Before giving the proof of
Theorem

1
we start by
deriving estimates on the logarithmic derivatives of the density of the
Ornstein-Ulhenbeck process given growth conditions on the initial density in

Section

C.1
. Note that our estimates are uniform w.r.t. the
time variable. We give the proof of
Theorem

1
in

Section

C.2
. Finally, we draw links with
existing works in
Section

C.3
.

C.1
Estimates for logarithmic derivatives

We start by recalling the following multivariate Faa di Bruno’s formula and a
useful technical lemma. Then in
Section

C.1.1
we derive bounds
for the logarithmic derivatives which are non-vacuous for small times. In

Section

C.1.2
we derive bounds for the logarithmic derivatives
which are non-vacuous for large times. We combine them in
Section

C.1.3
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
we denote
\left|\alpha\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}

and
\alpha!=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}!
. If
f:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is

m
-differentiable with
m\in\mathbb{N}
, then for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with

\left|\lambda\right|\leq m
we denote for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\partial_{\lambda}f(x)=\partial_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots\partial_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}f(x)
. Similarly to
Constantine and Savits, 1996
, we define
\prec
the
order on
\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that for any
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
,

\lambda^{1}\prec\lambda^{2}
if
|\lambda^{1}|<|\lambda^{2}|
or

|\lambda^{1}|=|\lambda^{2}|
and there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}

such that
\lambda_{j}^{1}<\lambda_{j}^{2}
and for any
i\in\{1,\dots,j\}
,

\lambda_{i}^{1}=\lambda_{i}^{2}
.

Proposition 7
.

Let
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}
open,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
f\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathsf{U},\mathbb{R})
,

g\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathsf{U})
and
h=f\circ g
. Then for any

\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
\left|\lambda\right|\leq N
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{\partial_{\lambda}h(x)=\sum_{k,s=1}^{\left|\lambda\right|}\sum_{p_{s}(\lambda,k)}f^{(k)}(g(x))\lambda!\prod_{j=1}^{s}\partial_{\ell_{j}}g(x)^{m_{j}}/(m_{j}!\ell_{j}!^{m_{j}}),}

(37)

with

p_{s}(\lambda,k)=\textstyle{\{\{\ell_{i}\}_{i=1}^{s}\in(\mathbb{N}^{d})^{s},\ \{m_{i}\}_{i=1}^{s}\in\mathbb{N}^{s}\,:\;\ell_{1}\prec\dots\prec\ell_{s},\ \sum_{i=1}^{s}m_{i}=k,\ \sum_{i=1}^{s}m_{i}\ell_{i}=\lambda\}.}

(38)

Proof.

The proposition is a direct application of
Constantine and Savits, 1996
.
∎

From this multivariate Faa di Bruno formula we derive the following lemma
drawing links between exponential and logarithmic derivatives.

Lemma 8
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
g_{1}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,

g_{2}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
,
h_{1}=\exp[g_{1}]
and

h_{2}=\log(g_{2})
. Then for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
\left|\lambda\right|\leq N
let

c_{d,\lambda}=\sum_{k=1}^{\left|\lambda\right|}d^{k}
and the following hold:

(a)

There exists
\mathrm{P}_{\lambda,\exp}
a real polynomial with
c_{d,\lambda}
variables such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\partial_{\lambda}h_{1}(x)=\mathrm{P}_{\lambda,\exp}((\partial_{\ell}g_{1}(x))_{\left|\ell\right|\leq\left|\lambda\right|})h_{1}(x).

(39)

(b)

There exists
\mathrm{P}_{\lambda,\log}
a real polynomial with
c_{d,\lambda}
variables such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\partial_{\lambda}h_{2}(x)=\mathrm{P}_{\lambda,\log}((\partial_{\ell}g_{2}(x)/g_{2}(x))_{\left|\ell\right|\leq\left|\lambda\right|}).

(40)

Proof.

The proof of
(a)
is a direct application of
Section

C.1

upon noting that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
f^{(k)}=\exp
if

f=\exp
. Similarly, the proof of
(b)
is a direct application of

Section

C.1
upon noting that, in the case where
f=\log
, for any

k\in\mathbb{N}
and
x>0
,
f^{(k)}(x)=(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!x^{-k}
and that for
any
s\in\{1,\dots,\left|\lambda\right|\}
and

(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s},m_{1},\dots,m_{s})\in p_{s}(\lambda,k)
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{s}m_{i}=k
.
∎

We will also make use of the following technical lemma.

Lemma 9
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for any
a\geq 0
,
b>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle-b\|x\|^{2p}+a\|x\|^{2p-1}\leq-(b/2)\|x\|^{2p}+a(2a/b)^{2p-1},

(41)

\displaystyle-b\|x\|^{2p}+a\|x\|^{2p-2}\leq-(b/2)\|x\|^{2p}+a(2a/b)^{p-1}.

(42)

In addition for any
a\geq 0
,
b>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

-b\|x\|^{2p}+a\|x\|^{2p-1}\leq(2p-1)^{2p-1}(2p)^{-2p}a^{2p}b^{1-2p}.

(43)

Proof.

For the first part of the proof, we only prove (
41
). The proof of
(
42
) is similar. Let
a\geq 0
,
b>0
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

with
\|x\|\leq(b/2a)^{-1}
we have

a\|x\|^{2p-1}\leq a(b/2a)^{-2p+1}
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\|x\|\geq(b/2a)^{-1}
we have

a\|x\|^{2p-1}\leq(b/2)\|x\|^{2p}
. Hence, we get that for
any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

a\|x\|^{2p-1}-b\|x\|^{2p}\leq a(b/2a)^{-2p+1}-(b/2)\left\|x\right\|^{2p},

(44)

which concludes the first part of the proof. For the second part of the
proof, remark that the maximum of
\mathrm{h}:\ t\mapsto-bt^{2p}+at^{2p-1}
is
attained for
t^{\star}=(2p-1)/(2p)(a/b)
. We conclude upon noting that

h(t^{\star})=(2p-1)^{2p-1}(2p)^{-2p}a^{2p}b^{1-2p}
.
∎

C.1.1
Small times estimates

Section

C.1
is key in the following proposition which establishes
upper bounds on the logarithmic derivatives of the density of the
Ornstein-Ulhenbeck process. In what follows, we define
(p_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
the
density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbf{X}_{t}
satisfying

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},\qquad\mathbf{X}_{0}\sim p_{\textup{data}},

(45)

with
\alpha\geq 0
. In the rest of this section,
\alpha
is fixed.

Proposition 10
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that

p_{\textup{data}}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
is bounded and that for any

\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
there exist
A_{\ell}\geq 0
and
\alpha_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}

such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\|\nabla^{\ell}\log p_{\textup{data}}(x)\|\leq A_{\ell}(1+\left\|x\right\|^{\alpha_{\ell}}).

(46)

Then for any
t\geq 0
,
p_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
and for
any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, there exist
B_{\ell}\geq 0
and

\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
t\geq 0

\textstyle{\|\nabla^{\ell}\log p_{t}(x)\|\leq c_{t}^{-2\beta_{\ell}}B_{\ell}(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{\beta_{\ell}}p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}),}

(47)

with
c_{t}^{2}=\exp[-2\alpha t]
.

Proof.

First note that for any
t\geq 0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{p_{t}(x_{t})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\mathrm{g}(x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{0},}

(48)

with for any
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

c_{t}=\exp[-\alpha t],\qquad\mathrm{g}(\tilde{x})=(2\uppi\sigma_{t}^{2})^{-d/2}\exp[-\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})],\quad\sigma_{t}^{2}=(1-\exp[-2\alpha t])/\alpha.

(49)

Let
t\geq 0
. We have that
p_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
upon
combining the fact that
p_{\textup{data}}
is bounded, (
48
) and the
dominated convergence theorem. Let
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and

\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
\left|\lambda\right|\leq\ell
. Using

Section

C.1
-
(b)
we have for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\partial_{\lambda}\log p_{t}(x_{t})=\mathrm{P}_{\lambda,\log}((\partial_{m}p_{t}(x_{t})/p_{t}(x_{t}))_{\left|m\right|\leq\left|\lambda\right|}).

(50)

Using (
48
) and the change of variable
z=x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0}
, we
have for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{p_{t}(x_{t})=c_{t}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\textup{data}}((x_{t}-z)/c_{t})\mathrm{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z.}

(51)

Hence, combining this result, the dominated convergence theorem and

Section

C.1
-
(a)
we get that for any

x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
\left|m\right|\leq\ell

\displaystyle\partial_{m}p_{t}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\textstyle{c_{t}^{-\left|m\right|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{m}p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\mathrm{g}(x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{0}}

(52)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{c_{t}^{-\left|m\right|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{P}_{m,\exp}((\partial_{j}\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}))_{\left|j\right|\leq\left|m\right|})p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\mathrm{g}(x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{0}}.

(53)

We conclude the proof upon combining this result, (
46
),
(
50
) and the fact that
c_{t}\leq 1
.
∎

For any
t\geq 0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we introduce the infinitesimal
generator

\mathcal{A}_{t,x_{t}}:\ \mathrm{C}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{C}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
given
for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t,x_{t}}(\varphi)(x_{0})

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{x_{0}}\log p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t}),\nabla\varphi(x_{0})\rangle+\Delta\varphi(x_{0})

(54)

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}),\nabla\varphi(x_{0})\rangle+(c_{t}/\sigma_{t}^{2})\langle x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0},\nabla\varphi(x_{0})\rangle+\Delta\varphi(x_{0}).

(55)

Establishing Foster-Lyapunov drift condition for this infinitesimal generator
will allow us to derive moment bounds for
x_{0}\mapsto p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})
. We now
introduce the Lyapunov functional which will allow us to control these moments.
For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
t>0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

V_{p,t,x_{t}}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[1,+\infty\right)
given for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

by

V_{p,t,x_{t}}(x_{0})=1+\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2p},\qquad c_{t}=\exp[-\alpha t].

(56)

Proposition 11
.

Assume
p_{\textup{data}}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and that there exist
\mathtt{m}_{0}>0
,
d_{0},C_{0}\geq 0

such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\langle x_{0},\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\rangle\leq-\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}\|^{2}+d_{0}\left\|x_{0}\right\|,\quad\|\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\|\leq C_{0}(1+\|x_{0}\|).

(57)

Then for any
t>0
,
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
there exist

\beta_{p}\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{p}>0
and
b_{p}\geq 0
(independent of
t
and

x_{t}
) such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\mathcal{A}_{t,x_{t}}(V_{p,t,x_{t}})(x_{0})\leq-a_{p}V_{p,t,x_{t}}(x_{0})+b_{p}(1+\|x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{\beta_{p}}),

(58)

with
\beta_{p}=2p
.

Proof.

Let
t\geq 0
,
x_{0},x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
.
First, we have for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle V_{p,t,x_{t}}(x_{0})=\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2p},\quad\nabla V_{p,t,x_{t}}(x_{0})=2p(x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t})\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2(p-1)},

(59)

\displaystyle\Delta V_{p,t,x_{t}}(x_{0})=2p(2p-1)\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2(p-1)}.

(60)

Second, using
Section

C.1
, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
57
), we have for any

x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}),x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\rangle\leq-\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}\|^{2}+d_{0}\left\|x_{0}\right\|+\|\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\|\|x_{t}/c_{t}\|

(61)

\displaystyle\quad\leq-\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2}+2\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}\|\|x_{t}\|/c_{t}+C_{0}(1+\|x_{0}\|)\|x_{t}\|/c_{t}

(62)

\displaystyle\qquad+d_{0}\left\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\right\|+d_{0}\left\|x_{t}\right\|/c_{t}+\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{t}\|^{2}/c_{t}^{2}

(63)

\displaystyle\quad\leq-\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2}+\{(2\mathtt{m}_{0}+C_{0})\|x_{t}\|/c_{t}+d_{0}\}\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|

(64)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(3\mathtt{m}_{0}+C_{0})\|x_{t}\|^{2}/c_{t}^{2}+(C_{0}+d_{0})\|x_{t}\|/c_{t}.

(65)

Combining this result and
(
59
), we have for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}),\nabla V_{p,t,x_{t}}(x_{0})\rangle

(66)

\displaystyle\quad\leq-2p\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2p}+2p\{(2\mathtt{m}_{0}+C_{0})\|x_{t}\|/c_{t}+d_{0}\}\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2p-1}

(67)

\displaystyle\qquad+2p((3\mathtt{m}_{0}+C_{0})\|x_{t}\|^{2}/c_{t}^{2}+(C_{0}+d_{0})\|x_{t}\|/c_{t})\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2p-2}.

(68)

Combining this result with (
54
) and the fact that for any

x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(c_{t}/\sigma_{t}^{2})\langle x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0},\nabla V_{p,t,x_{t}}(x_{0})\rangle\leq 0
, we
get that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t,x_{t}}(V_{p,t,x_{t}})(x_{0})

\displaystyle\leq-2p\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2p}+2p\{(2\mathtt{m}_{0}+C_{0})\|x_{t}\|/c_{t}+d_{0}\}\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2p-1}

(69)

\displaystyle\qquad+2p((3\mathtt{m}_{0}+C_{0})\|x_{t}\|^{2}/c_{t}^{2}+(C_{0}+d_{0})\|x_{t}\|/c_{t})\|x_{0}-x_{t}/c_{t}\|^{2p-2}.

(70)

Using
Section

C.1
there exist
\beta_{p}\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{p}>0
and

b_{p}\geq 0
(independent of
x_{t}
and
t
) such that for any

x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\mathcal{A}_{t,x_{t}}(V_{p,t,x_{t}})(x_{0})\leq-a_{p}V_{p,t,x_{t}}(x_{0})+b_{p}(1+(\|x_{t}\|/c_{t})^{\beta_{p}}),

(71)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Using this Foster-Lyapunov drift we are now ready to bound the moments of

x_{0}\mapsto p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})
.

Proposition 12
.

Assume that
p_{\textup{data}}\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and that there exist
\mathtt{m}_{0}>0
,
d_{0},C_{0}\geq 0

such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\langle x_{0},\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\rangle\leq-\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}\|^{2}+d_{0}\|x_{0}\|,\quad\|\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\|\leq C_{0}(1+\|x_{0}\|).

(72)

Then, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
there exist
C_{p}\geq 0
and
\beta_{p}\in\mathbb{N}

such that for any
t\geq 0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{p}p(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}\leq C_{p}c_{t}^{-2\beta_{p}}(1+\|x_{t}\|^{\beta_{p}}),}

(73)

with
c_{t}^{2}=\exp[-2\alpha t]
and
\beta_{p}=p
.

Proof.

Let
t\geq 0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using
(
Ikeda and Watanabe, 1989
, Theorem 2.3, Theorem
3.1)
,
Section

C.1.1
and
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1993
, Theorem 2.1)
for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists a unique strong solution
(\mathbf{X}_{u}^{x})_{u\geq 0}

such that
\mathbf{X}_{0}^{x}\sim\updelta_{x}
and

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{u}^{x}=\nabla\log p_{0|t}(\mathbf{X}_{u}^{x}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}u+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{u}.

(74)

Using
(
Leha and Ritter, 1984
, Theorem 5.19)
we get that

\{(\mathbf{X}_{u}^{x})_{u\geq 0}\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
is associated with a
Feller semi-group. In addition, we have that for any

f\in\mathrm{C}_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{A}_{t,x_{t}}(f)(x_{0})p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}=0
. Therefore, using
(
Revuz and Yor, 1999
, Proposition 1.5)
and

(
Ethier and Kurtz, 1986
, Theorem 9.17)
we get that the probability distribution
with density
x_{0}\mapsto p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})
is an invariant distribution for the
semi-group associated with

\{(\mathbf{X}_{u}^{x})_{u\geq 0}\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. Therefore, using

Section

C.1.1
and
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1993
, Theorem 4.6)
we get
that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\|x_{0}-c_{t}^{-1}x_{t}\|^{2p})p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}\leq b_{p}(1+\left\|x_{t}/c_{t}\right\|^{\beta_{p}})/a_{p}}

(75)

which concludes the proof upon using that
c_{t}\leq 1
and Jensen’s inequality.
∎

C.1.2
Large times estimates

In
Section

C.1.1
, the bound in (
73
) goes to

+\infty
as
t\to+\infty
since
\lim_{t\to+\infty}c_{t}^{-1}=+\infty
(if

\alpha>0
). This does not yield any degeneracy in our setting since we
consider a fixed time horizon
T>0
. However, we can improve the result by
deriving another bound which is bounded at
t\to+\infty
but explodes as

t\to 0
. In this section we assume that
h:\ u\mapsto(\exp[u]-1)/u
is
extended to
0
by continuity with
h(0)=1
.

The following proposition is the equivalent of
Section

C.1.1
with a
bound which explodes for
t\to 0
instead of
t\to+\infty
. Note that
contrary to
Section

C.1.1
we do not require any differentiability
condition the initial distribution
p_{\textup{data}}
.

Proposition 13
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that

p_{\textup{data}}\in\mathrm{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
is bounded. Then for any

t\geq 0
,
p_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
and for any

\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, there exist
B_{\ell}\geq 0
and

\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\textstyle{\|\nabla^{\ell}\log p_{t}(x)\|}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\sigma_{t}^{-\beta_{\ell}}B_{\ell}(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0}\right\|^{\beta_{\ell}}p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0})}

(76)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\sigma_{t}^{-\beta_{\ell}}B_{\ell}(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{t}-x_{0}\right\|^{\beta_{\ell}}q_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0})}.

(77)

with
\sigma_{t}^{2}=(1-\exp[-2\alpha t])/\alpha
and for any
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle q_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})=\textstyle{p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}/c_{t})\mathrm{g}(x_{t}-x_{0})/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}/c_{t})\mathrm{g}(x_{t}-x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{0}},

(78)

\displaystyle\mathrm{g}(\tilde{x})=(2\uppi\sigma_{t}^{2})\exp[-\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})].

(79)

Proof.

First note that for any
t\geq 0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{p_{t}(x_{t})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\mathrm{g}(x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{0},}

(80)

with

c_{t}=\exp[-\alpha t],\qquad\mathrm{g}(\tilde{x})=(2\uppi\sigma_{t}^{2})^{-d/2}\exp[-\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})],\quad\sigma_{t}^{2}=(1-\exp[-2\alpha t])/\alpha.

(81)

Let
t\geq 0
. We have
p_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
upon
combining the fact that
p_{\textup{data}}
is bounded, (
80
) and the
dominated convergence theorem. Let
\ell\in\{0,\dots,N\}
and

\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
\left|\lambda\right|\leq\ell
. Using

Section

C.1
-
(b)
we have for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\partial_{\lambda}\log p_{t}(x_{t})=\mathrm{P}_{\lambda,\log}((\partial_{m}p_{t}(x_{t})/p_{t}(x_{t}))_{\left|m\right|\leq\left|\lambda\right|}).

(82)

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
\left|m\right|\leq\left|\lambda\right|
, using the dominated
convergence theorem, there exist
C_{m}\geq 0
and
\beta_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
such
that for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{\left|\partial_{m}p_{t}(x_{t})\right|\leq C_{m}\sigma_{t}^{-2\beta_{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\left\|x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0}\right\|^{\beta_{m}})p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\mathrm{g}(x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{0},}

(83)

which concludes the proof.
∎

For any
t\geq 0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we introduce the infinitesimal
generator

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{t,x_{t}}:\ \mathrm{C}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{C}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
given
for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{t,x_{t}}(f)(x_{0})

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla\log q_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t}),\nabla\varphi(x_{0})\rangle+\Delta\varphi(x_{0})

(84)

\displaystyle=c_{t}^{-1}\langle\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}/c_{t}),\nabla\varphi(x_{0})\rangle+\sigma_{t}^{-2}\langle x_{t}-x_{0},\nabla\varphi(x_{0})\rangle+\Delta\varphi(x_{0}).

(85)

For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, let
V_{p}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[1,+\infty\right)
given for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

V_{p}(x_{0})=1+\|x_{0}\|^{2p}.

(86)

The following proposition is the counterpart to
Section

C.1.1
.

Proposition 14
.

Assume that
p_{\textup{data}}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and that there exist
\mathtt{m}_{0}>0
,
d_{0}\geq 0

such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\langle x_{0},\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\rangle\leq-\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}\|^{2}+d_{0}\left\|x_{0}\right\|.

(87)

Then for any
t>0
,
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
there exist

\beta_{p}\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{p}>0
and
b_{p}\geq 0
(independent of
t
and

x_{t}
) such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{t,x_{t}}(V_{p})(x_{0})\leq-a_{p}\sigma_{t}^{-2}V_{p}(x_{0})+b_{p}(1+\|x_{t}/\sigma_{t}^{2}\|^{\beta_{p}}),

(88)

with
\beta_{p}=2p
.

Proof.

Let
t\geq 0
,
x_{0},x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
. First, we have for
any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

V_{p}(x_{0})=1+\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p},\qquad\nabla V_{p}(x_{0})=2p\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2(p-1)}x_{0},\qquad\Delta V_{p}(x_{0})=2p(2p-1)\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2(p-1)}.

(89)

Using this result, (
87
) and
Section

C.1
, we
get that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle 2p\langle\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}/c_{t}),x_{0}/c_{t}\rangle\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2(p-1)}

\displaystyle\leq 2pc_{t}^{-1}(-\mathtt{m}_{0}\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p}/c_{t}+d_{0}\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p-1})

(90)

\displaystyle\leq c_{t}^{-1}(2p-1)^{2p-1}(2p)^{1-2p}(\mathtt{m}_{0}/c_{t})^{1-2p}d_{0}^{2p}.

(91)

Combining this result and the fact that
c_{t}\leq 1
, there exists

d_{p}\geq 0
(independent from
t
and
x_{t}
) such that for any

x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

2p\langle\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0}/c_{t}),x_{0}/c_{t}\rangle\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2(p-1)}\leq d_{p}.

(92)

In addition, we have for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle(2p/\sigma_{t}^{2})\langle x_{0},x_{t}-x_{0}\rangle\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2(p-1)}+2p(2p-1)\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2(p-1)}

(93)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq-(2p/\sigma_{t}^{2})\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p}+(2p/\sigma_{t}^{2})\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p-1}\left\|x_{t}\right\|+2p(2p-1)\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p-1}+2p(2p-1).

(94)

Combining this result and (
92
) we have for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{t,x_{t}}(V_{p})(x_{0})

(95)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq-(2p/\sigma_{t}^{2})\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p}+(2p/\sigma_{t}^{2})\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p-1}\left\|x_{t}\right\|+2p(2p-1)\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2p-1}+2p(2p-1)+d_{p}.

(96)

We conclude upon using
Section

C.1
.
∎

The next proposition is the counterpart of
Section

C.1.1
.

Proposition 15
.

Assume that
p_{\textup{data}}\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and that there exist
\mathtt{m}_{0}>0
,
d_{0}\geq 0

such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\langle x_{0},\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\rangle\leq-\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}\|^{2}+d_{0}\|x_{0}\|.

(97)

Then, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
there exist
C_{p}\geq 0
and
\beta_{p}\in\mathbb{N}

such that for any
t\in\geq 0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x_{t}-x_{0}\right\|^{p}q_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}\leq C_{p}\sigma_{t}^{-2\beta_{p}}(1+\|x_{t}\|^{\beta_{p}}),}

(98)

with
\sigma_{t}^{2}=(1-\exp[-2\alpha t])/\alpha
and
\beta_{p}=p
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of
Section

C.1.1
.
∎

C.1.3
Uniform in time logarithmic derivatives estimates

In this section we combine the results of
Section

C.1.2
and

Section

C.1.1
to establish uniform in time estimates for the
logarithmic derivatives of the density of the Ornstein-Ulhenbeck diffusion.

Theorem 16
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N\geq 2
. Assume that

p_{\textup{data}}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and that there exist
\mathtt{m}_{0}>0
,

d_{0},C_{0}\geq 0
such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\langle x_{0},\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\rangle\leq-\mathtt{m}_{0}\|x_{0}\|^{2}+d_{0}\|x_{0}\|,\quad\|\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\|\leq C_{0}(1+\|x_{0}\|).

(99)

In addition, assume that

p_{\textup{data}}
is bounded and that for any

\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
there exist
A_{\ell}\geq 0
and
\alpha_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}

such that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\|\nabla^{\ell}\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\|\leq A_{\ell}(1+\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{\alpha_{\ell}}).

(100)

Then for any
t\geq 0
,
p_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
and for
any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, there exist
D_{\ell}\geq 0
and

\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
t\geq 0

\textstyle{\|\nabla^{\ell}\log p_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq D_{\ell}(1+\left\|x_{t}\right\|^{\beta_{\ell}}).}

(101)

In particular if
\alpha_{1}=1
then
\beta_{1}=1
.

Proof.

Let
t\geq 0
and
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. Using
Section

C.1.1
and
Section

C.1.1
there exist

D_{\ell}^{1}\geq 0
and
\beta_{\ell}^{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{\|\nabla^{\ell}\log p_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq D_{\ell}^{1}c_{t}^{-2\beta_{\ell}^{1}}(1+\left\|x_{t}\right\|^{\beta_{\ell}^{1}}).}

(102)

Similarly, using
Section

C.1.2
and
Section

C.1.2
there exist
D_{\ell}^{2}\geq 0
and

\beta_{\ell}^{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{\|\nabla^{\ell}\log p_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq D_{\ell}^{2}(\alpha^{1/2}\sigma_{t})^{-2\beta_{\ell}^{2}}(1+\left\|x_{t}\right\|^{\beta_{\ell}^{2}}).}

(103)

Therefore, there exist
\tilde{D}_{\ell}\geq 0
and
\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{\|\nabla^{\ell}\log p_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq\tilde{D}_{\ell}\min(\alpha^{-1}\sigma_{t}^{-2},c_{t}^{-2})^{\beta_{\ell}}(1+\left\|x_{t}\right\|^{\beta_{\ell}}).}

(104)

Since for any
c_{t}^{-2}=\exp[2\alpha t]
and

\alpha^{-1}\sigma_{t}^{-2}=(1-\exp[-2\alpha t])^{-1}
. Hence we have

\min(\alpha^{-1}\sigma_{t}^{-2},c_{t}^{-2})^{\beta_{\ell}}\leq\max\{\min(1/u,1/(1-u))\,:\;u\in\left[0,1\right]\}\leq 2^{\beta_{\ell}},

(105)

which concludes the first part proof. We now show that if
\alpha_{1}=1
then

\beta_{1}=1
. Recall that for any
t\geq 0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{p_{t}(x_{t})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\mathrm{g}(x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{0},}

(106)

with for any
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

c_{t}=\exp[-\alpha t],\qquad\mathrm{g}(\tilde{x})=(2\uppi\sigma_{t}^{2})^{-d/2}\exp[-\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})],\quad\sigma_{t}^{2}=(1-\exp[-2\alpha t])/\alpha.

(107)

Therefore, using the dominated convergence theorem we get that for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{t})=\sigma_{t}^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0})p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}=\sigma_{t}^{-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0})q_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}.}

(108)

Similarly, using the dominate convergence theorem and change of variable
z=x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0}
, we have for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{t})=c_{t}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\log p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}.}

(109)

We conclude the proof upon combining this result, (
108
),
(
100
) with
\alpha_{1}=1
,
Section

C.1.2

and
Section

C.1.1
. In particular, we use that
\beta_{1}=1
.
∎

C.2
Proof of
Theorem

1

We start by recalling the following basic lemma.

Lemma 17
.

Let
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
and
(\mathsf{F},\mathcal{F})
be two measurable spaces and

\mathrm{K}:\ \mathsf{E}\times\mathcal{F}\to\left[0,1\right]
be a Markov kernel. Then for any

\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{E})
we have

\|\mu_{0}\mathrm{K}-\mu_{1}\mathrm{K}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\|\mu_{0}-\mu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

(110)

In addition, for any
\varphi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{F}
measurable we get that

\|\varphi_{\#}\mu_{0}-\varphi_{\#}\mu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\|\mu_{0}-\mu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}},

(111)

with equality if
\varphi
is injective.

Proof.

We divide the proof into two parts.

(a)

Note that for any
f:\ \mathsf{F}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1
we have
\left\|\mathrm{K}f\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.
Using this result we get

\displaystyle\|\mu_{0}\mathrm{K}-\mu_{1}\mathrm{K}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\sup\{\int_{\mathsf{F}}f(y)\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\mathrm{K})(y)-\int_{\mathsf{F}}f(y)\mathrm{d}(\mu_{1}\mathrm{K})(y)\,:\;\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}

(112)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\sup\{\int_{\mathsf{E}}\mathrm{K}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)-\int_{\mathsf{E}}\mathrm{K}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\,:\;\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}\leq\|\mu_{0}-\mu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.}

(113)

(b)

We have

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{\#}\mu_{0}-\varphi_{\#}\mu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\sup\{\int_{\mathsf{E}}f(\varphi(x))\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)-\int_{\mathsf{E}}f(\varphi(x))\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\,:\;\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}

(114)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\sup\{\int_{\mathsf{E}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)-\int_{\mathsf{E}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\,:\;\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}\leq\|\mu_{0}-\mu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.}

(115)

If
\varphi
is injective then there exists
\varphi^{-1}:\ \mathsf{F}\to\mathsf{F}

(measurable) such that
\varphi^{-1}\circ\varphi=\operatorname{Id}
. Therefore, for any

f:\mathsf{E}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
we have

f=(f\circ\varphi^{-1})\circ\varphi
and

\|f\circ\varphi^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\|\mu_{0}-\mu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\textstyle{\sup\{\int_{\mathsf{E}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)-\int_{\mathsf{E}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\,:\;\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}

(116)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\textstyle{\sup\{\int_{\mathsf{E}}f(\varphi(x))\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)-\int_{\mathsf{E}}f(\varphi(x))\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\,:\;\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}\leq\|\varphi_{\#}\mu_{0}-\varphi_{\#}\mu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}},}

(117)

which concludes the proof.

∎

We will also make use of the following inequality.

Lemma 18
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
t>2/\varepsilon
and
\varphi:\ \left[0,1\right]\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any

s\in\left[0,1\right]
,
\varphi(s)=\exp[-\left\|x-sy\right\|^{2}/(4t)]
. Then

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\left[0,1\right],\mathbb{R})
and we have for any
s\in\left[0,1\right]
