\left|\varphi^{\prime}(s)\right|\leq 2(1+\varepsilon^{-1})(1+\left\|x\right\|)\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{2}/(8t)]\exp[\varepsilon\left\|y\right\|^{2}]/t.

(118)

Proof.

Let
s\in\left[0,1\right]
, we have

\varphi^{\prime}(s)=(\langle x,y\rangle-s\left\|y\right\|^{2})\exp[-\left\|x-sy\right\|^{2}/(4t)]/(2t).

(119)

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and that for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

-\left\|a+b\right\|^{2}\leq-\left\|a\right\|^{2}/2+\left\|b\right\|^{2}
we get

\left|\varphi^{\prime}(s)\right|\leq(\left\|x\right\|\left\|y\right\|+\left\|y\right\|^{2})\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{2}/(8t)+\left\|y\right\|^{2}/(4t)]/(2t).

(120)

In addition, we have

\displaystyle\left\|y\right\|\exp[\|y\|^{2}/(4t)]\leq\left\|y\right\|\exp[\varepsilon\|y\|^{2}/2]\leq(1+\left\|y\right\|^{2})\exp[\varepsilon\left\|y\right\|^{2}/2]\leq 2(1+\varepsilon^{-1})\exp[\varepsilon\left\|y\right\|^{2}].

(121)

Finally we also have
\left\|y\right\|^{2}\exp[\|y\|^{2}/(4t)]\leq(1+\varepsilon^{-1})\exp[\varepsilon\left\|y\right\|^{2}]
.
Combining this result, (
120
) and (
121
) concludes the proof.
∎

Finally we show the following lemma which is a straightforward consequence of
Girsanov’s theorem
(
Liptser and Shiryaev, 2001
, Theorem 7.7)
. A similar version
of this lemma can be found in the proof of
(
Durmus and Moulines, 2017
, Proposition
2)
and in
(
Laumont et al., 2021
, Lemma 26)
(version
where the dependence of the drift in
w\in\mathrm{C}(\left[0,T\right],\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
replaced by a (simpler) dependence in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
). We refer to
(
Liptser and Shiryaev, 2001
, Section
4)
for the definitions of semi-group, non-anticipative
processes and diffusion type processes.

Lemma 19
.

Let
T>0
,

b_{1},b_{2}:\ \left[0,+\infty\right)\times\mathrm{C}(\left[0,T\right],\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
measurable such that for any
i\in\{1,2\}
and

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{(i)}=b_{i}(t,(\mathbf{X}_{s}^{(i)})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
admits a unique strong solution with
\mathbf{X}_{0}^{(i)}=x

and
(b_{i}(t,(\mathbf{X}_{s}^{(i)}))_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
is non-anticipative, with
Markov semi-group
(\mathrm{P}_{t}^{(i)})_{t\geq 0}
. In addition, assume that for
any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\mathbb{P}(\int_{0}^{T}\{\|b_{i}(t,(\mathbf{X}_{s}^{(i)})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})\|^{2}+\|b_{i}(t,(\mathbf{B}_{s})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})\|^{2}\}\mathrm{d}t<+\infty)=1
. Then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{\|\updelta_{x}\mathrm{P}_{T}^{(1)}-\updelta_{x}\mathrm{P}_{T}^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}^{2}\leq(1/2)\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\|b_{1}(t,(\mathbf{X}_{s}^{(1)})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})-b_{2}(t,(\mathbf{X}_{s}^{(1)})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}t.}

(122)

Proof.

Let
T>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any
i\in\{1,2\}
, denote

\mu_{(i)}^{x}
the distribution of
(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{(i)})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
on the
Wiener space
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}))
with
\mathbf{X}_{0}^{(i)}=x
. Similarly denote
\mu_{B}^{x}
the distribution of

(\mathbf{B}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
with
\mathbf{B}_{0}=x
, where we recall that

(\mathbf{B}_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion. Using
Pinsker’s inequality
(
Bakry et al., 2014
, Equation 5.2.2)
and the
transfer theorem
(
Kullback, 1997
, Theorem 4.1)
we get that

\|\updelta_{x}\mathrm{P}_{T}^{(1)}-\updelta_{x}\mathrm{P}_{T}^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}^{2}\leq 2\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{(1)}|\mu_{(2)}).

(123)

Since for any
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\mathbb{P}(\int_{0}^{T}\{\|b_{i}(t,(\mathbf{X}_{s}^{(i)})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})\|^{2}+\|b_{i}(t,(\mathbf{B}_{s})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})\|^{2}\}\mathrm{d}t<+\infty)=1
and the processes

(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{(i)})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
are of diffusion type for
i\in\{1,2\}

we can apply Girsanov’s theorem
(
Liptser and Shiryaev, 2001
, Theorem 7.7)
and

\mu_{B}
-almost surely for any
w\in\mathrm{C}(\left[0,T\right],\mathbb{R})
we get

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}\mu_{(1)}^{x}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{B}^{x})((w_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]})

(124)

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle{=\exp[(1/2)\int_{0}^{T}\langle b_{1}(t,(w_{s})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]}),\mathrm{d}w_{t}\rangle-(1/4)\int_{0}^{T}\|b_{1}(t,(w_{s})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t]}

(125)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}\mu_{B}^{x}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{(2)}^{x})((w_{t})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]})

(126)

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle{=\exp[-(1/2)\int_{0}^{T}\langle b_{2}(t,(w_{s})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})),\mathrm{d}w_{t}\rangle+(1/4)\int_{0}^{T}\|b_{2}(t,(w_{s})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]}))\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t].}

(127)

Hence, we obtain that

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{(1)}^{x}|\mu_{(2)}^{x})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\log((\mathrm{d}\mu_{(1)}^{x}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{(2)}^{x})((\mathbf{X}_{t}^{(1)})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}))]

(128)

\displaystyle\textstyle{=(1/4)\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\|b_{1}(t,(\mathbf{X}_{s}^{(1)})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})-b_{2}(t,(\mathbf{X}_{s}^{(1)})_{s\in\left[0,T\right]})\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}t}

(129)

which concludes the proof.
∎

We study distributions satisfying some curvature assumption and
show that they are sub-Gaussian. More precisely, we show the following proposition.

Lemma 20
.

Let
q\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
and
\mathtt{m}>0
and
c\geq 0

such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\langle\nabla\log q(x),x\rangle\leq-\mathtt{m}\left\|x\right\|^{2}+c\left\|x\right\|
. Then for any
\varepsilon\in\left[0,\mathtt{m}/2\right)
we have

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\left\|x\right\|^{2}]q(x)\mathrm{d}x<+\infty.}

(130)

Proof.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\log q(x)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\log q(0)+\int_{0}^{1}\langle\nabla\log q(tx),x\rangle\mathrm{d}t}

(131)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\log q(0)-\mathtt{m}\int_{0}^{1}t\left\|x\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+c\|x\|}\leq\textstyle{\log q(0)+c\|x\|-\mathtt{m}\left\|x\right\|^{2}}.

(132)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, we will use the following basic lemma.

Lemma 21
.

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\beta_{1}>0
and
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
such that

\mathbf{X}_{0}
has distribution
\mu
and

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=\alpha_{1}\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\beta_{1}^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},

(133)

where
(\mathbf{B}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Brownian motion. Then for any

\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta_{2}>0
we have that
(\mathbf{Y}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

given for any
t\geq 0
by
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\alpha_{2}\mathbf{X}_{\beta_{2}t}
satisfies

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\beta_{2}\alpha_{1}\mathbf{Y}_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\alpha_{2}(\beta_{2}\beta_{1})^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{t},

(134)

where
(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Brownian motion, and
\mathbf{Y}_{0}
has
distribution
(\tau_{\alpha_{2}})_{\#}\mu
, where for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\tau_{\alpha_{2}}(x)=\alpha_{2}x
.

Proof.

Let
t\geq 0
. Using the change of variable
u\mapsto\beta_{2}u
the following equalities hold in distribution

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}\int_{0}^{\beta_{2}t}\mathbf{X}_{s}\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{2}\beta_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{\beta_{2}t}}

(135)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\beta_{2}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{X}_{\beta_{2}s}\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{2}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{t}}=\textstyle{\beta_{2}\alpha_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{Y}_{s}\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{2}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{t},}

(136)

which concludes the proof.
∎

We now turn to the proof of
Theorem

1

Proof.

Let
\alpha\geq 0
. For any
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, denote
\mathrm{R}_{k}
the
Markov kernel such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
we have

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}_{k+1}(x,\mathsf{A})=(4\uppi\gamma_{k+1})^{-1/2}\int_{\mathsf{A}}\exp[-\left\|\tilde{x}-\mathcal{T}_{k+1}(x)\right\|^{2}/(4\gamma_{k+1})]\mathrm{d}\tilde{x},}

(137)

where for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mathcal{T}_{k+1}(x)=x+\gamma_{k+1}\left\{\alpha x+2s_{\theta}(t_{k},x)\right\}
,
where
t_{k}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\gamma_{\ell}
. Define for any

k_{0},k_{1}\in\{1,\dots,N\}
with
k_{1}\geq k_{0}

\mathrm{Q}_{k_{0},k_{1}}=\prod_{\ell=k_{0}}^{k_{1}}\mathrm{R}_{\ell}
. Finally, for
ease of notation, we also define for any
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,

\mathrm{Q}_{k}=\mathrm{Q}_{1,k}
. Note that for any
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
Y_{k}

has distribution
\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{k}
, where
\pi_{\infty}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure
p_{\textup{data}}
. Let

\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
be the probability measure associated with the diffusion

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},\quad\mathbf{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0},

(138)

where
\pi_{0}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
admits a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue
measure given by
p_{\textup{data}}
. First note that using that
\mathbb{P}_{0}=\pi_{0}
we
have for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}(\mathsf{A})=\mathbb{P}_{T}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}(\mathsf{A})=(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}(\mathsf{A})=(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T}(\mathsf{A})=\pi_{0}(\mathsf{A}).

(139)

Hence
\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}
. Using this result and

Section

C.2
, we have

\displaystyle\|\pi_{0}-\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle=\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

(140)

\displaystyle\leq\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}+\|\pi_{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

(141)

\displaystyle\leq\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}+\|\pi_{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

(142)

Note that
\mathcal{L}(X_{0})=\mathcal{L}(Y_{N})=\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{N}
and therefore

\|\mathcal{L}(X_{0})-\pi_{0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}+\|\pi_{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

(143)

We now bound each one of these terms.

(a)

First, assume that
\alpha>0
. Let
T_{\alpha}=\alpha T
and

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{C}(\left[0,T_{\alpha}\right],\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be associated with

(\mathbf{Z}_{t})_{t\in[0,T_{\alpha}]}
the classical
Ornstein-Ulhenbeck process with
\mathbf{Z}_{0}\sim(\tau_{\alpha})_{\#}\pi_{0}
,
where for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have
\tau_{\alpha}(x)=\alpha^{1/2}x
,
satisfying the following SDE:

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Z}_{t}=-\mathbf{Z}_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
. We denote

\pi_{0}^{\alpha}=(\tau_{\alpha})_{\#}\pi_{0}
,

\mu=(\tau_{\alpha})_{\#}\pi_{\infty}
. Note
that since
p_{\textup{prior}}
is the Gaussian density with zero mean and covariance
matrix
(1/\alpha)\operatorname{Id}
,
\mu
is the Gaussian distribution with zero mean
and identity covariance matrix.

First, using
(
Bakry et al., 2014
, Proposition 4.1.1, Proposition 4.3.1, Theorem
4.2.5)
, we get that for any

t\in[0,T_{\alpha}]
,
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mu)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{t|0}g(x))^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\exp[-2t]\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g^{2}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x),\quad\text{with}\ g(x)=f(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\tilde{x})\mathrm{d}\mu(\tilde{x}).}

(144)

Recall that
(\mathbf{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{t}+\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
. Using

Section

C.2
we have that for any
t\in\left[0,T\right]
,

\mathbf{Z}_{t}
and
\alpha^{1/2}\mathbf{X}_{\alpha^{-1}t}
have the same distribution.
Hence for any
t\in[0,T]
we have

\mathbb{P}_{t}=(\tau_{\alpha}^{-1})_{\#}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha t}
.
Therefore, using that
(\tau_{\alpha})_{\#}\pi_{\infty}=\mu
, that
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}

is Markov and
Section

C.2
, we get that

\displaystyle\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{t|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbb{P}_{t}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\|(\tau_{\alpha})_{\#}\mathbb{P}_{t}-(\tau_{\alpha})_{\#}\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

(145)

\displaystyle=\|\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha t}-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\|\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha t_{0}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(t-t_{0})|0}-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

(146)

Finally, note that we have for any
t\geq t_{0}\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(\mathrm{d}(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha t_{0}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(t-t_{0})|0})/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(t-t_{0})|0}f(x),\quad\text{with}\ f(x)=(\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha t_{0}}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x).

(147)

Let
g=f-1
. Using (
147
), (
144
) and
that
(\tau_{\alpha})_{\#}\pi_{\infty}=\mu
, we get that for any
t\geq t_{0}
with

t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{t|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha t_{0}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(t-t_{0})|0}-\mu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

(148)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(t-t_{0})|0}f(x)-1|\mathrm{d}\mu(x)}

(149)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha(t-t_{0})|0}g(x))^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(x))^{1/2}}

(150)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\exp[-\alpha(t-t_{0})](\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g^{2}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x))^{1/2}}

(151)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\exp[-\alpha(t-t_{0})](\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g^{2}(\alpha^{1/2}x)\mathrm{d}\pi_{\infty}(x))^{1/2}}.

(152)

In addition, we have for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)f(\alpha^{1/2}x)\mathrm{d}\pi_{\infty}(x)}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(\alpha^{-1/2}x)f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)}

(153)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(\alpha^{-1/2}x)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha t_{0}}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{t_{0}}(x).}

(154)

Hence, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
g(\alpha^{1/2}x)=(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{t_{0}}/\mathrm{d}\pi_{\infty})(x)-1
. Combining this result and (
148
) we get that for any
t\geq t_{0}
with

t\in[0,T]

\textstyle{\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{t|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\sqrt{2}\exp[-\alpha(t-t_{0})]\left(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{t_{0}}/\mathrm{d}\pi_{\infty})(x)^{2}\mathrm{d}\pi_{\infty}(x)\right)^{1/2}.}

(155)

Let
t_{0}\in[0,T]
.
We now derive an upper bound for

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{t_{0}}/\mathrm{d}\pi_{\infty})(x)^{2}\mathrm{d}\pi_{\infty}(x)
. We
recall that
\mathbb{P}_{t_{0}}
and
\pi_{\infty}
admit density w.r.t. the Lebesgue
measure denoted
p_{t_{0}}
and
p_{\infty}
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{p_{t_{0}}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{G}_{t_{0}}(x,\tilde{x})\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x}),\quad p_{\infty}(x)=(2\uppi/\alpha)^{-d/2}\exp[-\alpha\left\|x\right\|^{2}/2]},

(156)

where for any
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\mathrm{G}_{t_{0}}(x,\tilde{x})=(2\uppi\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2})^{-d/2}\exp[-\left\|x-m_{t_{0}}(\tilde{x})\right\|^{2}/(2\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2})],

(157)

\displaystyle\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}=(1-\exp[-2\alpha t_{0}])/\alpha,\qquad m_{t_{0}}(\tilde{x})=\exp[-\alpha t_{0}]\tilde{x}.

(158)

Combining this result and Jensen’s inequality we get

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t_{0}}^{2}(x)p_{\infty}^{-1}(x)\mathrm{d}x\leq\alpha^{-d/2}(2\uppi)^{-d/2}\sigma_{t_{0}}^{-2d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x-m_{t_{0}}(\tilde{x})\right\|^{2}/\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|x\right\|^{2}/2]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x}).}

(159)

For any
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|x-m_{t_{0}}(\tilde{x})\right\|^{2}/\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}-\alpha\left\|x\right\|^{2}/2

\displaystyle=\left\|x-m_{t_{0}}(\tilde{x})(2\tilde{\sigma}_{t_{0}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}_{t_{0}})\right\|^{2}/(2\tilde{\sigma}_{t_{0}}^{2})-\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}\phi(\alpha,t_{0})/\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2},

(160)

with

\tilde{\sigma}_{t_{0}}^{2}=(\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}/2)(1-\alpha\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}/2)^{-1}

and

\phi(\alpha,t_{0})=\alpha\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}(1-\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}\alpha)/(2-\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}\alpha)
.
Using this result, we get that

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\|x-m_{t_{0}}(\tilde{x})\|^{2}/\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}+\alpha\|x\|^{2}/2]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})\leq(2\uppi\tilde{\sigma}_{t_{0}}^{2})^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\phi(\alpha,t_{0})\|\tilde{x}\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})},

(161)

Let
\varepsilon=\mathtt{m}/4
and
t_{0}\geq 0
such that

\phi(\alpha,t_{0})\leq\varepsilon
. Using
Section

C.2
, we get that

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x-m_{t_{0}}(\tilde{x})\right\|^{2}/\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}+\alpha\left\|x\right\|^{2}/2]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})\leq(2\uppi\tilde{\sigma}_{t_{0}}^{2})^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x}).}

(162)

Combining this result, the fact that
\sigma_{t_{0}}^{2}\leq\alpha^{-1}
,
(
159
) and that for any
t\geq 0
,

(1-\mathrm{e}^{-t})^{-1}\leq 1+1/t
, we obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t_{0}}^{2}(x)p_{\infty}^{-1}(x)\mathrm{d}x}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq(\alpha^{-1}\tilde{\sigma}_{t_{0}}^{2}\sigma_{t_{0}}^{-4})^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})}

(163)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq(1-\exp[-2\alpha t_{0}])^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})}

(164)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq(1+1/(2\alpha t_{0}))^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x}).}

(165)

Combining this result and (
155
), we get that for any
t>t_{0}

\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{t|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq C_{1}^{a}\exp[-\alpha t],

(166)

with

\textstyle{C_{1}^{a}=\sqrt{2}(1+1/(2\alpha t_{0}))^{d/2}(1+(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\|\tilde{x}\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x}))^{1/2})\exp[\alpha t_{0}].}

(167)

For
t\leq t_{0}
, using that
\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{t|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq 1
we have

\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{t|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq C_{1}^{b}\exp[-\alpha t],\qquad\text{with }C_{1}^{b}=\exp[\alpha t_{0}].

(168)

Let
C_{1}=C_{1}^{a}+C_{1}^{b}
and we have that for any
t\in[0,T]

\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{t|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq C_{1}\exp[-\alpha t].

(169)

(b)

Second assume that
\alpha=0
.

\textstyle{\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(4\uppi T)^{-d/2}|\exp[-\left\|x-\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}/(4T)]-\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{2}/(4T)]|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x}).}

(170)

For any
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
with for any
s\in[0,1]
,

\varphi(s)=\exp[-\left\|x-s\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}/(4T)]
. First, assume that

T\geq 2/\varepsilon
. Using
Section

C.2
, we get that for any

s\in[0,1]

\left|\varphi^{\prime}(s)\right|\leq(1+\varepsilon^{-1})(1+\left\|x\right\|)\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{2}/(8T)]\exp[\varepsilon\left\|y\right\|^{2}]/T.

(171)

Using this result we get that

\displaystyle\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(4\uppi T)^{-d/2}|\exp[-\left\|x-\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}/(4T)]-\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{2}/(4T)]|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})}

(172)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(4\uppi T)^{-d/2}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})(1+\left\|x\right\|)\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{2}/(8T)]\exp[\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}]/T\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})}

(173)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2^{d/2}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(8\uppi T)^{-d/2}(1+\left\|x\right\|)\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{2}/(8T)]\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}]/T\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})}

(174)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2^{d/2}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})(1+2\sqrt{2}d^{1/2}T^{1/2})\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}]/T\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})}.

(175)

In addition, if
T\leq 2/\varepsilon
then

\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq(\varepsilon/2+(\varepsilon/2)^{1/2})^{-1}(T^{-1}+T^{-1/2}).

(176)

Hence, we get that there exists
C_{2}\geq 0
such that

\|\pi_{0}\mathbb{P}_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq C_{2}(T^{-1}+T^{-1/2}),

(177)

with

C_{2}=(\varepsilon/2+(\varepsilon/2)^{1/2})^{-1}+2^{d/2}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})(1+2\sqrt{2}d^{1/2})\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\varepsilon\left\|\tilde{x}\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(\tilde{x})}.

(178)

(c)

Recall that
\mathbb{P}^{R}
is associated with the diffusion

(\mathbf{Y}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
such that for any
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}=b_{1}(t,\mathbf{Y}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathbf{B}_{t},\quad b_{1}(t,x)=\alpha x+2\nabla\log p_{T-t}(x).

(179)

Similarly, for any
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
we have
\mathrm{Q}_{k}=\mathbb{Q}_{t_{k}}

where
\mathbb{Q}
is associated with the
diffusion
(\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
such that for any

(w_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}=b_{2}(t,(\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathbf{B}_{t},

(180)

\displaystyle\textstyle{b_{2}(t,(w_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbbm{1}_{\left[t_{k},t_{k+1}\right)}(t)\left\{2\alpha w_{t_{k}}+s_{\theta}(t_{k},w_{t_{k}})\right\}}

(181)

where for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N\}
,

t_{k}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\gamma_{\ell+1}
. Recall that for any

i\in\{1,2,3\}
there exist
A_{i}\geq 0
and
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
such
that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\|\nabla^{i}\log p_{0}(x)\|\leq A_{i}(1+\|x_{0}\|^{\alpha_{i}}),

(182)

with
\alpha_{1}=1
. Using this result and
Theorem

16
we get
that for any
i\in\{1,2,3\}
there exist
B_{i}\geq 0
and
\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{N}

with
\beta_{1}=1
such that for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]

\|\nabla^{i}\log p_{t}(x_{t})\|\leq B_{i}(1+\left\|x_{t}\right\|^{\beta_{i}}).

(183)

In addition, for any
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\partial_{t}p_{t}(x)=-\mathrm{div}(bp_{t})(x)+\Delta p_{t}(x),

(184)

with
b(x)=-\alpha x
. Therefore, since
\log p\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\left(0,T\right]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
we obtain that
for any
t\in\left(0,T\right]
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\partial_{t}\log p_{t}(x_{t})=-\mathrm{div}(b\log p_{t})(x_{t})+\Delta\log p_{t}(x_{t})+\left\|\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{t})\right\|^{2}.

(185)

Finally, we get that for any
t\in\left(0,T\right]
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\partial_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{t})=-\nabla\mathrm{div}(b\log p_{t})(x_{t})+\nabla\Delta\log p_{t}(x_{t})+\nabla\left\|\nabla\log p_{t}\right\|^{2}(x_{t}).

(186)

Therefore combining this result and (
183
) there exist

\tilde{A}\geq 0
and
\upbeta\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
t\in\left(0,T\right]
,

\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{t})\right\|\leq\tilde{A}(1+\left\|x_{t}\right\|^{\upbeta})
. Hence, for any

t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\left\|\nabla\log p_{t_{2}}(x)-\nabla\log p_{t_{1}}(x)\right\|\leq\tilde{A}\left|t_{2}-t_{1}\right|(1+\left\|x\right\|^{\upbeta}).

(187)

In addition, using (
183
), we have for any
t\in[0,T]
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\|\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{1})-\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla^{2}\log p_{t}((1-s)x_{1}+sx_{2})\|\mathrm{d}s\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|}

(188)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{B_{2}(1+\int_{0}^{1}\left\|(1-s)x_{1}+sx_{2}\right\|^{\beta_{2}}\mathrm{d}s)\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|}

(189)

\displaystyle\leq B_{2}(1+\left\|x_{1}\right\|^{\beta_{2}}+\left\|x_{2}\right\|^{\beta_{2}})\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|.

(190)

Since
s_{\theta}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\nabla\log p\in\mathrm{C}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
using
Section

C.2
, (
187
), (
188
)
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\|\pi_{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}^{2}\leq(1/2)\textstyle{\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\|b_{1}(t,\mathbf{Y}_{t})-b_{2}(t,(\mathbf{Y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}t}

(191)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})-s_{\theta}(\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}})\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}t}

(192)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}]}\mathrm{d}t

(193)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 6\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})-\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}})\|^{2}]}\mathrm{d}t

(194)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+6\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}})-\nabla\log p_{T-{t_{k}}}(\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}})\|^{2}]}\mathrm{d}t

(195)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+6\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\log p_{T-t_{k}}(\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}})-s_{\theta}(t_{k},\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}})\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}t}

(196)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}t}

(197)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 18\sqrt{2}\textstyle{B_{2}^{2}(1+2N_{T}(4\beta_{2}))^{1/2}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}}\|^{4}]^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t}

(198)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+12\tilde{A}^{2}(1+N_{T}(2\upbeta))\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}(t-t_{k})^{2}\mathrm{d}t}+6T\mathtt{M}^{2}

(199)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}t}

(200)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\{18\sqrt{2}\textstyle{B_{2}^{2}(1+2N_{T}(4\beta_{2}))^{1/2}+\alpha^{2}\}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}}\|^{4}]^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t}

(201)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+4\tilde{A}^{2}(1+N_{T}(2\upbeta))\textstyle{\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(t_{k+1}-t_{k})^{3}}+6T\mathtt{M}^{2}

(202)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\{18\sqrt{2}\textstyle{B_{2}^{2}(1+2N_{T}(4\beta_{2}))^{1/2}+\alpha^{2}\}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\mathbf{Y}_{t_{k}}\|^{4}]^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t}

(203)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+4\tilde{A}^{2}(1+N_{T}(2\upbeta))T\bar{\gamma}^{2}+6T\mathtt{M}^{2},

(204)

where for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

N_{T}(\ell)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}\|^{\ell}]
. For any
t\in[0,T]
, let

\mathcal{A}_{t}:\ \mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
the generator
given for any
t\geq 0
,
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\mathcal{A}_{t}(\varphi)(x)=\langle\alpha x+2\nabla\log p_{T-t}(x),\nabla\varphi(x)\rangle+\Delta\varphi(x).

(205)

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, let
V_{\ell}(x)=\left\|x\right\|^{2\ell}
. Hence, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]
we have using (
183
)

\mathcal{A}_{t}(V_{\ell})(x)=2\ell\alpha\left\|x\right\|^{2\ell}+2\ell B_{1}\left\|x\right\|^{2\ell-1}+2\ell B_{1}\left\|x\right\|^{2\ell}+2\ell(2\ell-1)\left\|x\right\|^{2(\ell-1)}.

(206)

Hence, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there exist
\tilde{B}_{\ell}
such that

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]

|\mathcal{A}_{t}(V_{\ell})(x)|\leq\tilde{B}_{\ell}(1+V_{\ell}(x)).

(207)

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

(M_{\ell,t})_{t\in[0,T]}=(V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})-V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{A}_{t}(V_{\ell})(\mathbf{Y}_{s})\mathrm{d}s)_{t\in[0,T]}
is a
local martingale. For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
(\tau_{\ell,k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
a sequence of stopping
times such that
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\tau_{\ell,k}=T
and

(M_{\ell,t\wedge\tau_{\ell,k}})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a martingale.
Using (
207
), we have for any
t\in[0,T]
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}

\mathbb{E}[V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{t\wedge\tau_{\ell,k}})]\leq\mathbb{E}[V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{0})]+\tilde{B}_{\ell}\textstyle{\int_{0}^{t}(1+\mathbb{E}[V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{s\wedge\tau_{\ell,k}})])\mathrm{d}s}.

(208)

Hence, using Grönwall’s lemma we get that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}[V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{t\wedge\tau_{\ell,k}})]<+\infty
. Therefore for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

((M_{\ell,t\wedge\tau_{k}})_{t\in[0,T]})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly
integrable and we have that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

(M_{\ell,t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a martingale. Therefore we get that for
any
t\in[0,T]
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}

\mathbb{E}[V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})]\leq\mathbb{E}[V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{0})]+\tilde{B}_{\ell}\textstyle{\int_{0}^{t}(1+\mathbb{E}[V_{\ell}(\mathbf{Y}_{s})]\mathrm{d}s)}.

(209)

Using Grönwall’s lemma we get that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there exist

\tilde{C}_{\ell}\geq 0
such that

N_{T}(\ell)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}\|^{2\ell}]\leq\tilde{C}_{\ell}\exp[\tilde{B}_{\ell}T].

(210)

We have that for any
s,t\in[0,T]

\textstyle{\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\mathbf{Y}_{s}+\int_{s}^{t}\{\alpha\mathbf{Y}_{u}+2\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{u})\}\mathrm{d}u+\sqrt{2}\int_{s}^{t}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{u}}.

(211)

Using (
187
) and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we have for any
s,t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\mathbf{Y}_{s}\|^{4}]

\displaystyle\leq 64(t-s)^{3}\textstyle{\int_{s}^{t}\{\alpha^{4}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{u}\|^{4}]+16\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{u})\|^{4}]\}\mathrm{d}u+48\sqrt{2}(t-s)^{2}}

(212)

\displaystyle\leq 64(t-s)^{3}\textstyle{\int_{s}^{t}\{\alpha^{4}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{u}\|^{4}]+128B_{1}^{4}(1+\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Y}_{u}\|^{4}])\}\mathrm{d}u+48\sqrt{2}(t-s)^{2}}

(213)

\displaystyle\leq 64(\alpha^{4}+128B_{1}^{4})(1+N_{T}(4))(t-s)^{4}+48\sqrt{2}(t-s)^{2}.

(214)

Combining (
210
) and (
214
) in (
191
) we get that there exist
C_{3}\geq 0
such that

\|\pi_{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{R})_{T|0}-\pi_{\infty}\mathrm{Q}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}^{2}\leq C_{3}\exp[C_{3}T](\bar{\gamma}+\mathtt{M}^{2}),

(215)

We conclude the proof upon combining (
169
) and
(
215
) if
\alpha>0
and (
177
) and
(
215
) if
\alpha=0
.
∎

C.3
General SGM and links with existing works

In this section we describe a general algorithm for SGM in

Section

C.3.1
and show that the formulation (
9
)
encompasses the ones of
(
Song et al., 2021
;
Ho et al., 2020
)
in

Section

C.3.2
.

C.3.1
General SGM algorithm

We first present a general algorithm to compute approximate reverse dynamics,

i.e.
to compute the reverse-time Markov chain associated with the forward process

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=f_{t}(\mathbf{X}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},\qquad\mathbf{X}_{0}\sim p_{\textup{data}}.

(216)

We use the Euler-Maruyama
discretization of (
216
),
i.e.
let
X_{0}\sim p_{\textup{data}}
and for
any
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

X_{k+1}=X_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}f_{k}(X_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}.

(217)

In general, we do not have that
p(x_{k}|x_{0})
is a Gaussian density contrary to

Song and Ermon, 2019
;
Ho et al., 2020
. However, in this case, we obtain that
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\textstyle{p_{k+1}(x)=(4\uppi\gamma_{k+1})^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{k}(\tilde{x})\exp[-\left\|\mathcal{T}_{k+1}(\tilde{x})-x\right\|^{2}/(4\gamma_{k+1})]\mathrm{d}\tilde{x},}

(218)

with
\mathcal{T}_{k+1}(x)=\tilde{x}+\gamma_{k+1}f_{k}(\tilde{x})
.
Therefore, we get that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{(2\gamma_{k+1}p_{k+1}(x))\nabla\log p_{k+1}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1}(\tilde{x})-x)p_{k}(\tilde{x})\exp[-\left\|\mathcal{T}_{k+1}(\tilde{x})-x\right\|^{2}/(4\gamma_{k+1})]\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}}.

(219)

Hence, we get that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\nabla\log p_{k+1}(x)=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}_{k+1}(X_{k})-X_{k+1}|X_{k+1}=x]/(2\gamma_{k+1})=-(2\gamma_{k+1})^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[Z_{k+1}|X_{k+1}=x].

(220)

From this formula we derive a regression problem similar to the one of

Section

2.1
. We obtain

Algorithm

2
. We highlight a few differences between
our approach and the ones of
Song and Ermon, 2019
;
Ho et al., 2020
:

(a)

As emphasized in (
220
), the regression problem in

Algorithm

2
is different from the one usually
considered in SGM which restrict themselves to the setting
f_{k}(x)=\alpha x

with
\alpha=0

(
Song and Ermon, 2019
)
or
\alpha>0

(
Ho et al., 2020
)
.

(b)

In the present algorithm we do not use any corrector step

(
Song et al., 2021
)
at sampling time. Note that the use of a corrector step
is only justified in the context of classical SGM algorithms and not the DSB
method introduced in
Section

3.3
. This is because, we do not
have access to the marginal of the time-reverse density during the IPF
iterations contrary to classical SGMs.

(c)

Finally, we do not present the Exponential Moving Average (EMA) procedure

Song and Ermon, 2020
which is key to prevent the network from
oscillating. Contrary to the corrector step, this technique can easily be
incorporated in
Algorithm

2
.

Further comments and additional techniques are presented in
Appendix

I
.

Algorithm 2

Generalized score-matching

1:

Inputs:

(b_{k})_{k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}

(nb. of iterations),
M\in\mathbb{N}
(batch size),
N_{\textup{epochs}}

(nb. of epochs),
(\gamma_{k})_{k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}}
(stepsizes),

\{s_{\theta}\,:\;\theta\in\Theta\}
(neural network),

\mathrm{opt}
(optimizer),
p_{\textup{prior}}
(prior distribution),
\lambda(k)
(weights)

2:

for

n_{\textup{epoch}}=0,\dots,N_{\textup{epoch}}-1

do

3:

for

j\in\{1,\dots,M\}

do

4:

X_{0}^{j}\sim p_{\textup{data}}

5:

for

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

do

6:

X_{k+1}^{j}=X_{k}^{j}+\gamma_{k+1}f_{k}(X_{k}^{j})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}^{j}

7:

end

for

8:

end

for

9:

\widehat{\ell}(\theta)=M^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\lambda(k)/(2\gamma_{k+1})\sum_{j=1}^{M}\|\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}s_{\theta}(k+1,X_{k+1}^{j})+Z_{k+1}^{j}\|^{2}

10:

\theta_{n_{\textup{epoch}}+1}=\mathrm{opt}(\ell,\theta_{n_{\textup{epoch}}})

11:

end

for

12:

X_{N}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}

13:

for

k\in\{N-1,\dots,0\}

do

14:

X_{k}=X_{k+1}+\gamma_{k+1}\{-f_{k}(X_{k+1})+2s_{\theta_{N_{\textup{epoch}}}}(k+1,X_{k+1})\}+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}

15:

end

for

16:

Output:

X_{0}

C.3.2
Links with existing work

In this section, we show that we can recover the training and sampling algorithm
of
Song and Ermon, 2019
and
Ho et al., 2020
by reversing homogeneous
diffusions. Note that
Song et al., 2021
identified links with non-homogeneous
SDEs. We explicitly characterize the fundamental difference between
the approaches of
Song and Ermon, 2019
;
Ho et al., 2020
by identifying the
two corresponding forward homogeneous processes (Brownian motion or
Ornstein-Ulhenbeck).

Brownian motion

First, we show that we can recover the sampling procedure and the loss function of

Song and Ermon, 2019
by reversing a Brownian motion. Assume that we have

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},\qquad\mathbf{X}_{0}\sim p_{\textup{data}}.

(221)

In what follows we define

\{Y_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
such that
\{Y_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
approximates

\{\mathbf{X}_{T-t_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
for a specific sequence of times

\{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}\in\left[0,T\right]^{N}
. We recall that the time-reversal of
(
221
) is associated with the following SDE

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}=2\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}.

(222)

The Euler-Maruyama discretization of (
222
) yields for any

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{k+1}=\tilde{Y}_{k}+2\gamma_{k+1}\nabla\log p_{T-t_{k}}(\tilde{Y}_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}.

(223)

where
\{\gamma_{k+1}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
is a sequence of stepsizes and for any

k\in\{0,\dots,N\}
,
t_{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\gamma_{j+1}
. A close form
for
\{\nabla\log p_{T-t_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
is not available and in practice we
consider

Y_{k+1}=Y_{k}+2\gamma_{k+1}s_{\theta^{\star}}(T-t_{k},Y_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1},

(224)

where for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
,
s_{\theta^{\star}}(T-t_{k},\cdot)
is an
approximation of
\nabla\log p_{T-t_{k}}
. The sampling procedure
(
224
) is similar to the one of
Song and Ermon, 2019
upon
setting (with the notations of
Song and Ermon, 2019
)
T\leftarrow 1
in

(
Song and Ermon, 2019
, Algorithm 1)
(no corrector step),

\alpha_{k}/2\leftarrow\gamma_{k}
and

\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\cdot,\sigma_{k+1})\leftarrow 2s_{\theta^{\star}}(T-t_{k},\cdot)
. It remains to show that
2s_{\theta^{\star}}
is the solution to the
same regression problem as
\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in
(
Song and Ermon, 2019
, Equation
6)
. First, note that for any
t>0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

we have

\textstyle{p_{t}(x_{t})=(4\uppi t)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\exp[-\left\|x_{t}-x_{0}\right\|^{2}/(4t)]\mathrm{d}x_{0}.}

(225)

Therefore, we get that for any
t>0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{t})}\textstyle{=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x_{0}-x_{t})/(2t)p_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}}={\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{X}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{t}|\mathbf{X}_{t}=x_{t}]/(2t).

(226)

Hence, we have that
\theta^{\star}
satisfies the following regression problem

\textstyle{\theta^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\lambda(k)\mathbb{E}[\|(\mathbf{X}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{T-t_{k}})/(T-t_{k})-2s_{\theta}(T-t_{k},\mathbf{X}_{T-t_{k}})\|]}.

(227)

Note that this loss function is similar to the one of
(
Song and Ermon, 2019
, Equation 6)

upon letting
\sigma_{k+1}^{2}\leftarrow 2(T-t_{k})
and
L\leftarrow N
. Hence,
the two recursions approximately define the same scheme if for any

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
,

\sigma_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{k+1}^{2}\approx(1/2)\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\alpha_{j+1}
since
t_{0}=0

implies
T=(1/2)\sigma_{1}^{2}
. In
Song and Ermon, 2019
we have for any

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
,
\sigma_{k}^{2}=\kappa^{N-k}\sigma_{N}^{2}
(recall that

N=L
) with
\kappa>1
. In addition, we have for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
,

\alpha_{k}=\varepsilon\sigma_{k}^{2}/\sigma_{N}^{2}
for some
\varepsilon>0
. We get that

\displaystyle(1/2)\textstyle{\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\alpha_{j+1}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{(\varepsilon/2)\kappa^{N-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\kappa^{-j}}

(228)

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon/2)(\kappa^{N-1}-\kappa^{N-k-1})/(1-\kappa^{-1})

(229)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon/(2(1-\kappa^{-1})\sigma_{N}^{2})(\sigma_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{k+1}^{2}).

(230)

Hence, the two schemes are identical if

\varepsilon=2(1-\kappa^{-1})\sigma_{N}^{2}
. In practice in

Song and Ermon, 2019
the authors choose
N=10
,
\sigma_{N}=10^{-2}
,

\sigma_{1}=1
(hence
\kappa=10^{4/9}
) and
\varepsilon=2\times 10^{-5}
. We
have
2(1-\kappa^{-1})\sigma_{N}^{2}\approx 1.3\times 10^{-4}
which has one
order of difference with
\varepsilon
.

Ornstein-Ulhenbeck

Second, we show that we can recover the sampling procedure and the loss function
of
Ho et al., 2020
by reversing an Ornstein-Ulhenbeck process. Contrary
to the previous analysis we do not show a strict equivalence between the two
recursions but instead that our algorithm can be seen as a first order
approximation of the one of
Ho et al., 2020
.

In this section, we consider the following diffusion

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=-\alpha\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},\qquad\mathbf{X}_{0}\sim p_{\textup{data}}.

(231)

In what follows we define

\{Y_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
such that
\{Y_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
approximates

\{\mathbf{X}_{T-t_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
for a specific sequence of times

\{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}\in\left[0,T\right]^{N}
. We recall that the time-reversal of
(
231
) is associated with the following SDE

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\{\alpha\mathbf{Y}_{t}+2\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})\}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}.

(232)

In what follows, we fix
\alpha=1
. The Euler-Maruyama discretization of
(
232
) yields for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{k+1}=(1+\gamma_{k+1})\tilde{Y}_{k}+2\gamma_{k+1}\nabla\log p_{T-t_{k}}(\tilde{Y}_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}.

(233)

where
\{\gamma_{k+1}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
is a sequence of stepsizes and for any

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
,
t_{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\gamma_{j+1}
. A close
form for
\{\nabla\log p_{T-t_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
is not available and in practice
we consider

Y_{k+1}=(1+\gamma_{k+1})Y_{k}+2\gamma_{k+1}s_{\theta^{\star}}(T-t_{k},Y_{k})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}.

(234)

In
(
Ho et al., 2020
, Equation 11)
the backward
recursion is given for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

Y_{k+1}=\alpha_{N-k}^{-1/2}(Y_{k}-\beta_{N-k}/(1-\bar{\alpha}_{N-k})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\theta}(Y_{k},T-t_{k}))+\sigma_{N-k}Z_{k+1}.

(235)

In (
235
) we set
\sigma_{k}^{2}=\beta_{k}
as suggested in

Ho et al., 2020
where for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\displaystyle\sigma_{k+1}^{2}=\beta_{k+1},\quad\alpha_{k+1}=1-\beta_{k+1},\quad\textstyle{\bar{\alpha}_{k+1}=\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\alpha_{i}.}

(236)

We consider a first-order expansion of (
235
) with respect to

\{\beta_{k+1}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
. We obtain the following recursion for any

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

Y_{k+1}=(1+\beta_{N-k}/2)Y_{k}-\beta_{N-k}/(1-\bar{\alpha}_{N-k})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\theta}(Y_{k},T-t_{k})+\sqrt{\beta_{N-k}}Z_{k+1}.

(237)

This last recursion is equivalent to (
234
) upon
setting
\beta_{N-k}\leftarrow 2\gamma_{k+1}
and

-\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\theta}(\cdot,T-t_{k})/(1-\bar{\alpha}_{N-k})^{1/2}\leftarrow-s_{\theta^{\star}}(T-t_{k},\cdot)
.
It remains to show that
s_{\theta^{\star}}
is the solution to the same regression
problem as
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\theta}/(1-\bar{\alpha}_{N-\cdot})
in
(
Ho et al., 2020
, Equation 12)
. First, note that for any

t>0
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{p_{t}(x_{t})=(2\uppi\bar{\sigma}_{t}^{2})^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\exp[-\left\|x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0}\right\|^{2}/(2\bar{\sigma}_{t}^{2})]\mathrm{d}x_{0}},

(238)

with

c_{t}^{2}=\exp[-2t],\qquad\bar{\sigma}_{t}^{2}=1-\exp[-2t].

(239)

Therefore we get that for any
t\in\left[0,T\right]
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\nabla\log p_{t}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(c_{t}x_{0}-x_{t})p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\exp[-\left\|x_{t}-c_{t}x_{0}\right\|^{2}/(2\bar{\sigma}_{t}^{2})]\mathrm{d}x_{0}}

(240)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[c_{t}\mathbf{X}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{t}\middle|\mathbf{X}_{t}=x_{t}\right]/\bar{\sigma}_{t}^{2}=-{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{Z}\middle|\mathbf{X}_{t}=x_{t}\right]/\bar{\sigma}_{t},

(241)

where we recall that
\mathbf{X}_{t}
has the same distribution as

c_{t}\mathbf{X}_{0}+\bar{\sigma}_{t}\mathbf{Z}
, with
\mathbf{Z}
a
d
-dimensional Gaussian random
variable with zero mean and identity covariance matrix. Hence, we have that

\theta^{\star}
satisfies the following regression problem

\textstyle{\theta^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\lambda(k)\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}/\sigma_{T-t_{k}}+s_{\theta}(T-t_{k},\mathbf{X}_{T-t_{k}})\|]}.

(242)

Note that we have

\textstyle{\sum_{i=1}^{N-k}\beta_{i}=\sum_{i=k}^{N-1}\beta_{N-i}=2\sum_{i=k}^{N-1}\gamma_{i+1}=2(T-t_{k}).}

(243)

Using this result we have for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\textstyle{1-\bar{\alpha}_{N-k}=1-\exp[-\sum_{i=1}^{N-k}\log(1-\beta_{i})]\approx 1-\exp[-\sum_{i=1}^{N-k}\beta_{i}]\approx\bar{\sigma}_{T-t_{k}}^{2}.}

(244)

Let
\tilde{\theta}^{\star}
the minimizer of
(
Ho et al., 2020
, Equation
12)
we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}^{\star}

\displaystyle\approx\textstyle{\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(2\alpha_{N-k}(1-\alpha_{N-k}))^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}-\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\theta}(\mathbf{X}_{T-t_{k}},T-t_{k})\|^{2}]}

(245)

\displaystyle\approx\textstyle{\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(2\alpha_{N-k})^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}/(1-\alpha_{N-k})^{1/2}-\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\theta}(\mathbf{X}_{T-t_{k}},T-t_{k})/(1-\alpha_{N-k})^{1/2}\|^{2}]}

(246)

\displaystyle\approx\textstyle{\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(2\alpha_{N-k})^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}/\bar{\sigma}_{T-t_{k}}+s_{\theta}(T-t_{k},\mathbf{X}_{T-t_{k}})\|^{2}]}.

(247)

Hence the two regression problems are approximately the same (for small values
of
\{\beta_{k+1}\}_{k=0}^{N-1}
) if we set
\lambda(k)=(2\alpha_{N-k})^{-1}
.

Appendix D
Schrödinger bridges with potentials and DSB recursion

In this section, we start by proving an additive formula for the
Kullback–Leibler divergence in
Section

D.1
following

Léonard, 2014a
. We recall the classical IPF formulation using
potentials in
Section

D.2
. Then,
Section

3.2
is
proved in
Section

D.3
. Finally, we highlight a link between
our formulation and autoencoders in
Section

D.4
.

D.1
Additive formula for the Kullback–Leibler divergence

In this section, we prove a formula for the Kullback–Leibler divergence
following the proof of
Léonard, 2014a
which extends the result to
unbounded measures defined on the space of right-continuous left-limited
functions from
\left[0,T\right]
. We recall that a Polish space is a complete metric
separable space.

We start with the following disintegration theorem for probability measures.

Theorem 22
.

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
and
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
be two Polish spaces. Let

\pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{X})
and
\varphi:\ \mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
measurable. Then there
exists a Markov kernel
\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}:\ \mathsf{Y}\times\mathcal{X}\to\left[0,1\right]

such that the following hold:

(a)

For any
y\in\mathsf{Y}
,
\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}(y,\varphi^{-1}(\{y\}))=1
.

(b)

For any
f:\ \mathsf{X}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
measurable we have

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\pi(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Y}}\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}(y,f)\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}(y)}
,

where
\pi_{\varphi}=\varphi_{\#}\pi
.

Proof.

See
(
Dellacherie and Meyer, 1988
, III-70)
for instance.
∎

\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}
is called the disintegration of
\pi
w.r.t.
\varphi
and
is unique, see
(
Dellacherie and Meyer, 1988
, III-70)
. In particular, for any

\mathsf{X}
-valued random variable
X
with distribution
\pi
we have

{\mathbb{E}}[f(X)|\varphi(X)]=\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}(\varphi(X),f)
. Next we
prove the following proposition, see
(
Léonard, 2014a
, Proposition A.13)

for an extension to unbounded measures. In what follows, for any

\varphi:\ \mathsf{X}to\mathbb{R}
measurable we denote

\pi_{\varphi}=\varphi_{\#}\pi
.

Proposition 23
.

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
and
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
be two Polish spaces. Let

\pi,\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{X})
and
\varphi:\ \mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
measurable. Assume
that
\pi\ll\mu
. Then the following holds:

(a)

\pi_{\varphi}\ll\mu_{\varphi}

(b)

There exists
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{Y}
with
\pi_{\varphi}(\mathsf{A})=1
such that for any
y\in\mathsf{A}
,
\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\pi}(y,\cdot)\ll\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\mu}(y,\cdot)
.

In addition, we have for any
y\in\mathsf{Y}
,
y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{A}
and
x\in\mathsf{X}

(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(y)=\mathrm{K}^{\mu}_{\varphi}(y,(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)),\quad(\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\pi}(y^{\prime},\cdot)/\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\mu}(y^{\prime},\cdot))(x)=(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)/(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(y^{\prime}).

(248)

Finally, there exists
\mathsf{C}\in\mathcal{X}
with
\pi(\mathsf{C})=1
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{C}
we have

(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)=(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(\varphi(x))(\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\pi}(\varphi(x),\cdot)/\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\mu}(\varphi(x),\cdot))(x).

(249)

Proof.

Let
f:\ \mathsf{X}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
measurable. Using
Theorem

22
we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\varphi}[f]

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(\varphi(x))\mathrm{d}\pi(x)}=\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(\varphi(x))(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)}=\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(y)\mathrm{K}^{\mu}_{\varphi}(y,(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu))\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}(y),}

(250)

which concludes the first part of the proof. For the second part of the proof,
let

\mathsf{B}=\{y\in\mathsf{Y}\,:\;(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(y)=0\}
. We have

\textstyle{0=\int_{\mathsf{Y}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{B}}(y)(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}(y)=\pi_{\varphi}(\mathsf{B}).}

(251)

Therefore, there exists
\mathsf{A}_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}
such that

\pi_{\varphi}(\mathsf{A}_{1})=1
and for any
y\in\mathsf{A}_{1}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(y)>0
. Let

g:\ \mathsf{Y}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
. Using
Theorem

22
we have

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{X}}g(\varphi(x))f(x)\mathrm{d}\pi(x)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}g(\varphi(x))f(x)(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Y}}g(y)\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\mu}(y,f\times(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu))\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}(y).}

(252)

Similarly, using
Theorem

22
we have

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{X}}g(\varphi(x))f(x)\mathrm{d}\pi(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Y}}g(y)\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\pi}(y,f)\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}(y)=\int_{\mathsf{Y}}g(y)\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\pi}(y,f)(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(y)\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}(y).}

(253)

Hence, we get that there exists
\mathsf{A}_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
with

\mu_{\varphi}(\mathsf{A}_{2})=1
(hence
\pi_{\varphi}(\mathsf{A}_{2})=1
) such that for any

y\in\mathsf{A}_{2}
we have

\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\pi}(y,f)(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(y)=\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\mu}(y,f\times(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)).

(254)

We conclude upon letting
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}_{1}\cap\mathsf{A}_{2}
and using the fact that
for any
y\in\mathsf{A}
,
(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(y)>0
.
Finally, since
\pi_{\varphi}(\mathsf{A})=1
if and only if
\pi(\varphi^{-1}(\mathsf{A}))=1
, we have for any
x\in\varphi^{-1}(\mathsf{A})

(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)=(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(\varphi(x))(\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\pi}(\varphi(x),\cdot)/\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}_{\varphi}^{\mu}(\varphi(x),\cdot))(x),

(255)

which concludes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to state the additive formula.

Proposition 24
.

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
and
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
be two Polish spaces and

\pi,\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{X})
with
\pi\ll\mu
. Then for any
\varphi:\ \mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
we have

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)=\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{\varphi}|\mu_{\varphi})+\int_{\mathsf{Y}}\operatorname{KL}(\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}(y,\cdot)|\mathrm{K}^{\mu}_{\varphi}(y,\cdot))\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}(y).}

(256)

Proof.

First assume that

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left|\log((\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x))\right|\mathrm{d}\pi(x)=+\infty
. Then, using
Section

D.1
we have

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left|\log((\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(\varphi(x)))\right|\mathrm{d}\pi(x)=+\infty
or

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left|\log((\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}(\varphi(x),\cdot)/\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}^{\mu}_{\varphi}(\varphi(x),\cdot))(x))\right|\mathrm{d}\pi(x)=+\infty
,
i.e.
either
\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{\varphi}|\mu_{\varphi})=+\infty
or

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\operatorname{KL}(\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}(\varphi(x),\cdot)|\mathrm{K}^{\mu}_{\varphi}(\varphi(x),\cdot))\mathrm{d}\pi(x)=+\infty
using

Theorem

22
, which concludes the first part of the proof. Second, assume
that
\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left|\log((\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x))\right|\mathrm{d}\pi(x)<+\infty
. Using Pinsker’s inequality
(
Bakry et al., 2014
, Equation
5.2.2)
we get that

\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{\varphi}|\mu_{\varphi})<+\infty
,
i.e.

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left|\log((\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi})(\varphi(x)))\right|\mathrm{d}\pi(x)<+\infty
. Hence, we get that

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left|\log((\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}(\varphi(x),\cdot)/\mathrm{d}\mathrm{K}^{\mu}_{\varphi}(\varphi(x),\cdot))(x))\right|\mathrm{d}\pi(x)<+\infty
. Therefore
we have

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)=\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{\varphi}|\mu_{\varphi})+\int_{\mathsf{Y}}\operatorname{KL}(\mathrm{K}^{\pi}_{\varphi}(y,\cdot)|\mathrm{K}^{\mu}_{\varphi}(y,\cdot))\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varphi}(y)}

(257)

which concludes the proof
∎

We emphasize that in the case where
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\varphi=\mathrm{proj}_{0}
the projection on the first variable and
\pi
,

\mu
admit densities w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure denoted
p
and
q
such that
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
p(x,y)=p_{0}(x)p_{1|0}(y|x)
and

q(x,y)=q_{0}(x)q_{1|0}(y|x)
then one can avoid using disintegration theory and

Section

D.1
can be proved directly.

D.2
Iterative Proportional Fitting via potentials

In this section, before recalling the usual definition of the IPF via potentials
we provide a condition under which the IPF sequence is well-defined which is
used throughout
Section

3.2
.

Proposition 25
.

Assume that there exists
\tilde{\pi}\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
such that

\tilde{\pi}_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}
,
\tilde{\pi}_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}
and

\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|\pi^{0})<+\infty
. Then the IPF sequence is well-defined.

Proof.

We prove the existence of the IPF sequence by recursion. First, note that

\pi^{1}
is well-defined since
\tilde{\pi}\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
with

\tilde{\pi}_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}
and
\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|\pi^{0})<+\infty
. Second,
assume that the sequence is well-defined up to
n
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Using

(
Csiszár, 1975
, Theorem 2.2)
we have

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|\pi^{0})=\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|\pi^{n})+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{j+1}|\pi^{j}).}

(258)

Hence
\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|\pi^{n})<+\infty
. Using that
\tilde{\pi}_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}

if
n
is odd and that
\tilde{\pi}_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}
if
n
is even, we get that

\pi^{n+1}
is well-defined, which concludes the proof.
∎

We now introduce the IPF using potentials. This construction is not new and can
be found in

Bernton et al., 2019
;
Chen et al., 2016
;
Chen et al., 2021b
;
Pavon et al., 2021
;
Peyré and Cuturi, 2019

for instance (in continuous state spaces). In discrete settings the recursion
can be found in the following earlier works

Kruithof, 1937
;
Deming and Stephan, 1940
;
Fortet, 1940
;
Sinkhorn and Knopp, 1967
;
Kullback, 1968
;
Ruschendorf et al., 1995
. The
IPF is defined by the following recursion
\pi^{0}=p
given in
(
2
) and for
n\geq 0

\displaystyle\pi^{2n+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1},\ \pi_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\},

(259)

\displaystyle\pi^{2n+2}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n+1})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1},\ \pi_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}\right\}.

(260)

In the classical IPF presentation we obtain under mild assumptions that

\pi^{2n+1}
admits a density
q^{n}
w.r.t the Lebesgue measure and that

\pi^{2n}
admits a density
p^{n}
w.r.t the Lebesgue measure, given by the
following expressions

\displaystyle\textstyle{q^{n}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{data}}^{n}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}p^{n+1}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})},

(261)

\displaystyle\textstyle{p^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}p^{n+1}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})},

(262)

where
(p_{\textup{data}}^{n}(x_{0}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(p^{n}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are
densities which are iteratively computed, with
p^{0}_{k+1|k}=p_{k+1|k}
.

In the context of generative modelling the derivation (
261
) is
not useful because it does not provide a generative model,
i.e.
a probabilistic
transition from
p_{\textup{prior}}
to
p_{\textup{data}}
but instead defines a transition from

p_{\textup{data}}
to
p_{\textup{prior}}
. Therefore, in this section only, we reverse the roles of

p_{\textup{prior}}
and
p_{\textup{data}}
and consider a reference density
\bar{p}
such that for
any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
we have

\textstyle{\bar{p}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}_{k+1|k}(x_{k+1}|x_{k}).}

(263)

Then, we consider the following recursion
\pi^{0}=\bar{p}
given in
(
263
) and for
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\pi^{2n+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1},\ \pi_{N}=p_{\textup{data}}\right\},

(264)

\displaystyle\pi^{2n+2}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n+1})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1},\ \pi_{0}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\}.

(265)

Again, we emphasize that the roles of
p_{\textup{prior}}
and
p_{\textup{data}}
are exchanged in this formulation.
Using the classical IPF presentation we obtain the following expressions under mild assumptions

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{prior}}^{n}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k}),}

(266)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k}).}

(267)

In this case, we get that
\pi^{2n+1}
(approximately) defines a generative model
for large values of
n\in\mathbb{N}
since it provides a transition from to

p_{\textup{prior}}
to (approximately)
p_{\textup{data}}
. In the following proposition we give the
precise statement corresponding to (
266
). We assume that

\bar{p}^{0}=\bar{p}
.

Proposition 26
.

Assume that
\operatorname{KL}(p_{\textup{prior}}\otimes p_{\textup{data}}|\bar{p}_{0,N})<+\infty
. Then

(\pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
given by (
264
) is
well-defined and for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that
\pi^{2n+1}
and

\pi^{2n+2}
admit a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measures denoted
\bar{q}^{n}

and
\bar{p}^{n+1}
. In addition, we have for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{prior}}^{n}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})},

(268)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})},

(269)

where for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

p_{\textup{prior}}^{n}(x_{0})=\psi_{0}^{n}(x_{0})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{0}),\quad\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})=\bar{p}^{n}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\psi_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})/\psi_{k}^{n}(x_{k}),

(270)

with

\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{N})/\bar{p}_{N}^{n}(x_{N}),\quad\textstyle{\psi_{k}^{n}(x_{k})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})\bar{p}^{n}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\mathrm{d}x_{k+1}.}

(271)

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\pi}=(p_{\textup{prior}}\otimes p_{\textup{data}})\bar{p}_{|0,N}
. Using

Section

D.1
we get that

\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|\bar{p})=\operatorname{KL}(p_{\textup{prior}}\otimes p_{\textup{data}}|\bar{p}_{0,N})<+\infty
. Using

Section

D.2
the IPF sequence is well-defined. In addition, using

(
Csiszár, 1975
, Theorem 3.1)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists

\psi_{N}^{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
such that for any

x_{0:N}\in\mathsf{A}
with
\tilde{\pi}(\mathsf{A})=1
we have

\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=\bar{p}^{n}(x_{0:N})\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N}).

(272)

Since
\tilde{\pi}
is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure we get that for any

x_{0:N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=\bar{p}^{n}(x_{0:N})\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N}).

(273)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We have for any
x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

p_{\textup{data}}(x_{N})=\bar{q}^{n}(x_{N})=\bar{p}^{n}_{N}(x_{N})\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})
. Hence, we get
that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{N})/\bar{p}^{n}_{N}(x_{N})
. For
any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
let

\textstyle{\psi_{k}^{n}(x_{k})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})\bar{p}^{n}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\mathrm{d}x_{k+1}.}

(274)

We obtain that for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}

\textstyle{\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{0})\psi_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}(\bar{p}^{n}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\psi_{k+1}(x_{k+1})/\psi_{k}(x_{k})).}

(275)

Hence, we get that for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0})=p_{\textup{prior}}^{n}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})
. Using

Section

D.1
we get that for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{0})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})
, which
concludes the proof.
∎

The previous expression is not symmetric and the IPF iterations appear as a
policy refinement of the original forward dynamic
\bar{p}
. In the next proposition
we present another potential formulation of the IPF iterations which is symmetric.

Proposition 27
.

Assume that
\operatorname{KL}(p_{\textup{prior}}\otimes p_{\textup{data}}|q_{0,N})<+\infty
. Then

(\pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
given by (
264
) is
well-defined and for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that
\pi^{2n+1}
and

\pi^{2n+2}
admit a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measures denoted
\bar{q}^{n}

and
\bar{p}^{n+1}
. In addition, we have for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=\varphi_{0}^{n}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})},

(276)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=\varphi_{0}^{n+1}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})},

(277)

where for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\displaystyle\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{N})/\varphi_{N}^{n}(x_{N}),\quad\textstyle{\psi_{k}^{n}(x_{k})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})\bar{p}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\mathrm{d}x_{k+1},}

(278)

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}^{n+1}(x_{0})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{0})/\psi_{0}^{n}(x_{0}),\quad\textstyle{\varphi_{k+1}^{n+1}(x_{k+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{k}^{n+1}(x_{k})\bar{p}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\mathrm{d}x_{k},}

(279)

and
\varphi_{0}^{0}=p_{\textup{prior}}
and
\psi_{N}^{-1}=1
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\pi}=(p_{\textup{prior}}\otimes p_{\textup{data}})q_{|0,N}
. Using

Section

D.1
we get that

\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|q)=\operatorname{KL}(p_{\textup{prior}}\otimes p_{\textup{data}}|p_{0,N})<+\infty
. Using

Section

D.2
the IPF sequence is well-defined. In addition, using

(
Csiszár, 1975
, Theorem 3.1)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists

\psi_{N}^{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
such that for any

x_{0:N}\in\mathsf{A}
with
\tilde{\pi}(\mathsf{A})=1
we have

\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=\bar{p}^{n}(x_{0:N})\tilde{\psi}_{N}^{n}(x_{N}),\qquad\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})\tilde{\varphi}_{0}^{n}(x_{0}).

(280)

Since
\tilde{\pi}
is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure we get that for any

x_{0:N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=\bar{p}^{n}(x_{0:N})\tilde{\psi}_{N}^{n}(x_{N}),\qquad\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})\tilde{\varphi}_{0}^{n}(x_{0}).

(281)

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\psi_{N}^{n}=\psi_{N}^{n-1}\tilde{\psi}^{n}_{N}
and

\varphi_{0}^{n+1}=\varphi_{0}^{n}\tilde{\varphi}_{0}^{n}
. By recursion, we get
that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{q}^{n}(x_{0:N})=\varphi_{0}^{n}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})},

(282)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bar{p}^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=\varphi_{0}^{n+1}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\bar{p}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N}).}

(283)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\bar{q}^{n}_{N}(x_{N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{N})=\bar{p}^{n}_{N}(x_{N})\tilde{\psi}_{N}^{n}(x_{n}).

(284)

In addition, for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
and
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
we define

\varphi_{k+1}^{n+1}(x_{k+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{k}^{n+1}(x_{k})\bar{p}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})\mathrm{d}x_{k}
. We have for any
x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\bar{p}_{N}^{n}(x_{N})=\varphi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})\psi_{N}^{n-1}(x_{n})
. Combining this result
with (
284
) we get that for any
x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\psi_{N}^{n}(x_{N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{N})/\varphi^{n}_{N}(x_{N}).

(285)

Similarly, we get that for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\varphi_{0}^{n+1}(x_{0})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{0})/\psi_{0}^{n}(x_{0})
, which concludes the
proof.
∎

D.3
Proof of
Section

3.2

Let
\tilde{\pi}=(p_{\textup{prior}}\otimes p_{\textup{data}})p_{|0,N}
. Using

Section

D.1
we get that

\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|p)=\operatorname{KL}(p_{\textup{prior}}\otimes p_{\textup{data}}|p_{0,N})<+\infty
. Using
Section

D.2
the IPF sequence is
well-defined. Note that
\pi^{0}
admits a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure
given by
p>0
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that
p^{n}>0
is given for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
by

\textstyle{p^{n}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}q^{n-1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k}).}

(286)

Using

Section

D.1
we get that for any
\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
such
that
\pi_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}
we have

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n})=\operatorname{KL}(p_{\textup{prior}}|\pi^{2n}_{0})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{|N}|\pi^{2n}_{|N})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\mathrm{d}x_{N}.}

(287)

Hence, we have that
\pi^{2n+1}=p_{\textup{prior}}\pi^{2n}_{|N}
. Since
p^{n}>0
we
get that for any
\pi^{2n}_{|N}
satisfies for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X})
and
x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\pi^{2n}_{|N}(\mathsf{A}|x_{N})=\int_{\mathsf{A}}p^{n}(x_{0:N})/p^{n}(x_{N})\mathrm{d}x_{0:N}\updelta_{x_{N}}(\mathsf{A}_{N})}.

(288)

Therefore,
\pi^{2n+1}
admits a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure denoted

q^{n}
and given for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}
by

\displaystyle q^{n}(x_{0:N})

\displaystyle=p^{n}(x_{0:N})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})/p^{n}(x_{N})

(289)

\displaystyle\textstyle{=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}p^{n}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})p^{n}(x_{k})/p^{n}(x_{k+1})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}p^{n}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})},

(290)

where we have used (
286
).
Note that
q^{n}>0
. Similarly, we get that for any
x_{0:N}\in\mathcal{X}

\textstyle{p^{n+1}(x_{0:N})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}q^{n}(x_{k+1}|x_{k}).}

(291)

Note that again that
p^{n+1}>0
. We conclude by recursion.

D.4
Link with autoencoders

Consider the maximum likelihood problem

\textstyle{q^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}\{\mathbb{E}_{p_{\textup{data}}}[\log q_{0}(X_{0})]\,:\;q\in\mathscr{P}_{d}(\mathcal{X}),\ q_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}\},}

(292)

where
\mathscr{P}_{d}(\mathcal{X})
is the subset of the probability distribution over
\mathcal{X}

which admit a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure.
Using Jensen’s inequality we have for any
q\in\mathscr{P}_{d}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{\textup{data}}}[\log q_{0}(X_{0})]

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log(\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N-1}}q(x_{0:N})p(x_{1:N}|x_{0})/p(x_{1:N}|x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{1:N})p_{0}(x_{0})\mathrm{d}x_{0}}

(293)

\displaystyle\geq\textstyle{\int_{\mathcal{X}}\log(q(x_{0:N})/p(x_{1:N}|x_{0}))p(x_{0:N})\mathrm{d}x_{0:N}}\geq-\operatorname{KL}(p|q)-\mathrm{H}(p_{0}).

(294)

This Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO) is similar to the one identified in

Ho et al., 2020
. Maximizing this ELBO is equivalent to solving the
following problem

\textstyle{q^{0}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{\operatorname{KL}(q|p)\,:\;q\in\mathscr{P}_{d}(\mathcal{X}),\ q_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}\},}

(295)

which is the first step of IPF. Hence subsequent steps can be obtained by
maximizing ELBOs associated with the following maximum likelihood problems
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\textstyle{q^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}\{\mathbb{E}_{p_{\textup{data}}}[\log q_{0}(X_{0})]\,:\;q\in\mathscr{P}_{d}(\mathcal{X}),\ q_{N}=p_{\textup{prior}}\},}

(296)

\displaystyle\textstyle{p^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}\{\mathbb{E}_{p_{\textup{prior}}}[\log p_{N}(X_{N})]\,:\;p\in\mathscr{P}_{d}(\mathcal{X}),\ p_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}\}}.

(297)

Appendix E
Alternative variational formulations

In this section, we draw links between IPF and score-matching techniques. We
start by proving
Section

3.3
in

Section

E.1
. We then present alternative
variational formulations in
Section

E.2
.

E.1
Proof of
Section

3.3

We only prove (
27
) since the proof (
28
) is
similar. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. For any

x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=(4\uppi\gamma_{k+1})^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p^{n}(x_{k})\exp[-\|F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})-x_{k+1}\|^{2}/(4\gamma_{k+1})]\mathrm{d}x_{k},}

(298)

with
F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})=x_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}f_{k}^{n}(x_{k})
. Since
p^{n}_{k}>0
is bounded
using the dominated convergence theorem we have for any
x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\nabla\log p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})-x_{k+1})/(2\gamma_{k+1})p_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})\mathrm{d}x_{k}.}

(299)

Therefore we get that for any
x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{b_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})-F_{k}^{n}(x_{k+1}))/\gamma_{k+1}p_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})\mathrm{d}x_{k}.}

(300)

This is equivalent to

\textstyle{B_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})={\mathbb{E}}[X_{k+1}+F_{k}^{n}(X_{k})-F_{k}^{n}(X_{k+1})|X_{k+1}=x_{k+1}]},

(301)

with
(X_{k},X_{k+1})\sim p_{k,k+1}(x_{k},x_{k+1})
. Hence, we get that

\textstyle{B^{n}_{k+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{{p^{n}_{k,k+1}}}[\|\mathrm{B}(X_{k+1})-(X_{k+1}+F^{n}_{k}(X_{k})-F^{n}_{k}(X_{k+1}))\|^{2}]},

(302)

which concludes the proof.

E.2
Variational formulas

In
Section

3.3
and
Section

3.3
we
present a variational formula for
B_{k+1}^{n}
and
F_{k}^{n+1}
for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
, where we recall that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

B_{k+1}^{n}(x)=x+\gamma_{k+1}b_{k+1}^{n}(x),\qquad F_{k}^{n+1}=x+\gamma_{k+1}f_{k}^{n+1}(x),

(303)

where we have

b_{k+1}^{n}(x)=-f_{k}^{n}(x)+2\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{n}(x),\qquad f_{k}^{n+1}(x)=-b_{k+1}^{n}(x)+2\nabla\log q_{k}^{n}(x).

(304)

In the rest of this section we assume that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle{q_{k|k+1}^{n}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})=(4\uppi\gamma_{k+1})^{-d/2}\exp[-\|x_{k}-B_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})\|^{2}/(4\gamma_{k+1})]},

(305)

\displaystyle\textstyle{p_{k+1|k}^{n+1}(x_{k+1}|x_{k})=(4\uppi\gamma_{k+1})^{-d/2}\exp[-\|x_{k+1}-F_{k}^{n+1}(x_{k})\|^{2}/(4\gamma_{k+1})]}.

(306)

We recall that in this case
Section

3.3
ensures that
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\displaystyle\textstyle{B^{n}_{k+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{{p^{n}_{k,k+1}}}[\|\mathrm{B}(X_{k+1})-(X_{k+1}+F^{n}_{k}(X_{k})-F^{n}_{k}(X_{k+1}))\|^{2}]},

(307)

\displaystyle\textstyle{F^{n+1}_{k}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\mathrm{F}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{q^{n}_{k,k+1}}[\|\mathrm{F}(X_{k})-(X_{k}+B^{n}_{k+1}(X_{k+1})-B^{n}_{k+1}(X_{k}))\|^{2}]}.

(308)

In the rest of this section we derive other variational formulas and
discuss their practical limitations/advantages.

E.2.1
Score-matching formula and sum of networks

First, using (
304
) we have for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle b_{k+1}^{n}(x)=\textstyle{\alpha x+2\sum_{j=0}^{n}\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{j}(x)-2\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\nabla\log q_{k}^{j}(x),}

(309)

\displaystyle f_{k}^{n}(x)=\textstyle{-\alpha x+2\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\nabla\log q_{k}^{j}(x)-2\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{j}(x).}

(310)

In the following proposition we derive a variational formula for

\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{n}
and
\nabla\log q_{k}^{n}(x)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
.

Proposition 28
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle{\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{n}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{{p^{n}_{k,k+1}}}[\|u(X_{k+1})-(F_{k}^{n}(X_{k})-X_{k+1})/(2\gamma_{k+1})\|^{2}]},

(311)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\nabla\log q_{k}^{n}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{v\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{q^{n}_{k,k+1}}[\|v(X_{k})-(B_{k+1}^{n}(X_{k+1})-X_{k})/(2\gamma_{k+1})\|^{2}]}.

(312)

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of
Section

3.3
but is
provided for completeness.
We only prove (
318
) since the proof (
319
) is
similar. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. For any

x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=(4\uppi\gamma_{k+1})^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p^{n}(x_{k})\exp[-\|F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})-x_{k+1}\|^{2}/(4\gamma_{k+1})]\mathrm{d}x_{k},}

(313)

with
F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})=x_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}f_{k}^{n}(x_{k})
. Since
p^{n}_{k}>0
is bounded
using the dominated convergence theorem we have for any
x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\nabla\log p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})-x_{k+1})/(2\gamma_{k+1})p_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})\mathrm{d}x_{k}.}

(314)

This is equivalent to

\textstyle{\nabla\log p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})={\mathbb{E}}[(F_{k}^{n}(X_{k})-X_{k+1})/(2\gamma_{k+1})|X_{k+1}=x_{k+1}]},

(315)

with
(X_{k},X_{k+1})\sim p_{k,k+1}(x_{k},x_{k+1})
. Hence, we get that

\textstyle{\nabla\log p^{n}_{k+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{{p^{n}_{k,k+1}}}[\|u(X_{k+1})-(F^{n}_{k}(X_{k})-X_{k+1})/(2\gamma_{k+1})\|^{2}]},

(316)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Note that (
318
) and (
319
) can be
simplified upon remarking that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\textstyle{X_{k+1}^{n}=F_{k}^{n}(X_{k}^{n})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}^{n},\quad\tilde{X}_{k}^{n}=F_{k}^{n}(\tilde{X}_{k+1}^{n})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}\tilde{Z}_{k+1}^{n},}

(317)

with
\{X_{k}^{n}\}_{k=0}^{N}\sim p^{n}
,
\{\tilde{X}_{k}^{n}\}_{k=0}^{N}\sim q^{n}
and

\{(Z_{k+1}^{n},\tilde{Z}_{k+1}^{n})\,:\;n\in\mathbb{N},\ k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}\}
a family of independent Gaussian random variables with zero mean an
identity covariance matrix. Using this result we get that
for
any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{n}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{{p^{n}_{k,k+1}}}[\|u(X_{k+1})-Z_{k+1}^{n}/\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}\|^{2}]},

(318)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\nabla\log q_{k}^{n}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{v\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{q^{n}_{k,k+1}}[\|v(X_{k})-\tilde{Z}_{k+1}^{n}/\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}\|^{2}]}.

(319)

In practice, neural networks
u_{\alpha^{n}}(k,x)\approx\nabla\log p_{k}^{n}(x)
,
and
v_{\beta^{n}}(k,x)\approx\nabla\log q_{k}^{n}(x)
are used. Hence, we sample
approximately from
q^{n}
and
p^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
using the following
recursion:

\displaystyle\textstyle{\tilde{X}_{k}^{n}=\tilde{\tau}_{k+1}\tilde{X}_{k+1}^{n}+2\gamma_{k+1}\{\sum_{j=0}^{n}u_{\alpha^{j}}(k+1,\tilde{X}_{k+1}^{n})-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}v_{\beta^{j}}(k,\tilde{X}_{k+1}^{n})\}+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}\tilde{Z}_{k+1}^{n},}

(320)

\displaystyle\textstyle{X_{k+1}^{n}=\tau_{k+1}X_{k}^{n}+2\gamma_{k+1}\{\sum_{j=0}^{n}u_{\alpha^{j}}(k+1,X_{k}^{n})-\sum_{j=0}^{n}v_{\beta^{j}}(k,X_{k}^{n})\}+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}^{n},}

(321)

where
\tilde{\tau}_{k+1}=1+\alpha\gamma_{k+1}
,
\tau_{k+1}=1-\alpha\gamma_{k+1}
and
X_{0}^{n}\sim p_{\textup{data}}
,
\tilde{X}_{N}^{n}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
.

E.2.2
Drift-matching formula

In
Section

3.3
we have given a variational formula for

B_{k+1}^{n}
and
F_{k}^{n+1}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. In
Section

E.2.1
we have
given a variational formula for
\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{n}
and
\nabla\log q_{k}^{n}

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. In the following
proposition we give a variational formula for the drifts
b_{k+1}^{n}
and
f_{k}^{n+1}
.

Proposition 29
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle{b^{n}_{k+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{b\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{{p^{n}_{k,k+1}}}[\|b(X_{k+1})-(F^{n}_{k}(X_{k})-F^{n}_{k}(X_{k+1}))/\gamma_{k+1}\|^{2}]}

(322)

\displaystyle\textstyle{f^{n+1}_{k}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{q^{n}_{k,k+1}}[\|f(X_{k})-(B^{n}_{k+1}(X_{k+1})-B^{n}_{k+1}(X_{k}))/\gamma_{k+1}\|^{2}]}

(323)

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of
Section

3.3
but is
provided for completeness.
We only prove (
322
) since the proof (
323
) is
similar. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. For any

x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\textstyle{p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=(4\uppi\gamma_{k+1})^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p^{n}(x_{k})\exp[-\|F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})-x_{k+1}\|^{2}/(4\gamma_{k+1})]\mathrm{d}x_{k},}

(324)

with
F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})=x_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}f_{k}^{n}(x_{k})
. Since
p^{n}_{k}>0
is bounded
using the dominated convergence theorem we have for any
x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\nabla\log p^{n}_{k+1}(x_{k+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})-x_{k+1})/(2\gamma_{k+1})p_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})\mathrm{d}x_{k}.}

(325)

Therefore we get that for any
x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{b_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F_{k}^{n}(x_{k})-F_{k}^{n}(x_{k+1}))/\gamma_{k+1}p_{k|k+1}(x_{k}|x_{k+1})\mathrm{d}x_{k}.}

(326)

This is equivalent to

\textstyle{b_{k+1}^{n}(x_{k+1})={\mathbb{E}}[(F_{k}^{n}(X_{k})-F_{k}^{n}(X_{k+1}))/\gamma_{k+1}|X_{k+1}=x_{k+1}]},

(327)

with
(X_{k},X_{k+1})\sim p(x_{k},x_{k+1})
. Hence, we get that

\textstyle{b^{n}_{k+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{b\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathbb{E}_{{p^{n}_{k,k+1}}}[\|b(X_{k+1})-(F^{n}_{k}(X_{k})-F^{n}_{k}(X_{k+1}))/\gamma_{k+1}\|^{2}]},

(328)

which concludes the proof.
∎

In practice, neural networks

b_{\beta^{n}}(k,x)\approx b^{n}_{k}(x)
, and

f_{\alpha^{n}}(k,x)\approx f^{n}_{k}(x)
are used.
Hence, we sample
approximately from
q^{n}
and
p^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
using the following
recursion:

\displaystyle\textstyle{\tilde{X}_{k}^{n}=\tilde{X}_{k+1}^{n}+\gamma_{k+1}b_{\beta^{n}}(k+1,\tilde{X}_{k+1}^{n})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}\tilde{Z}_{k+1}^{n},}

(329)

\displaystyle\textstyle{X_{k+1}^{n}=X_{k}^{n}+\gamma_{k+1}f_{\alpha^{n}}(k,X_{k}^{n})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z_{k+1}^{n},}

(330)

with
X_{0}^{n}\sim p_{\textup{data}}
,
\tilde{X}_{N}^{n}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
.

E.2.3
Discussion

We identify three variational formulas associated with

Section

3.3
,
Section

E.2.1

and
Section

E.2.2
. In practice we discard the
approach of
Section

E.2.1
because it requires storing
2n

neural networks to sample from
p^{n}
, see (
321
). Hence
the algorithm requires more memory as
n
increases and the sampling procedure
requires
\mathcal{O}(nN)
passes through a neural network. The approaches described
in
Section

3.3
and

Section

E.2.2
yield sampling procedures which only
require
\mathcal{O}(N)
passes through a neural network and have fixed memory cost
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In practice we observed that the approach of

Section

3.3
yields better results. We conjecture that
this favorable behavior is mainly due to the architecture of the neural networks
used to approximate
B_{k+1}^{n}
and
F_{k}^{n+1}
which have residual
connections and therefore are better suited at representing functions of the

x\mapsto x+\Phi(x)
where
\Phi
is a perturbation.

Appendix F
Theoretical study of Schrödinger bridges and the IPF

In this section, we explore some of the theoretical properties of Schrödinger bridges
and the IPF procedure.
Section

3.4
and
Section

3.4

are proved in
Section

F.1
and
Section

F.2

respectively.

F.1
Proof of
Section

3.4

In this section, we prove
Section

3.4
. First we gather novel
monotonicity results for the IPF in
Section

F.1.1
, see

Section

F.1.1
. Then we prove our quantitative
convergence bounds in
Theorem

36
, see

Section

F.1.2
.

F.1.1
Monotonicity results

We consider the static IPF recursion:
\pi^{0}=\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
and

\displaystyle\pi^{2n+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{2},\ \pi_{1}=\nu_{1}\right\},

(331)

\displaystyle\pi^{2n+2}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n+1})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{2},\ \pi_{0}=\nu_{0}\right\},

(332)

where
\nu_{0},\nu_{1}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We also consider the following assumption.

B 1
.

\mu
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}
and

\operatorname{KL}(\nu_{0}\otimes\nu_{1}|\mu)<+\infty
. In addition,
\nu_{i}
and

\mu_{i}
are equivalent for
i\in\{0,1\}
.
