First we draw links between
1
and
1
.

Proposition 30
.

1
implies
1
with
\mu=p_{0,N}
.

Proof.

Since
p_{N}>0
we get that
p_{N}
and
p_{\textup{prior}}
are equivalent. Hence
\mu_{1}

and
\nu_{1}
are equivalent and
\mu_{0}=\nu_{0}
. Let us show that
\mu
is
absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}
,
i.e.
that
p_{0,N}
is
absolutely continuous w.r.t.
p_{\textup{data}}\otimes p_{N}
. Since
p_{N}>0
we get
that
p_{0,N}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
p_{\textup{data}}\otimes p_{N}
with
density
p_{N|0}/p_{N}
. Finally we have

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\log(p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})/(p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})p_{N|0}(x_{N}|x_{0})))p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\mathrm{d}x_{0}\mathrm{d}x_{N}}

(333)

\displaystyle\qquad=\textstyle{\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\log(p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})/p_{N|0}(x_{N}|x_{0}))p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\mathrm{d}x_{0}\mathrm{d}x_{N}}

(334)

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle{\leq|\mathrm{H}(p_{\textup{prior}})|+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\log p_{N|0}(x_{N}|x_{0})|p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\mathrm{d}x_{0}\mathrm{d}x_{N}<+\infty}

(335)

which concludes the proof.
∎

In this section we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 31
.

Assume
1
. Then, the IPF sequence is well-defined and for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1
we have

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n+1}|\pi^{n})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n-1}|\pi^{n}),\qquad\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}|\pi^{n+1})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}|\pi^{n-1}).

(336)

In addition, the following results hold:

(a)

(\|\pi^{n+1}-\pi^{n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\operatorname{J}(\pi^{n+1},\pi^{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are non-increasing.

(b)

(\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+2}|\pi^{2n+1}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are non-increasing.

(c)

(\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}_{1}|\nu_{1}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}_{0}|\nu_{0}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are non-increasing.

(d)

(\|\pi^{2n+1}_{1}-\nu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\|\pi^{2n}_{0}-\nu_{0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are non-increasing.

First, we show that under
1
, the IPF sequence is
well-defined and is associated with a sequence of potentials.

Proposition 32
.

Assume
1
. Then, the IPF sequence is well-defined and
there exist
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{n},b_{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left(0,+\infty\right)
and for any

x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+1}/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}))(x,y)=a_{n}(x)h(x,y)b_{n}(y)}

(337)

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+2}/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}))(x,y)=a_{n+1}(x)h(x,y)b_{n}(y),}

(338)

and

\displaystyle\textstyle{v_{0}(x)=a_{n+1}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)b_{n}(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y),\quad v_{1}(y)=b_{n}(y)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)a_{n}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x),}

(339)

where
v_{i}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{i}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

First, we show that the IPF sequence is well-defined. Note that
\pi^{1}
is
well-defined since
\operatorname{KL}(\nu_{0}\otimes\nu_{1}|\mu)<+\infty
. Assume that

\{\pi^{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{n}
is well-defined. Using
(
Csiszár, 1975
, Theorem
2.2)
we have

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\nu_{0}\otimes\nu_{1}|\mu)=\operatorname{KL}(\nu_{0}\otimes\nu_{1}|\pi^{n})+\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\ell+1}|\pi^{\ell}).}

(340)

In particular,
\operatorname{KL}(\nu_{0}\otimes\nu_{1}|\pi^{n})<+\infty
and

\pi^{n+1}
is well-defined. We conclude by recursion.

Using
(
Csiszár, 1975
, Theorem 3.1)
and
1
,
there exists
(\tilde{b}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\tilde{b}_{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
and for any
x,y\in\mathsf{A}_{n}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+1}/\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n})(x,y)=\tilde{b}_{n}(y)
with

\mathsf{A}_{n}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\tilde{\pi}(\mathsf{A}_{n})=0
for any
\tilde{\pi}

such that
\tilde{\pi}_{1}=\nu_{1}
and
\operatorname{KL}(\tilde{\pi}|\pi^{2n})<+\infty
. In
particular we have
(\nu_{0}\otimes\nu_{1})(\mathsf{A}_{n})=0
. Since
\nu_{i}
is
equivalent to
\mu_{i}
for any
i\in\{0,1\}
we have

(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1})(\mathsf{A}_{n})=0
. Similarly, there exists

(\tilde{a}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\tilde{a}_{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
and for any
x,y\in\mathsf{B}_{n}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+2}/\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+1})(x,y)=\tilde{a}_{n+1}(x)
with

\mathsf{B}_{n}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1})(\mathsf{B}_{n})=0
. As a
result, there exist
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with

a_{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
and

b_{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+1}/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}))(x,y)=a_{n}(x)h(x,y)b_{n}(y)}

(341)

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+2}/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}))(x,y)=a_{n+1}(x)h(x,y)b_{n}(y),}

(342)

where
h=\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1})
and
a_{0}=1
.
In addition, setting
b_{-1}=1
, we have for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{0}/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}))(x,y)=a_{0}(x)h(x,y)b_{-1}(y).

(343)

Using
that
\nu_{i}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\mu_{i}

for
i\in\{0,1\}
with density
v_{i}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left(0,+\infty\right)
we get
that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\textstyle{v_{0}(x)=a_{n+1}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)b_{n}(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y),\quad v_{1}(y)=b_{n}(y)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)a_{n}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x).}

(344)

Since
v_{0},v_{1}>0
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{n},b_{n}>0
.
∎

Note that the system of equations (
339
) corresponds to
iteratively solving the Schrödinger system, see
Léonard, 2014b
for a
survey. In addition, (
339
) has connections with Fortet’s
mapping

(
Léonard, 2019
;
Fortet, 1940
)
.

In the rest of the section we detail the proof of
Section

3.4
. We
start by deriving identities between the marginals of the IPF and its joint
distribution both w.r.t. the Kullback-Leibler divergence and the total
variation norm in
Section

F.1.1
. Second, we establish
that
(\|\pi^{n+1}-\pi^{n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-increasing in

Section

F.1.1
. Then, we prove (
336
) in

Section

F.1.1
. We conclude with the proof of

Section

F.1.1
.

Lemma 33
.

Assume
1
.
Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\|\pi^{2n+1}-\pi^{2n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\|\pi^{2n}_{1}-\nu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}},\qquad\|\pi^{2n+2}-\pi^{2n+1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\|\pi^{2n+1}_{0}-\nu_{0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

(345)

In addition, we have

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}|\pi^{2n+1})=\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}_{1}|\nu_{1}),\qquad\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n+2})=\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}_{0}|\nu_{0}).

(346)

Proof.

We divide the proof into two parts. First, we prove (
345
). Second,
we show that (
346
) holds.

(a)

We only show that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\|\pi^{2n+1}-\pi^{2n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\|\pi^{2n}_{1}-\nu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
. The proof that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\|\pi^{2n+2}-\pi^{2n+1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\|\pi^{2n+1}_{0}-\nu_{0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
is similar. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Using (
337
) and
(
339
) we have

\displaystyle\|\pi^{2n+1}-\pi^{2n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\left|b_{n}(y)-b_{n-1}(y)\right|a_{n}(x)h(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y)}

(347)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|1-b_{n-1}(x)/b_{n}(x)\right|\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}(y).}

(348)

In addition, we have that for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\textstyle{\pi^{2n}_{1}(\mathsf{A})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{A}}a_{n}(x)b_{n-1}(y)h(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y)=\int_{\mathsf{A}}(b_{n-1}/b_{n})(y)\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}(y).}

(349)

We get that for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi_{1}^{2n}/\mathrm{d}\nu_{1})(y)=(b_{n-1}/b_{n})(y)
. Hence, using
(
347
) we get that

\textstyle{\|\pi^{2n}_{1}-\nu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|1-a_{n}(x)/a_{n+1}(x)\right|\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}(x)=\|\pi^{2n+1}-\pi^{2n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.}

(350)

(b)

We only show that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}|\pi^{2n+1})=\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}_{1}|\nu_{1})
. The proof that for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n+2})=\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}_{0}|\nu_{0})
is
similar. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Using that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n}_{1}/\mathrm{d}\nu_{1})(y)=b_{n-1}(y)/b_{n}(y)
and that

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+1}/\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n})(x,y)=b_{n}(y)/b_{n-1}(y)
we have

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}|\pi^{2n+1})=-\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log(b_{n}(y)/b_{n-1}(y))\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n}_{1}(y)}=\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}_{1}|\nu_{1}).

(351)

This concludes the proof.

∎

Lemma 34
.

Assume
1
. Then
(\|\pi^{n+1}-\pi^{n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
non-increasing.

Proof.

We only prove that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1
,

\|\pi^{2n+1}-\pi^{2n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\|\pi^{2n}-\pi^{2n-1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
. The
proof that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\|\pi^{2n+2}-\pi^{2n+1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\|\pi^{2n+1}-\pi^{2n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
is
similar. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1
.
Similarly to the proof of
Section

F.1.1
we have that

\textstyle{\|\pi^{2n+1}-\pi^{2n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|1-b_{n-1}(y)/b_{n}(y)\right|\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|b_{n}^{-1}(y)-b_{n-1}^{-1}(y)\right|b_{n-1}(y)\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}(y).}

(352)

In addition, we have that for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\left|b_{n-1}^{-1}(y)-b_{n}^{-1}(y)\right|\leq v_{1}^{-1}(y)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)\left|a_{n-1}(x)-a_{n}(x)\right|\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x).}

(353)

Combining this result and (
352
) we get that

\displaystyle\|\pi^{2n+1}-\pi^{2n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|b_{n-1}^{-1}(y)-b_{n}^{-1}(y)\right|b_{n-1}(y)\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}(y)}

(354)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\left|a_{n}(x)-a_{n-1}(x)\right|h(x,y)b_{n-1}(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y)}

(355)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|1-a_{n-1}(x)/a_{n}(x)\right|\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}(x)\leq\|\pi^{2n}-\pi^{2n-1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}},

(356)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 35
.

Assume
1
. Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1
we have

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n+1}|\pi^{n})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n-1}|\pi^{n}),\qquad\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}|\pi^{n+1})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}|\pi^{n-1}).

(357)

Proof.

Using
Section

F.1.1
and the data
processing theorem
(
Ambrosio et al., 2008
, Lemma 9.4.5)
we get that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}|\pi^{2n+1})=\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}_{1}|\nu_{1})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}|\pi^{2n+1}).

(358)

Similarly, we get that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n+2})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n})
. Hence, we get that
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}|\pi^{n+1})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n}|\pi^{n-1})
.

In addition, using that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1
and

x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that
\pi^{2n+1}_{1}=\nu_{1}
and

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+1}/\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n})(x,y)=b_{n}(y)/b_{n-1}(y)
we get for
any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log(b_{n-1}(y)/b_{n}(y))\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}(y).}

(359)

Using Jensen’s inequality we have for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\textstyle{-\log(b_{n-1}(y)/b_{n}(y))\leq-\log\left(\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)a_{n}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)a_{n-1}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\right.\right)}

(360)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\textstyle{-\log\left(\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(a_{n}(x)/a_{n-1}(x))h(x,y)a_{n-1}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(y)\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)a_{n-1}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\right.\right)}

(361)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\textstyle{-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log(a_{n}(x)/a_{n-1}(x))b_{n-1}(y)h(x,y)a_{n-1}(x)/v_{1}(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x).}

(362)

Combining this result, (
359
), Fubini’s theorem and that for
any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n-1}_{0}/\mathrm{d}\nu_{0})(x)=a_{n-1}(x)/a_{n}(x)
we get that for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n})

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\log(a_{n-1}(x)/a_{n}(x))a_{n-1}(x)h(x,y)b_{n-1}(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)}

(363)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\log(a_{n-1}(x)/a_{n}(x))(a_{n-1}(x)/a_{n}(x))\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}(x)\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n-1}_{0}|\nu_{0}).}

(364)

Using
Section

F.1.1
(or the data processing theorem)
we get that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 1
,

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n-1}|\pi^{2n})
. Similarly, we get
that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+2}|\pi^{2n+1})\leq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}|\pi^{2n+1})
, which concludes the
proof.
∎

We now turn to the proof of
Section

F.1.1

Proof.

First, (
336
) is a direct consequence of

Section

F.1.1
. Using
Section

F.1.1
we get that

(\|\pi^{n+1}-\pi^{n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-increasing. Since for
any
\eta_{0},\eta_{1}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\operatorname{J}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})=(1/2)\{\operatorname{KL}(\eta_{0}|\eta_{1})+\operatorname{KL}(\eta_{0}|\eta_{1})\}
and using (
336
), we get that

(\operatorname{J}(\pi_{n+1},\pi_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-increasing which proves

Section

F.1.1
-
(a)
.
Section

F.1.1
-
(b)

is a straightforward consequence of (
336
).

Section

F.1.1
-
(c)
is a consequence of

Section

F.1.1
and

Section

F.1.1
-
(a)
. Finally,

Section

F.1.1
-
(c)
is a consequence of

Section

F.1.1
and (
336
).
∎

Note that we also have that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n}|\pi^{2n+1}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n+2}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are non-increasing.

F.1.2
Quantitative convergence bounds

In this section we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 36
.

Assume
1
. Then, the IPF sequence
(\pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is well-defined and there
exists a probability measure
\pi^{\infty}
such that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|\pi^{n}-\pi^{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=0
and the following
hold:

(a)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}n^{1/2}\left\{\|\pi_{0}^{n}-\nu_{0}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}+\|\pi_{1}^{n}-\nu_{1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\right\}=0.

(b)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}n\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{0}^{n}|\nu_{0})+\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{1}^{n}|\nu_{1})\right\}=0.

We begin with
Section

F.1.2
which is an adaption of
(
Ruschendorf et al., 1995
, Proposition
2.1)
. Then we state and prove
Section

F.1.2

which is a classical lemma from real analysis. Combining these two lemmas and
the monotonicity results from
Section

F.1.1
conclude the
proof.

Lemma 37
.

Assume
1
.
Then,
(\pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is well-defined and
we have
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{n+1}|\pi^{n})<+\infty
.

Proof.

The sequence is well-defined using
Section

F.1.1
.
In addition, using
(
Csiszár, 1975
, Theorem 2.2)
we have for any

n\in\mathbb{N}

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\mu^{\star}|\pi^{0})=\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\pi^{n})+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{k+1}|\pi^{k}),}

(365)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 38
.

Let
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\left[0,+\infty\right)^{\mathbb{N}}
a non-increasing
sequence such that
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}<+\infty
. Then

\lim_{n\to+\infty}c_{n}n=0
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
n\geq n_{0}
,

\sum_{k=n}^{+\infty}c_{k}\leq\varepsilon
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
with

n\geq 2n_{0}
. Note that
n-n_{0}\geq n/2\geq n_{0}
. Therefore we have

\varepsilon\geq(n-n_{0})c_{n}\geq(n/2)c_{n}
. Hence, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with

n\geq 2n_{0}
,
c_{n}n\leq 2\varepsilon
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We now conclude with the proof of
Theorem

36
.

Proof.

Using
Section

F.1.2
and Pinsker’s inequality
(
Bakry et al., 2014
, Equation
5.2.2)
we have

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|\pi^{n+1}-\pi^{n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}<+\infty
. For any

N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
S_{N}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}\pi^{n+1}-\pi^{n}=\pi^{N+1}-\mu
. Since the space of finite signed measures endowed with

\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
is a Banach space
(
Douc et al., 2019
, Theorem
D.2.7)
we have that
(S_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges. Hence there exists a finite signed measure
\pi^{\infty}
such that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|\pi^{n}-\pi^{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=0
.
\pi^{\infty}
is
a probability measure since for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\pi^{n}
is a probability
measure.

In addition, since
(\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+1}|\pi^{2n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{2n+2}|\pi^{2n+1}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are non-increasing by

Section

F.1.1
, using
Section

F.1.2
, we get that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}n\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{0}^{n}|\nu_{0})+\operatorname{KL}(\pi_{1}^{n}|\nu_{1})\right\}=0.

(366)

We conclude upon using Pinsker’s inequality
(
Bakry et al., 2014
, Equation
5.2.2)
.
∎

F.2
Proof of
Section

3.4

Similarly to
Section

F.1
, we consider the static IPF recursion:
\pi^{0}=\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
and

\displaystyle\pi^{2n+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{2},\ \pi_{1}=\nu_{1}\right\},

(367)

\displaystyle\pi^{2n+2}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{2n+1})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{2},\ \pi_{0}=\nu_{0}\right\},

(368)

where
\nu_{0},\nu_{1}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We recall that in this context that if the Schrödinger bridge
\pi^{\star}
exists it is given by

\pi^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{2},\ \pi_{0}=\nu_{0},\ \pi_{1}=\nu_{1}\}.

(369)

In this section, we prove the following proposition which directly implies

Section

3.4
.

Proposition 39
.

Assume
1
and denote

h=\mathrm{d}\mu/(\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1})
. Assume that

h\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right])
and that there exist

\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\left(0,+\infty\right))
such that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle h(x,y)\leq\Phi_{0}(x)\Phi_{1}(y),\text{~and}

(370)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\left|\log h(x_{0},x_{1})\right|+\left|\log\Phi_{0}(x_{0})\right|+\left|\log\Phi_{1}(x_{1})\right|)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1})<+\infty.}

(371)

Then there exists a solution

\pi^{\star}
to the Schrödinger bridge and the IPF sequence satisfies

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|\pi^{n}-\pi^{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=0
with

\pi^{\infty}\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
. If
\mu
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.

\pi^{\infty}
then
\pi^{\infty}=\pi^{\star}
.

We begin with an adaptation of
(
Rüschendorf and Thomsen, 1993
, Proposition 2)
.

Proposition 40
.

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
and assume that
\mu
is absolutely continuous
w.r.t.
\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}
. Let
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

a_{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left(0,+\infty\right)
and
b_{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left(0,+\infty\right)
. Assume that there exists

\Phi:\ (\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
and
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

\mu(\mathsf{A})=1
such that for any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}

\lim_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}(x)b_{n}(y)=\Phi(x,y).

(372)

Then, there exist
a:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
,

b:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
and
\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

\mu(\mathsf{B})=1
such that for any
x,y\in\mathsf{B}

\Phi(x,y)=a(x)b(y),\qquad\text{or }\quad\Phi(x,y)=0.

(373)

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}=\{(x,y)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\,:\;\Phi(x,y)=0\}
and

\mathsf{A}_{a}=\tilde{\mathsf{A}}\cap\mathsf{A}
and

\mathsf{A}_{b}=\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathrm{c}}\cap\mathsf{A}
. If
\mathsf{A}_{b}=\emptyset
, we
conclude the proof. Otherwise, let
(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathsf{A}_{b}
. Let

\mathsf{C}_{0},\mathsf{C}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{0}^{0}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\lim_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}^{0}(x)=a^{0}(x)\ \text{exists and $a^{0}(x)>0$}\},

(374)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}^{0}

\displaystyle=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\lim_{n\to+\infty}b_{n}^{0}(y)=b^{0}(y)\ \text{exists and $b^{0}(y)>0$}\},

(375)

where for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
a_{n}^{0}(x)=a_{n}(x)/a_{n}(x_{0})

and
b_{n}^{0}(y)=b_{n}(y)a_{n}(x_{0})
, which is well-defined since for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{n}(x_{0})>0
. Note that
x_{0}\in\mathsf{C}_{0}^{0}
and that
y_{0}\in\mathsf{C}_{1}^{0}
. If

\mathsf{A}_{b}\subset\mathsf{C}_{0}^{0}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{0}
, we conclude the proof. Otherwise,
let
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathsf{A}_{b}\cap(\mathsf{C}_{0}^{0}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{0})^{\mathrm{c}}
and define

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\lim_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}^{1}(x)=a^{1}(x)\ \text{exists and $a^{1}(x)>0$}\},

(376)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}^{1}

\displaystyle=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\lim_{n\to+\infty}b_{n}^{1}(y)=b^{1}(y)\ \text{exists and $b^{1}(y)>0$}\},

(377)

where for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
a_{n}^{1}(x)=a_{n}(x)/a_{n}(x_{1})

and
b_{n}^{1}(y)=b_{n}(y)a_{n}(x_{1})
, which is well-defined since for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{n}(x_{1})>0
. Note that
\mathsf{C}_{0}^{0}\cap\mathsf{C}_{0}^{1}=\emptyset

and
\mathsf{C}_{1}^{0}\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}^{1}=\emptyset
. Indeed, if there exists

x\in\mathsf{C}_{0}^{0}\cap\mathsf{C}_{0}^{1}
, then

a^{0}(x)=\lim_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}(x)/a_{n}(x_{0})>0
and

a^{1}(x)=\lim_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}(x)/a_{n}(x_{1})>0
exists. Therefore

\lim_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}(x_{1})/a_{n}(x_{0})>0
exists and

\lim_{n\to+\infty}b_{n}(y_{1})a_{n}(x_{0})>0
exists. Hence

(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathsf{C}_{0}^{0}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{0}
which is absurd. Similarly, if there
exists
y\in\mathsf{C}_{1}^{0}\cap\mathsf{C}_{1}^{1}
then

(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathsf{C}_{0}^{0}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{0}
which is absurd. Hence, we consider

T:\mathsf{A}_{b}\to 2^{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}
such that for any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{b}
,

T(x,y)=\mathsf{C}_{0}^{(x,y)}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{(x,y)}
, where

\mathsf{C}_{0}^{(x,y)}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{(x,y)}
is constructed as in
(
374
) replacing
(x_{0},y_{0})
by
(x,y)
.

Consider a well order on
(\mathsf{A}_{b},\leq)
, which is possible by the
well-ordering principle
(
Enderton, 1977
, p. 196)
. For any

(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

\mathsf{A}_{b}^{(x,y)}=\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\,:\;(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})<(x,y)\}
. Using
the transfinite recursion theorem
(
Enderton, 1977
, p. 175)
there
exists
f:\ \mathsf{A}_{b}\to\{0,1\}
such that for any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{b}
if
there exists
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
such that
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})<(x,y)
,
f(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=1

and
(x,y)\in T(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
then
f(x,y)=0
and
f(x,y)=1
otherwise. Let

I=f^{-1}(\{1\})
. Let
(x,y),(x^{\prime},y)\in I
with
(x,y)\neq(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
then
for
(x,y)<(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
for instance. Since
f(x,y)=f(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=1
we have that

(\mathsf{C}_{0}^{(x,y)}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{(x,y)})\cap(\mathsf{C}_{0}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})})=\emptyset
. Let
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{b}
. If
f(x,y)=1
then

(x,y)\in\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)}_{0}\times\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)}_{1}
. If
f(x,y)=0
then there
exists
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})<(x,y)
such that

(x,y)\in\mathsf{C}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}_{0}\times\mathsf{C}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}_{1}
. Therefore, we get that

\{\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)}=(\mathsf{C}_{0}^{(x,y)}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{(x,y)})\cap\mathsf{A}_{b}\,:\;(x,y)\in I\}
is a partition of
\mathsf{A}_{b}
.

Since
\mu(\mathsf{A}_{b})\leq 1
, and

\{\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)}=(\mathsf{C}_{0}^{(x,y)}\times\mathsf{C}_{1}^{(x,y)})\cap\mathsf{A}_{b}\,:\;(x,y)\in I\}
is a partition of
\mathsf{A}_{b}
, we get that

J=\{\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)}\,:\;(x,y)\in I,\mu_{0}(\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)}_{0})\mu_{1}(\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)}_{1})>0\}
is countable. Denote

\mathsf{A}_{c}=\cup_{(x,y)\in J}\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)}
. Let us show that

\mu(\mathsf{A}_{c}^{\mathrm{c}}\cap\mathsf{A}_{b})=\mu(\cup_{(x,y)\in I\cap J^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathsf{C}^{(x,y)})=0
. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and define

\mathsf{D}_{x}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{b}\cap\mathsf{A}_{c}^{\mathrm{c}}\}
. If
\mathsf{D}_{x}
is not empty, then there exists

(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in I
such that
x\in\mathsf{C}_{0}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}
. Then, for any

y\in\mathsf{D}_{x}
,
y\in\mathsf{C}_{1}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}
. Hence,

(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in I\cap J^{\mathrm{c}}
by definition of
\mathsf{D}_{x}
and

\mu_{1}(\mathsf{D}_{x})=0
. We get that

\textstyle{\mu(\mathsf{A}_{b}\cap\mathsf{A}_{c}^{\mathrm{c}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathsf{D}_{x}}h(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y)\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)=0,}

(378)

where
h
is the density of
\mu
w.r.t.
\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}
. Note that this
is the only instance in the proof, where we use that
\mu
is absolutely
continuous w.r.t.
\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}
. For any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{c}
define for
any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\textstyle{\hat{a}_{n}(x)=\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in J}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{C}_{0}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}}(x)a_{n}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}(x),\quad\hat{b}_{n}(y)=\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in J}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}}(x)b_{n}^{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}(y).}

(379)

There exist
\hat{a},\hat{b}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left(0,+\infty\right)
such that for
any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{c}
,
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\hat{a}_{n}(x)=\hat{a}(x)
and

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\hat{b}_{n}(y)=\hat{b}(y)
. In addition, for any

(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{c}
,
a_{n}(x)b_{n}(y)=\hat{a}_{n}(x)\hat{b}_{n}(y)
. Hence, for
any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{c}
,
\Phi(x,y)=\hat{a}(x)\hat{b}(y)
. Since

\mathsf{A}_{a}\cap\mathsf{A}_{c}=\emptyset
and
\mu(\mathsf{A}_{c})=\mu(\mathsf{A}_{b})
, we have

\mu(\mathsf{A}_{a})+\mu(\mathsf{A}_{c})=\mu(\mathsf{A}_{a})+\mu(\mathsf{A}_{b})=\mu(\mathsf{A})=1.

(380)

We conclude the proof upon remarking that for any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{a}
,

\Phi(x,y)=0
and for any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}_{c}
,

\Phi(x)=\hat{a}(x)\hat{b}(y)
.
∎

In what follows we prove
Section

F.2
.

Proof.

Since
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|\pi^{n}-\pi^{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=0
by

Theorem

36
and
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\infty}|\mu)<+\infty
,
there exist
\mathsf{A}
with
\mu(\mathsf{A})=1
and

\Phi:\ (\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
such that, up to extraction, for any

x,y\in\mathsf{A}

\lim_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}(x)b_{n}(y)=\Phi(x,y),

(381)

and
(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\infty}/\mathrm{d}\mu)=\Phi
. Using

Section

F.2
, there exist

a,b:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
and
\mathsf{B}
with
\pi^{\infty}(\mathsf{B})=1

such that for any
x,y\in\mathsf{B}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\infty}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x,y)=a(x)b(y)
. Since
\mu
is absolutely
continuous w.r.t.
\pi^{\infty}
, we get that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\infty}/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}))(x,y)=a(x)b(y)h(x,y)
.
In addition, the
Schrödinger bridge
\pi^{\star}\in\mathscr{P}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2})
exists, see
(
Rüschendorf and Thomsen, 1993
, Theorem
3)
, and there exist

a^{\prime},b^{\prime}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
and
\mathsf{B}^{\prime}
with
\mu(\mathsf{B}^{\prime})=1

such that for any
x,y\in\mathsf{B}^{\prime}

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}))(x,y)=a^{\prime}(x)b^{\prime}(y)h(x,y).

(382)

Let
\mathscr{M}_{+,\times}
be the space of non-negative product measures over

\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let

\mathbf{\Psi}_{\bar{h}}:\ \mathscr{M}_{+,\times}\to\mathscr{M}_{+,\times}
be given for any

\lambda=\lambda_{0}\otimes\lambda_{1}\in\mathscr{M}_{+,\times}
by

\mathbf{\Psi}_{\bar{h}}(\lambda)=\Psi_{h}^{\lambda}
where for any

\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\textstyle{\Psi_{h}^{\lambda}(\mathsf{A}\times\mathsf{B})=(\int_{\mathsf{A}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{h}(x,y)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(x))(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{B}}\bar{h}(x,y)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(y))}

(383)

where for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\bar{h}(x,y)=h(x,y)\Phi_{0}^{-1}(x)\Phi_{1}^{-1}(y)
. Note that

\bar{h}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\left[0,+\infty\right))
and is
bounded. Hence, using
(
Beurling, 1960
, Theorem 2)
and
(
371
) we get that
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\bar{h}}
is a bijection.
Let
\lambda=(a\Phi_{0}\mu_{0},b\Phi_{1}\mu_{1})
and

\lambda^{\prime}=(a^{\prime}\Phi_{0}\mu_{0},b^{\prime}\Phi_{1}\mu_{1})
. Then, since

\pi^{\star}_{i}=\pi^{\infty}_{i}=\nu_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
we get that

\mathbf{\Psi}_{h}(\lambda)=\mathbf{\Psi}_{h}(\lambda^{\prime})
. Hence
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
and

\pi^{\infty}=\pi^{\star}
which concludes the proof.
∎

In
Section

F.2
we derive an alternative proposition to

Section

F.2
. We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 41
.

Let
\pi^{\star}\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
with
\pi^{\star}_{i}=\nu_{i}
for

i\in\{0,1\}
. Assume that
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\mu)<+\infty
and that

\mathrm{L}^{1}(\nu_{0})\oplus\mathrm{L}^{1}(\nu_{1})
is closed in
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\pi^{\star})
. In
addition, assume that there exist
a,b:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
and

\mathsf{A}
with
\pi^{\star}(\mathsf{A})=1
such that for any
(x,y)\in\mathsf{A}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x,y)=a(x)b(y).

(384)

Then
\pi^{\star}
is the Schrödinger bridge.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\mu)<+\infty
we have that

\textstyle{\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\left|\log(a(x)b(y))\right|\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}(x,y)<+\infty.}

(385)

Using
(
Kober, 1939
, Theorem 1)
and that
\pi^{\star}_{i}=\nu_{i}
for

i\in\{0,1\}
, we get that

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\log a(x)\right|\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\log b(y)\right|\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}(y)<+\infty.}

(386)

Let
\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
such that
\pi_{i}=\nu_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and

\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)<+\infty
. Using (
386
), we have that

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\left|\log((\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x,y))\right|\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)<+\infty
. Hence,
(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)>0
,
\pi
-almost surely.
Using this result we have for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\pi[\mathsf{A}]

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}}(x)(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\pi(x)}

(387)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}}(x)(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)^{-1}(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)}

(388)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}}(x)(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)^{-1}(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}(x)}.

(389)

Hence we get that

\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}=(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)^{-1}
.
In addition, we have that

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log(a(x))\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log(b(y))\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}(y)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\log((\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x,y))\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y).}

(390)

We get that

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{\star})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log((\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\mu)(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x,y)^{-1})\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)=\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)-\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\mu).}

(391)

Hence,
\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)\geq\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\mu)
with equality if and only if

\pi^{\star}=\pi
. Therefore,
\pi^{\star}
is the Schrödinger bridge.
∎

The following proposition is an alternative to
Section

F.2
.

Proposition 42
.

Assume
1
. Then there exists a solution
\pi^{\star}
to the
Schrödinger bridge and the IPF sequence
(\pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|\pi^{n}-\pi^{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=0
with

\pi^{\infty}\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
. If
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\infty}|\mu)<+\infty

and
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\nu_{0})\oplus\mathrm{L}^{1}(\nu_{1})
is closed in
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\pi^{\infty})

then
\pi^{\infty}=\pi^{\star}
.

Proof.

Since
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|\pi^{n}-\pi^{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=0
by

Theorem

36
and
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\infty}|\mu)<+\infty
,
there exist
\mathsf{A}
with
\mu(\mathsf{A})=1
and

\Phi:\ (\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)
such that, up to extraction, for any

x,y\in\mathsf{A}

\lim_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}(x)b_{n}(y)=\Phi(x,y),

(392)

and
(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\infty}/\mathrm{d}\mu)=\Phi
. Using

Section

F.2
, there exist
a,b:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\left[0,+\infty\right)

and
\mathsf{B}
with
\pi^{\infty}(\mathsf{B})=1
such that for any
x,y\in\mathsf{B}
,

(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\infty}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x,y)=a(x)b(y)
. We conclude upon using

Section

F.2
.
∎

Appendix G
Geometric convergence rates and convergence to ground-truth

In this section, we derive geometric convergence rates in

Section

G.1
in a Gaussian setting. In particular, we provide an
explicit upper-bound on the convergence rate that depends only on the covariance
of the reference measure and the target. In
Section

G.2
, we
show that DSB (with Brownian reference measure) converges towards the Schrödinger bridge in a Gaussian setting where the ground-truth is available. In

Section

4
we show that our implementation actually recovers the
Schrödinger bridge in this setting.

G.1
Geometric convergence rates

In the following proposition we show that we recover a geometric convergence
rate in a Gaussian setting and derive intuition from this case study. We set

N=1
and assume that for any
x_{0},x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

p(x_{0},x_{N})\propto\exp[-\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x_{0},x_{N}\rangle-\left\|x_{N}\right\|^{2}],

(393)

with
\alpha\in\left[0,1\right)
. In this case assume that there exists
\beta>0
such that the target marginals are
given for any
x_{0},x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})\propto\exp[-\beta\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2}],\qquad p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{N})\propto\exp[-\beta\left\|x_{N}\right\|^{2}].

(394)

Proposition 43
.

Let
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
and
\beta>0
. Then the Schrödinger bridge

\pi^{\star}
exists and there exists
C\geq 0
(explicit in the proof) such
that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\pi^{n})\leq C\kappa^{2n}
,
with
\kappa<1
given by
\kappa=\rho/(1+\rho)
and

\rho=2\alpha/\beta^{2}
. In addition,
\pi^{\star}
admits a density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure denoted

p^{\star}
and given for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\textstyle{p^{\star}(x,y)=\exp[-\gamma^{\star}\|x\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x,y\rangle-\gamma^{\star}\|y\|^{2}]/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\gamma^{\star}\|x\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x,y\rangle-\gamma^{\star}\|y\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,}

(395)

with
\gamma^{\star}=(\beta^{2}/2)(1+(1+4\alpha^{2}/\beta^{2})^{1/2})
.

Remark that if
\beta^{2}=1-\alpha^{2}
then
\gamma^{\star}
and
p^{\star}=p
,
i.e.
the IPF leaves
\mu
invariant. Note that the performance of the IPF improves
if
\kappa
is close to
0
,
i.e.
if
\rho=2\alpha/\beta^{2}
is close to

0
. This is the case if
\alpha\approx 0
(the marginals are almost
independent) or if
\beta\approx+\infty
(the target distribution is close to

\updelta_{0}
), see
Figure

8
. This behavior is in accordance with the limit case where the
marginals are independent or one of the target distribution is a Dirac mass in
which case the IPF converges in two iterations.

Figure 8:
Evolution of
\kappa^{2}
depending on
\alpha
and
\beta
.

Also, note that the convergence rate does not depend on the dimension but only
on the constants of the problem. In what follows we first derive the IPF
sequence for this Gaussian problem and establish that
\alpha
controls the
amount of information shared by the marginals. Then we prove

Section

G.1
. In the rest of this section, we let

\mu\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
with density
p
w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure such that for any

x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{p(x_{0},x_{1})=\exp[-\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x_{0},x_{1}\rangle-\left\|x_{1}\right\|^{2}]/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x_{0},x_{1}\rangle-\left\|x_{1}\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}x_{0}\mathrm{d}x_{1}.}

(396)

We have that
\mu
is the Gaussian distribution with zero mean and covariance matrix
\Sigma
such that

\textstyle{\Sigma=(2(1-\alpha^{2}))^{-1}\left(\begin{matrix}\operatorname{Id}&\alpha\operatorname{Id}\\
\alpha\operatorname{Id}&\operatorname{Id}\end{matrix}\right).}

(397)

We have that
\det(\Sigma)=2^{2d}(1-\alpha^{2})^{-d}
using Schur complement

(
Petersen et al., 2008
, Section 9.1.2)
. Hence we get that for any

x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{p(x_{0},x_{1})=\uppi^{-d}(1-\alpha^{2})^{d/2}\exp[-\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x_{0},x_{1}\rangle-\left\|x_{1}\right\|^{2}].}

(398)

In what follows, we denote
C=\uppi^{d}(1-\alpha^{2})^{-d/2}
. Similarly, we
get that
\mu_{0}=\mu_{1}
and that they admit the density
p_{0}
w.r.t. the
Lebesgue measure given for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

p_{0}(x)=\uppi^{-d/2}(1-\alpha^{2})^{d/2}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{2}(1-\alpha^{2})].

(399)

In what follows, we denote
C_{0}=\uppi^{d/2}(1-\alpha^{2})^{-d/2}
. In this
case note that
\mu
admits a density w.r.t.
\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}
given for any

x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

h(x_{0},x_{1})=(\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}(\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1}))(x_{0},x_{1})=(1-\alpha^{2})^{-d/2}\exp[-\alpha^{2}\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2}-2\alpha\langle x_{0},x_{1}\rangle-\alpha^{2}\left\|x_{1}\right\|^{2}].

(400)

Remark that

p_{\textup{prior}}=p_{\textup{data}}=q
with for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

q(x)=\uppi^{-d/2}\beta^{d/2}\exp[-\beta\left\|x\right\|^{2}]
.
We have for any
x_{1},x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

p_{1|0}(x_{1}|x_{0})=p(x_{0},x_{1})/p_{0}(x_{0})=\uppi^{-d/2}(1-\alpha^{2})^{d/2}\exp[-\alpha^{2}\left\|x_{0}\right\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x_{0},x_{1}\rangle-\left\|x_{1}\right\|^{2}].

(401)

Hence, we have that
1
holds and the IPF sequence is
well-defined and converges using
Section

3.4
. In what follows
we start to show that
\alpha
controls the amount of information shared by the
two marginals
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
,
i.e.
the mutual information. More precisely
we have the following result.

Proposition 44
.

For any
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
we have
\operatorname{KL}(\mu|\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1})=-(d/2)\log(1-\alpha^{2})
.

Proof.

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

(\mathrm{d}\mu/(\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}))(x,y)=\exp[-\alpha^{2}\|x\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x,y\rangle-\alpha^{2}\|y\|^{2}](1-\alpha^{2})^{-d/2}.

(402)

We have that

\textstyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(-\alpha^{2}\left\|x\right\|^{2}-\alpha^{2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}+2\alpha\langle x,y\rangle)\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y)=0.}

(403)

Hence,
\operatorname{KL}(\mu|\mu_{0}\otimes\mu_{1})=-(d/2)\log(1-\alpha^{2})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

In what follows,
we denote by
(\pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the IPFP sequence, defined for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
we have for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x,y)=a_{n}(x)b_{n}(y)h(x,y),\qquad(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n+1}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x,y)=a_{n+1}(x)b_{n}(y)h(x,y),}

(404)

where for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\textstyle{a_{n+1}(x)=(\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{0})(x)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)b_{n}(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y)\right)^{-1},}

(405)

\displaystyle\textstyle{b_{n+1}(x)=(\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{1})(y)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x,y)a_{n+1}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)\right)^{-1}.}

(406)

We now turn to the proof of the
Section

G.1
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
and
\beta>1
. We have for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{0})(x)=\exp[(1-\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})\|x\|^{2}]/C_{2},\qquad(\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{1})(y)=\exp[(1-\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})\|y\|^{2}]/C_{2},

(407)

with
C_{2}=C_{1}/C_{0}
with

C_{1}=\uppi^{d/2}\beta^{d/2}
. For any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\gamma\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\nu_{0}/\mathrm{d}\mu_{0})(x)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\gamma\left\|y\right\|^{2}]h(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(y)\right)^{-1}}

(408)

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle{=(C_{0}C_{2})^{-1}C\exp[(1-\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})\left\|x\right\|^{2}]\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\gamma\left\|y\right\|^{2}-\left\|y-\alpha x\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}y\right)}^{-1}

(409)

\displaystyle\qquad=(C_{0}C_{2})^{-1}C\exp[(1-\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})\left\|x\right\|^{2}]

(410)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\textstyle{\times\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-(\gamma+1)\left\|y-\alpha/(\gamma+1)x\right\|^{2}-\alpha^{2}(1-1/(\gamma+1))\left\|x\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}y\right)^{-1}}

(411)

\displaystyle\qquad=(C_{0}C_{2})^{-1}C\exp[(1-\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{2}\gamma/(\gamma+1))\left\|x\right\|^{2}]

(412)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\textstyle{\times\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-(\gamma+1)\left\|y-\alpha/(\gamma+1)x\right\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}y\right)^{-1}}

(413)

\displaystyle\qquad=(C_{0}C_{2}\tilde{C}_{\gamma})^{-1}C\exp[(1-\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}/(\gamma+1))\left\|x\right\|^{2}],

(414)

with
\tilde{C}_{\gamma}=\pi^{d/2}(1+\gamma)^{-d/2}
.
Note that
a_{0}=b_{0}=1
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that for any

y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

b_{n}(y)=\exp[-\gamma_{2n}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]/C_{2n}
with

\gamma_{2n}\geq 0
and
C_{2n}>0
then we have for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

a_{n+1}(x)=(C_{0}C_{2}\tilde{C}_{\gamma_{2n}})^{-1}CC_{2n}\exp[-(1-\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}/(\gamma_{2n}+1))\left\|x\right\|^{2}]=\exp[-\gamma_{2n+1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}]/C_{2n+1},

(415)

with

\gamma_{2n+1}=\beta^{2}-1+\alpha^{2}/(\gamma_{2n}+1),\qquad(C_{0}C_{2}\tilde{C}_{\gamma_{2n}})/(CC_{2n})=C_{2n+1}.

(416)

Similarly, if we assume that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

a_{n+1}(x)=\exp[-\gamma_{2n+1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}]/C_{2n+1}
with

\gamma_{2n+1}\geq 0
and
C_{2n+1}>0
then we have for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle b_{n+1}(y)

\displaystyle=(C_{0}C_{2}\tilde{C}_{\gamma_{2n+1}})^{-1}(CC_{2n+1})\exp[-(1-\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}/(\gamma_{2n+1}+1))\left\|y\right\|^{2}]

(417)

\displaystyle=\exp[-\gamma_{2n+2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]/C_{2n+2},

(418)

with

\gamma_{2n+2}=\beta^{2}-1+\alpha^{2}/(\gamma_{2n+1}+1),\qquad(C_{0}C_{2}\tilde{C}_{\gamma_{2n+1}})/(CC_{2n+1})=C_{2n+2}.

(419)

Combining this result, (
416
) and using the recursion principle we
get that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

a_{n+1}(x)=\exp[-\gamma_{2n+1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}]/C_{2n+1},\qquad b_{n+1}(y)=\exp[-\gamma_{2n+2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]/C_{2n+2}.

(420)

The recursion can be extended to
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
by setting

\gamma_{-1}=\gamma_{0}=0
and
C_{-1}=C_{0}=1
. Therefore, for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\gamma_{n+1}=\beta^{2}-1+\alpha^{2}/(\gamma_{n}+1).

(421)

We now study the convergence of the sequence
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. By
recursion, we have that for any
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, if

\gamma_{k}\geq\gamma_{\ell}
then for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m
even we have

\gamma_{m+k}\geq\gamma_{m+\ell}
and for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m
odd we
have
\gamma_{m+k}\leq\gamma_{m+\ell}
. We have
\gamma_{0}=0
and

\gamma_{1}=\beta^{2}+\alpha^{2}-1,\qquad\gamma_{2}=\beta^{2}-1+\alpha^{2}/(\beta^{2}+\alpha^{2}).

(422)

We divide the rest of the proof into three parts.

(a)

First assume that
\beta^{2}>1-\alpha^{2}
. Using (
422
) we
have that
\gamma_{1}>\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{2}>\gamma_{0}
. Therefore, we
obtain that
(\gamma_{2n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-decreasing, that

(\gamma_{2n+1})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-increasing and that for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq\gamma_{2n}\leq\gamma_{2n+1}\leq\gamma_{1}
. Therefore,
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges and we denote

\gamma^{\star}
its limit. We have

\gamma^{\star}=\beta^{2}-1+\alpha^{2}/(\gamma^{\star}+1)
. Hence,

\gamma^{\star}
is a root of
X^{2}+(2-\beta^{2})X+1-\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}
. We get that
\gamma^{\star}=\gamma_{0}^{\star}
or

\gamma^{\star}=\gamma_{1}^{\star}
with

\gamma_{0}^{\star}=\beta^{2}/2-1-(1/2)(\beta^{4}+4\alpha^{2})^{1/2},\qquad\gamma_{1}^{\star}=\beta^{2}/2-1+(1/2)(\beta^{4}+4\alpha^{2})^{1/2},

(423)

\gamma_{0}^{\star},\gamma_{1}^{\star}
are non-decreasing function of
\beta
. We get
that for any
\beta\geq 0
such that
\beta^{2}\geq 1-\alpha^{2}
,

\gamma_{0}^{\star}\leq 0
. In addition, we have
\gamma_{1}^{\star}=0
for

\beta^{2}=1-\alpha^{2}
, hence for any
\beta\geq 0
such that

\beta^{2}\geq 1-\alpha^{2}
,
\gamma_{1}^{\star}\geq 0
. Since
\gamma^{\star}\geq 0

we have

\gamma^{\star}=-1+\beta^{2}/2+(1/2)(\beta^{4}+4\alpha^{2})^{1/2}.

(424)

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
\xi_{n}=\gamma_{n}-\gamma^{\star}
and

\tau=\gamma^{\star}+1
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Since

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\xi_{n}=0
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\left|\xi_{n}\right|/\tau\leq\varepsilon
. Using (
421
), we obtain that
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\textstyle{|\xi_{n+1}|=\alpha^{2}|1/(\gamma_{n}+1)-\tau^{-1}|=(\alpha^{2}/\tau)|1-(\xi_{n}/\tau+1)^{-1}|\leq(\alpha/\tau)^{2}|\xi_{n}|/(1-\varepsilon).}

(425)

Hence, we get
that for any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,1\right)
, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\left|\xi_{n}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon}\kappa^{n},\qquad\kappa=(\alpha/(\tau(1-\varepsilon)^{1/2}))^{2}.

(426)

Note that
\tau>\alpha
using (
424
) and

\kappa\in\left(0,1\right)
if
\varepsilon<1-\alpha/\tau
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle=a_{n+1}(x)b_{n+1}(y)=\exp[-\gamma_{2n+1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}-\gamma_{2n+2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]/(C_{2n+1}C_{2n+2})

(427)

\displaystyle=\exp[-\gamma_{2n+1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}-\gamma_{2n+2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]/(\tilde{C}\tilde{C}_{\gamma_{2n+1}}),

(428)

with
\tilde{C}=C_{0}C_{2}/C
.
Therefore we obtain that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\Phi^{\star}(x,y)=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\Phi_{n}(x,y)
exists and we have

\Phi^{\star}(x,y)=\exp[-\gamma^{\star}\left\|x\right\|^{2}-\gamma^{\star}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]/(\tilde{C}\tilde{C}_{\gamma^{\star}}).

(429)

Using this result we get that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n}/\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star})(x,y)

\displaystyle=\exp[-\xi_{2n+1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}-\xi_{2n+2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]C_{\gamma^{\star}}/C_{\gamma_{2n+1}}

(430)

\displaystyle=\exp[-\xi_{2n+1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}-\xi_{2n+2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]\left\{(1+\gamma_{2n+1})/(1+\gamma^{\star})\right\}^{-d/2}

(431)

\displaystyle=\exp[-\xi_{2n+1}\left\|x\right\|^{2}-\xi_{2n+2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}]\left\{1+\xi_{2n+1}/(1+\gamma^{\star})\right\}^{-d/2}.

(432)

Therefore we have for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\log\left((\mathrm{d}\pi^{2n}/\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star})(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\xi_{2n+1}\right|\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\left|\xi_{2n+2}\right|\left\|y\right\|^{2}+(d/2)\left|\log\left(1+\xi_{2n+1}/(1+\gamma^{\star})\right)\right|

(433)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\xi_{2n+1}\right|\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\left|\xi_{2n+2}\right|\left\|y\right\|^{2}+(d/2)\left|\xi_{2n+1}\right|.

(434)

Therefore we obtain that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\pi^{n})\leq(d/2)(\beta^{-2}\left|\xi_{2n+1}\right|+\beta^{-2}\left|\xi_{2n+2}\right|+\left|\xi_{2n+1}\right|).

(435)

A similar inequality holds for
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\pi^{n})
. Therefore we get
that for any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,1-\alpha/\tau\right)
there exists

C_{\varepsilon}\geq 0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\pi^{n})\leq C_{\varepsilon}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{2n},

(436)

with

\displaystyle\kappa_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\alpha/(\tau(1-\varepsilon)^{1/2})=(2\alpha)/((\beta^{2}+(\beta^{4}+4\alpha^{2})^{1/2})(1-\varepsilon)^{1/2})

(437)

\displaystyle\leq\rho/((1+(1+\rho^{2})^{1/2})(1-\varepsilon)^{1/2}).

(438)

Let
\varepsilon<1-(1+\rho)/(1+(1+\rho^{2})^{1/2})
. Then we get that

\kappa_{\varepsilon}\leq\kappa
which concludes the first part of the proof.

(b)

If
\beta^{2}=1-\alpha^{2}
then the IPF is stationary since
the IPF leaves
\mu
invariant.

(c)

Finally we assume that
\beta^{2}<1-\alpha^{2}
. Using (
422
) we
have that
\gamma_{1}<\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{2}<\gamma_{0}
since

\beta^{2}<1-\alpha^{2}
. Therefore, we obtain that

(\gamma_{2n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-increasing, that

(\gamma_{2n+1})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-decreasing and that for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\geq\gamma_{2n}\geq\gamma_{2n+1}\geq\gamma_{1}
. Therefore,
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges and we denote

\gamma^{\star}
its limit. We have

\gamma^{\star}=\beta^{2}-1+\alpha^{2}/(\gamma^{\star}+1)
. Hence,

\gamma^{\star}
is a root of
X^{2}+(2-\beta^{2})X+1-\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}
. We
recall that the two roots of this polynomial are given by

\gamma_{0}^{\star}=\beta^{2}/2-1-(1/2)(\beta^{4}+4\alpha^{2})^{1/2},\qquad\gamma_{1}^{\star}=\beta^{2}/2-1+(1/2)(\beta^{4}+4\alpha^{2})^{1/2}.

(439)

We have

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{0}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\beta^{2}+\alpha^{2}-1-\beta^{2}/2+1-(1/2)(\beta^{4}+4\alpha^{2})^{1/2}}

(440)

\displaystyle=(1/2)(\beta^{2}+2\alpha^{2}-(\beta^{4}+4\alpha^{2})^{1/2})\geq 0.

(441)

Since
\gamma_{3}>\gamma_{1}
we get that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 3
,

\gamma_{n}\geq\gamma_{3}>\gamma_{0}^{\star}
. Therefore

\gamma^{\star}>\gamma_{0}^{\star}
and then
\gamma^{\star}=\gamma_{1}^{\star}
. The
rest of the proof is similar to the case where
\beta^{2}>1-\alpha^{2}
.

∎

G.2
Convergence to ground-truth

In this section, we provide an analytic form for the Schrödinger bridge in a Gaussian
context. Let
\nu_{0}
be the
d
dimensional Gaussian distribution with mean
-a

(with
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
) and covariance matrix
\mathbf{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
Similarly, let
\nu_{1}
be the one-dimensional Gaussian distribution with mean

a
and covariance matrix
\mathbf{I}
. We consider the reference distribution

\pi^{0}
such that
\pi^{0}_{0}=\nu_{0}
and for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi_{1|0}^{0}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x,y)=(2\uppi)^{-d/2}\exp[-\|x-y\|^{2}/2],}

(442)

where
\lambda
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
Note that
\pi_{1|0}^{0}
can be obtained by running a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion up to time

1
. We consider the following Schrödinger bridge problem

\pi^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{0})\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}),\ \pi_{0}=\nu_{0},\pi_{1}=\nu_{1}\}.

(443)

Before giving the analytic solution of the SB problem we consider the following algebraic lemma.

Lemma 45
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and

M=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{I}&A\\
A^{\top}&\mathbf{I}\end{matrix}\right),\qquad M^{S}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{I}&(A+A^{\top})/2\\
(A+A^{\top})/2&\mathbf{I}\end{matrix}\right),

(444)

such that
M
is symmetric and positive semi-definite. Then
\det(M)\leq\det(M^{S})
.

Proof.

Let
M^{\mathrm{up}}=M
and
M^{\mathrm{down}}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{I}&A^{\top}\\
A&\mathbf{I}\end{matrix}\right)
. Since
M^{\mathrm{up}}
is symmetric and real-valued,
M^{\mathrm{up}}
is
diagonalizable. Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\theta\geq 0
such that

M^{\mathrm{up}}X=\theta X
with
X=(x,y)
. Let
Y=(y,x)
. We have

M^{\mathrm{down}}Y=\theta Y
. Hence
M^{\mathrm{down}}
is symmetric,
positive semi-definite and

\det(M^{\mathrm{up}})=\det(M^{\mathrm{down}})
. Hence using that

M\mapsto\log(\det(M))
is concave on the space of symmetric positive
semi-definite matrices we get that

\det(M^{\mathrm{up}})\leq\det((M^{\mathrm{up}}+M^{\mathrm{down}})/2)=\det(M^{S})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 46
.

The solution to (
443
) exists and
\pi^{\star}
is a Gaussian
distribution with mean
m\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and covariance matrix

\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}
where

\textstyle{m=(-a,a),\qquad\Sigma=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{I}&\beta\mathbf{I}\\
\beta\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}\end{matrix}\right),}

(445)

where
\beta=(-1+\sqrt{5})/2
and
\mathbf{I}
is the
d
-dimensional identity matrix.

Proof.

The fact that
\pi^{\star}
exists and is Gaussian is similar to

Section

G.1
.
\pi^{\star}
has mean
m
since

\pi^{\star}_{i}=\nu_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. Similarly, we have that

\Sigma_{00}=\Sigma_{11}=\mathbf{I}
since
\pi^{\star}_{i}=\nu_{i}
for

i\in\{0,1\}
. We have that
\pi^{0}
admits a density
p^{0}
with
respect to the Lebesgue measure such that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
we have

p^{0}(x,y)\propto\exp[-(1/2)\{2\left\|x\right\|^{2}+\left\|y\right\|^{2}+2\langle a,x\rangle-2\langle x,y\rangle+\left\|a\right\|^{2}\}].

(446)

Hence
\pi^{0}
is a Gaussian distribution with mean
m^{0}
and covariance matrix

\Sigma^{0}
where

\textstyle{m^{0}=(-a,-a),\qquad\Sigma^{0}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}\\
\mathbf{I}&2\mathbf{I}\end{matrix}\right).}

(447)

The Kullback–Leibler divergence between a Gaussian distribution
\pi
, with mean

\tilde{m}
and covariance matrix
\tilde{\Sigma}
, and
\pi^{0}
, with mean
m^{0}
and covariance
\Sigma^{0}
is given by

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{0})=(1/2)\{\log(\det(\Sigma^{0})/\det(\tilde{\Sigma}))-d+\operatorname{Tr}(\left(\Sigma^{0}\right)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma})+(\tilde{m}-m^{0})^{\top}\left(\Sigma^{0}\right)^{-1}(\tilde{m}-m^{0})\}.}

(448)

Assume that
\tilde{m}=(-a,a)
and

\tilde{\Sigma}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{I}&S\\
S^{\top}&\mathbf{I}\end{matrix}\right)
with
S\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that
\tilde{\Sigma}
is
positive semi-definite . Then we have

\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{0})=(1/2)\{-\log(\det(\tilde{\Sigma}))-2\operatorname{Tr}(S)+C\},

(449)

where
C\geq 0
is a constant which does not depend on
\Sigma
. In what follows, let
\tilde{\Sigma^{\prime}}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{I}&(S+S^{\top})/2\\
(S+S^{\top})/2&\mathbf{I}\end{matrix}\right)
and denote
\pi
the distribution with mean

\tilde{m}
and covariance matrix
\tilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}
. Using
Section

G.2
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\prime}|\pi^{0})

\displaystyle=(1/2)\{-\log(\det(\tilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}))-2\operatorname{Tr}(S)+C\}

(450)

\displaystyle\leq(1/2)\{-\log(\det(\tilde{\Sigma}))-2\operatorname{Tr}(S)+C\}=\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{0}).

(451)

Hence, we can assume that
S=S^{\top}
and therefore (since
S
is real-valued),

S
is diagonalizable. Let
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
the eigenvalues of
S
.
Using Schur complements
(
Petersen et al., 2008
, Section 9.1.2)
we have

\textstyle{\det(\tilde{\Sigma})=\det(\mathbf{I}-S^{2})=\det(\mathbf{I}-S)\det(\mathbf{I}+S)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}(1-\lambda_{i}^{2}).}

(452)

Therefore we have that for any
\lambda\in\left(0,1\right)

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\pi^{0})=(1/2)\sum_{i=1}^{d}f(\beta_{i})+C,\qquad f(\lambda)=-\log(1-\lambda^{2})-2\lambda.}

(453)

Hence we get that
\Sigma_{0,1}=\beta\mathbf{I}
with
\beta=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{I}f
, where

I=\left(-1,0\right)\cup\left(0,1\right)
. We have that
f^{\prime}(\beta)=0
if and only if

\beta=(-1+\sqrt{5})/2
or
\beta=-(1+\sqrt{5})/2
. We conclude the
proof using that
\beta\in I
.
∎

Appendix H
Continuous-time Schrödinger bridges

In this section, we prove
Section

3.5
in

Section

H.1
and draw a link between the potential
approach to Schrödinger bridges and DSB in continuous time in

Section

H.2
.

H.1
Proof of
Section

3.5

We recall the continuous Schrödinger problem is given by

\textstyle{\Pi^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\Pi|\mathbb{P})\,:\;\Pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}),\ \Pi_{0}=p_{\textup{data}},\ \Pi_{T}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\},\quad T=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\gamma_{k+1}.}

(454)

In this section, we prove
Section

3.5
.
We start with the following property which
can be found in
(
Léonard, 2014b
, Proposition 2.3, Proposition 2.10)
and
establishes basic properties of dynamic continuous Schrödinger bridges.

Proposition 47
.

The solution to (
454
) exists if and only if the solution to
the static Schrödinger bridge
exists. In addition, if the solution exists
and
\mathbb{P}
is Markov then the Schrödinger bridge is Markov.

We now turn to the proof of
Section

3.5
.
First we highlight that
(\Pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is well-defined since its static
counterpart
(\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is well-defined using
Section

F.1.1
.
We only prove that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(\Pi^{2n+1})^{R}
is the path measure
associated with the process
(\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{2n+1})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
such that

\mathbf{Y}_{0}^{2n+1}
has distribution
p_{\textup{prior}}
and satisfies

\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{2n+1}=b^{n}_{T-t}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2n+1})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}.}

(455)

The proof for
\Pi^{2n+2}
is similar. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that

\Pi^{2n}
is the path measure associated with the process

(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2n})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
such that
\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2n}
has
distribution
p_{\textup{data}}
and satisfies

\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2n}=f^{n}_{t}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2n})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}.}

(456)

We have that

\textstyle{\Pi^{2n+1}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\Pi|\Pi^{2n})\,:\;\Pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}),\ \Pi_{T}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\}.}

(457)

Let
\phi=\mathrm{proj}_{T}
such that for any
\omega\in\mathcal{C}
,

\mathrm{proj}_{T}(\omega)=\omega_{T}
. Using
Section

D.1
we
get that for any
\Pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
we have

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}(\Pi|\Pi^{2n})=\operatorname{KL}(\Pi_{T}|\Pi_{T}^{2n})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\operatorname{KL}(\mathrm{K}(x,\cdot)|\mathrm{K}^{2n}(x,\cdot))\mathrm{d}\Pi_{T}(x),}

(458)

where
\mathrm{K}
and
\mathrm{K}^{2n}
are the disintegrations of
\Pi
and

\Pi^{2n}
with respect to
\phi
. Therefore, we get that

\Pi^{2n+1}=p_{\textup{prior}}\mathrm{K}^{2n}
.
Since
\operatorname{KL}(\Pi^{2n}|\mathbb{Q})<+\infty
and
\Pi^{2n}
is Markov, Using

(
Cattiaux et al., 2021
, Theorem 4.9)
we get that
(\Pi^{2n})^{R}=\Pi_{T}\mathrm{K}^{2n}
satisfies the
martingale problem associated with the diffusion

\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}^{2n}_{t}=\left\{-f^{n}_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}^{2n}_{t})+2\nabla\log p_{T-t}^{n}(\mathbf{Y}^{2n}_{t})\right\}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}.}

(459)

Since
\Pi^{2n+1}=p_{\textup{prior}}\mathrm{K}^{2n}
we get that
\Pi^{2n+1}
also
satisfies the martingale problem associated with (
459
) and
is Markov which concludes the proof by recursion.

H.2
IPF in continuous time and potentials

First, we recall that the IPF
(\Pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

with
\Pi^{0}=\mathbb{P}
associated with (
9
) and for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\Pi^{2n+1}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\Pi|\Pi^{2n})\,:\;\Pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}),\ \Pi_{T}=p_{\textup{prior}}\right\},}

(460)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\Pi^{2n+2}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\operatorname*{arg\,min}\left\{\operatorname{KL}(\Pi|\Pi^{2n+1})\,:\;\Pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C}),\ \Pi_{0}=p_{\textup{data}}\right\}.}

(461)

In this section, we draw a link between our time-reversal approach and the
potential approach in continuous time. More precisely, we explicit an identity
between the two in
Section

H.2
.

Proposition 48
.

Assume
1
and that there exist

\mathbb{M}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{C})
,
U\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,
C\geq 0

such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\operatorname{KL}(\Pi^{n}|\mathbb{M})<+\infty
,

\langle x,\nabla U(x)\rangle\geq-C(1+\|x\|^{2})
and
\mathbb{M}
is
associated with

\textstyle{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}=-\nabla U(\mathbf{X}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},}

(462)

with
\mathbf{X}_{0}
distributed according to the invariant distribution of
(
34
). For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\{\varphi_{t}^{n,\star},\varphi_{t}^{n,\circ}\}_{t=0}^{T}
such that for any

t\in\left[0,T\right]
,
\varphi_{T}^{n,\star}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\varphi_{0}^{n,\circ}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, for any
x_{0},x_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\varphi_{T}^{\star,n}(x_{T})=p_{\textup{prior}}(x_{T})/p_{T}^{n}(x_{T}),\qquad\varphi_{0}^{\circ,n}(x_{0})=p_{\textup{data}}(x_{0})/p_{0}^{n+1}(x_{0}),

(463)

and for any
t\in\left(0,T\right)
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\varphi_{t}^{\star,n}(x_{t})=\int\varphi_{T}^{\star,n}(x_{T})p_{T|t}^{n}(x_{T}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{T},\qquad\varphi_{t}^{\circ,n+1}(x)=\int\varphi_{0}^{\circ,n+1}(x_{0})q^{n}_{0|t}(x_{0}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{0}.}

(464)

We have for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in\left[0,T\right]
and
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

q_{t}^{n}(x_{t})=p_{t}^{n}(x_{t})\varphi_{t}^{\star,n}(x_{t}),\qquad p_{t}^{n+1}(x_{t})=q_{t}^{n}(x_{t})\varphi_{t}^{\circ,n}(x_{t}).

(465)

In particular, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(a)

(\Pi^{2n+1})^{R}
is associated with
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{2n+1}=b^{n}_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{2n+1})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
with
\mathbf{Y}_{0}^{2n+1}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
;

(b)

\Pi^{2n+2}
is associated with

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2n+2}=f^{n+1}_{t}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2n+2})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}
with
\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2n+2}\sim p_{\textup{data}}
;

with for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in\left(0,T\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle{f_{t}^{n}(x)=f(x)+2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}^{\star,n}(x),\ b_{t}^{n}(x)=-f(x)+\nabla\log p_{t}^{0}(x)+2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}^{\circ,n}(x).}

(466)

Proof.

We only prove that (
465
) holds. Then
(
466
) is a direct consequence of
(
465
) and
Section

3.5
. Let

n\in\mathbb{N}
. Similarly to the proof of
Section

D.2
,
there exists
\varphi_{T}^{\star,n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any

\{\omega_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}\in\mathcal{C}
we have

(\mathrm{d}\Pi^{2n+1}/\mathrm{d}\Pi^{2n})(\{\omega_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T})=\varphi_{T}^{\star,n}(\omega_{T}).

(467)

Note that as in
Section

3.5
, that for any

s,t\in\left[0,T\right]
,
\Pi^{2n+1}_{s,t}
admits a positive density w.r.t the Lebesgue
measure denoted
q^{n}_{s,t}
and
\Pi^{2n}_{s,t}
admits a positive density w.r.t the Lebesgue
measure denoted
p^{n}_{s,t}
. Combining this result and (
467
), we
get that for any
t\in\left[0,T\right]
and
x_{t},x_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

q_{t,T}^{n}(x_{t},x_{T})=p_{t,T}^{n}(x_{t},x_{T})\varphi_{T}^{\star,n}(x_{T}).

(468)

We have that for any
t\in\left[0,T\right]

\textstyle{q_{t}(x_{t})=p_{t}^{n}(x_{t})\int\varphi_{T}^{\star,n}(x_{T})p_{T|t}^{n}(x_{T}|x_{t})\mathrm{d}x_{T}=p_{t}^{n}(x_{t})\varphi_{t}^{\star,n}(x_{t}).}

(469)

The proof for that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in\left[0,T\right]
and

x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
p_{t}^{n+1}(x_{t})=q_{t}^{n}(x_{t})\varphi_{t}^{\circ,n}(x_{t})
,
is similar.
∎

The link between the two formulations is explicit in
(
465
). Then, (
466
) is a
straightforward consequence of (
465
) and should be
compared with
Section

H.1
. Another proof of

Section

H.2
make use of a generalization of
(
465
) to joint densities and use the fact that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Pi^{n+1}
is a Doob
h
-transform of
\Pi^{n}
(see
(
Rogers and Williams, 2000
, Paragraph 39.1)
for a definition). Note that this relationship
between the potential and the density of the half-bridge is not new. In
particular, a similar version of this equation can be found in

Bernton et al., 2019
. In
Finlay et al., 2020
, the authors establish a
similar relationship in the case of the full Schrödinger bridge.

H.3
Likelihood computation for Schrödinger bridges

We provide here details on the likelihood computation of generative
models obtained with Schrödinger bridges. Under the conditions of

(
Léonard, 2011
, Theorem 4.12)
, we define
(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
the
diffusion associated with
\Pi^{\star}
, see (
454
) as well as
its time reversal,
(\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
. There exist

f^{\star},b^{\star}:\ \left[0,T\right]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
and
(\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}

are weak solutions to the following SDEs

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\star}=f^{\star}_{t}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\star})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t},\qquad\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{\star}=b^{\star}_{T-t}(\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{\star})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t}.

(470)

We assume that for any
t\in\left[0,T\right]
there exists

p_{t}^{\star}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}_{t}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)=p_{t}^{\star}(x)
. In addition, we assume that

p^{\star}\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\left[0,T\right]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}_{+})
. In this case, we
have that
\Pi^{\star}
is also associated with the process

(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
associated with the ODE

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\star}_{t}=\{f^{\star}_{t}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\star})-\nabla\log p_{t}^{\star}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\star})\}\mathrm{d}t,

(471)

and
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{T}^{\star}
has distribution
p_{\textup{prior}}
; see e.g.
(
Song et al., 2021
, Section A)
.
Since
(\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
is the time-reversal of

(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
we have that for any
t\in\left[0,T\right]

and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

b^{\star}_{t}(x)=-f^{\star}_{t}(x)+2\nabla\log p_{t}^{\star}(x).

(472)

Therefore, we get that
(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
is associated with the ODE

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\star}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\left(f^{\star}_{t}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\star})-b^{\star}_{t}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\star})\right)\mathrm{d}t.

(473)

Using this result we can compute the log-likelihood of the model using the
instantaneous change of variable formula
(
Chen et al., 2018
)
, see also

(
Song et al., 2021
, Appendix D.2)

\textstyle{\log p_{\textup{data}}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{0}^{\star})=\log p_{\textup{prior}}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{T}^{\star})+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{div}(f^{\star}_{t}-b^{\star}_{t})(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\star})\mathrm{d}t\;.}

(474)

As in
Song et al., 2021
, we can use the Skilling–Hutchinson trace estimator
to compute the divergence operator
(
Skilling, 1989
;
Hutchinson, 1989
)
. In practice, we
discretize the dynamics of
(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
and use the
network
B_{\beta^{n}}
obtained with the last iterate of
Algorithm

1

and solve the ODE backward in time, recalling that
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{T}^{\star}
has
distribution
p_{\textup{prior}}
. Similarly, we can define

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}=\{b^{\star}_{T-t}(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\star})-\nabla\log p_{T-t}^{\star}(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\star})\}\mathrm{d}t,

(475)

and
\mathbf{Y}_{0}^{\star}
has distribution
p_{\textup{prior}}
. Similarly to (
473
), we get that
(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
is associated with the ODE

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\left(b^{\star}_{T-t}(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\star})-f^{\star}_{T-t}(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\star})\right)\mathrm{d}t.

(476)

Similarly to (
477
), we have

\textstyle{\log p_{\textup{data}}(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{T}^{\star})=\log p_{\textup{prior}}(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{0}^{\star})+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{div}(b^{\star}_{T-t}-f^{\star}_{T-t})(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\star})\mathrm{d}t\;.}

(477)

In practice, we discretize the dynamics of
(\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}

and use the networks
F_{\alpha^{n}}
,
B_{\beta^{n}}
obtained with the last iterate of

Algorithm

1
and solve the ODE forward in time, recalling that
\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{0}^{\star}
has distribution
p_{\textup{prior}}
. Note that in this case, we solve the ODE forward in time contrary to
Durkan and Song, 2021
.

Appendix I
Training Techniques

In this section we present some practical guidelines for the implementation of DSB, based on
Algorithm

1
. We emphasize that, contrarily to previous approaches
Song et al., 2021
;
Song and Ermon, 2020
;
Ho et al., 2020
;
Dhariwal and Nichol, 2021
, we do not weight the loss functions as we do not notice any improvement. Let

I\subset\{0,N-1\}\times\{1,M\}
. We define the generalized losses

\hat{\ell}_{n,I}^{b}
and
\hat{\ell}_{n,I}^{f}
given by

\displaystyle\textstyle{\hat{\ell}^{b}_{n,I}(\beta)=M^{-1}\sum_{(k,j)\in I}\|B_{\beta}(k+1,X^{j}_{k+1})-(X^{j}_{k+1}+F^{n}_{k}(X^{j}_{k+1})-F^{n}_{k}(X^{j}_{k}))\|^{2}},

(478)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\hat{\ell}^{f}_{n+1,I}(\alpha)=M^{-1}\sum_{(k,j)\in I}\|F_{\alpha}(k,X^{j}_{k})-(X^{j}_{k}+B^{n}_{k+1}(X^{j}_{k+1})-B^{n}_{k+1}(X^{j}_{k}))\|^{2}}.

(479)

We first describe three techniques to compute these losses, then further methods to improve performance.

Technique 1
.

Simulated Trajectory

The losses (
478
) and
(
479
) may be computed by simulating diffusion
trajectories as described in
Algorithm

1
. For each sample
j\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
the skeleton of points in the sampled trajectory,
\{X_{k}^{j}\}_{k}
, will be correlated hence only a single uniformly sampled time-step per sample is used to compute the loss per gradient step. In addition, after the initial DSB iteration, simulating the diffusion trajectory involves computationally heavy neural network operations per diffusion step.

Technique 2
.

Closed Form Sampling

Since
f_{\alpha}^{0}(x)=-\alpha x
, with fixed
\alpha
, it is
not necessary to compute full trajectories for the first IPF iteration and one
may sample points along the trajectory in closed-form by sampling from a Gaussian distribution with appropriate mean and covariance. This technique also improves the computational speed of the first DSB iteration.

Technique 3
.

Cached Trajectory

After the initial DSB iterations it is not possible perform closed form sampling as per
2
. Simulating the full diffusion trajectory is both wasteful and expensive as described in
1
. In order to obtain a speed-up we consider a cached-version of
Algorithm

1
given by
Algorithm

3
which entails storing and then resampling diffusion trajectories. Resampled trajectories are then used to compute losses (
478
) and (
479
). The cache may be refreshed at a certain frequency by once again simulating the diffusion. One may tune the cache-size and refresh frequency to available memory. This modification allows for significant speed-up as the trajectories are not
simulated at each training iteration.

Algorithm 3

Cached Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge

1:

for

n\in\{0,\dots,L\}

do

2:

while
not converged
do

3:

Sample and store
\{X^{j}_{k}\}_{k,j=0}^{N,M}
where
X^{j}_{0}\sim p_{\textup{data}}
and
X^{j}_{k+1}=X^{j}_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}f_{\alpha^{n}}(k,X^{j}_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z^{j}_{k+1}

4:

while
not refreshed
do

5:

Sample
I
(uniform in
\{0,N-1\}\times\{1,M\}
)

6:

Compute
\hat{\ell}^{b}_{n,I}(\beta^{n})
using (
478
)

7:

\beta^{n}=\textrm{Gradient Step}(\hat{\ell}^{b}_{n,I}(\beta^{n}))

8:

end

while

9:

end

while

10:

while
not converged
do

11:

Sample
\{X^{j}_{k}\}_{k,j=0}^{N,M}
, where
X^{j}_{N}\sim p_{\textup{prior}}
, and
X^{j}_{k}=X^{j}_{k+1}+\gamma_{k}b_{\beta^{n}}(k,X^{j}_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma_{k+1}}Z^{j}_{k}

12:

while
not refreshed
do

13:

Sample
I
(uniform in
\{0,N-1\}\times\{1,M\}
)

14:

Compute
\hat{\ell}^{f}_{n+1,I}(\alpha^{n+1})
using (
479
)

15:

\alpha^{n+1}=\textrm{Gradient Step}(\hat{\ell}^{f}_{n+1,I}(\alpha^{n+1}))

16:

end

while

17:

end

while

18:

end

for

19:

Output:

(\alpha^{L+1},\beta^{L})

Technique 4
.

Tune Gaussian Prior mean/ variance

The
convergence of the IPF is affected by the mean and covariance matrix of
the target Gaussian. In
Section

J.1
we investigate
possible choices for these values. In practice we recommend to choose the
variance of the Gaussian prior
p_{\textup{prior}}
to be slightly larger than the one of
the target dataset and to choose the mean of
p_{\textup{prior}}
to be equal to the one
of the target dataset. This remark is in accordance with
(
Song and Ermon, 2020
, Technique
1)
.

Technique 5
.

Network Refinement / Fine Tuning

Training large networks from scratch, per DSB iteration, is very expensive. However, from (
309
)-(
310
),

\displaystyle b_{k+1}^{n}(x)=\textstyle{b_{k+1}^{n-1}(x)+2\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{n}(x)-2\nabla\log q_{k}^{n-1}(x),}

(480)

\displaystyle f_{k}^{n}(x)=\textstyle{f_{k}^{n-1}(x)+2\nabla\log q_{k}^{n-1}(x)-2\nabla\log p_{k+1}^{n-1}(x).}

(481)

One may therefore initialize networks at DBS iteration
n
from
n-1
in order
to reduce training time. In future work, we plan to investigate more
sophisticated warm-start approaches through meta-learning.

Technique 6
.

Exponential Moving Average

Similar to

(
Song and Ermon, 2020
, Technique 5)
, we found taking the exponential moving
average of network parameters across training iterations, with rate
0.999
,
improved performance.

Appendix J
Additional Experimental Results and Details

We provide additional examples for the two-dimensional setting in
Section

J.1
. We then turn to higher dimensional generative modeling in

Section

J.2
. Finally, we detail our dataset interpolation
experiments in
Section

J.3
. Code is available here:

https://github.com/JTT94/diffusion_schrodinger_bridge
.

Two-dimensional experiments

In the case of two-dimensional distributions we use a simple
architecture for the networks
f_{\alpha}
and
b_{\beta}
, see

Figure

9
. We use the variational formulation

Section

E.2.2
because our network architecture does not
have a residual structure. To optimize our networks we use ADAM

Kingma and Ba, 2014
with momentum
0.9
and learning rate
10^{-4}
.

. MLPBlock (1a) has shape

, MLPBlock

. The

\gamma_{k}=10^{-2}
and use a batch

. The mean and variance of

Effect of the variance of
p_{\textup{prior}}
on the convergence of DSB. If

has a small variance
\sigma^{2}
(here

in (a) and

, where

is the variance of

iterations of the algorithm.

Finally, since DSB does not require the number of Langevin iterations
N
to be
large, one may question why not use
N=1
in order to derive a
feed-forward generative model. In practice this choice of
N
is not desirable for two reasons.

(a)

Firstly, since
p_{N}
is not a good approximation of
p_{\textup{prior}}
, theoretical results
such as
(
Léger, 2020
, Corollary 1)
indicates that more IPF iterations are needed.

(b)

Second, in our experiments we observe that in order to obtain similar
results to
N=10
with
N=1
we need to substantially increase the size of the
networks, even for a large number of IPF iterations, see
Figure

12
.

J.2
Generative Modeling

Implementation details

We use a reduced version of the U-net architecture from

Nichol and Dhariwal, 2021
for
F_{\alpha}
and
B_{\beta}
, where we set the
number of channels to
64
rather than
128
for computational resource
purposes. We tried the architecture of
Song and Ermon, 2020
, however we
observed worse results in our framework. Although we observed improvement using
the corrector scheme of
Song et al., 2021
, this improvement was
similar to augmenting the number of steps in the Langevin
scheme. We therefore chose to avoid using such techniques altogether because of
the increase in computing time when sampling, often by doubling the number of
passes through the network.

We chose the sequence
\{\gamma_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N}
to be invariant by time reversal,

i.e.
for any
k\in\{0,\dots,N\}
,
\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{N-k}
. In practice,
we assume that
N
is even and let

\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{0}+(2k/N)(\bar{\gamma}-\gamma_{0})
for

k\in\{0,\dots,N/2\}
with
\gamma_{0}=10^{-5}
and

\bar{\gamma}=10^{-1}
. The rest of the sequence is obtained by symmetry.

In the case of the MNIST dataset (dimension
d=28\times 28=784
) we set the
batch size to
128
, the number of samples in the cache to
5\times 10^{4}
with

10
time-points sampled from each trajectory for each sample of
p_{\textup{data}}
. We end up with an
effective cache of size
5\times 10^{5}
. The cache is refreshed each
10^{3}

iterations and the networks are trained for
5\times 10^{3}
iterations. Again we
use the ADAM optimizer with momentum
0.9
and learning rate

10^{-4}
.
p_{\textup{prior}}
is a Gaussian density with zero mean and identity
covariance matrix. We have presented results for varying number of diffusion steps,
N
.

In the case of the CelebA dataset (dimension
d=32\times 32\times 3=3072
)
we set the batch size to
256
, number of steps
N=50
, the number of samples in the cache to
250
with

1
time-point sampled from each trajectory for each sample of
p_{\textup{data}}
. The cache is
refreshed each
10^{2}
iterations and the networks are trained for

5\times 10^{3}
iterations. Again we use the ADAM optimizer with momentum
0.9

and learning rate
10^{-4}
.
p_{\textup{prior}}
is a Gaussian density with zero mean and
identity covariance matrix.

Our results on MNIST and CelebA are computed using up to 4 NVIDIA Tesla V100 from the
Google Cloud Platform.

Additional examples

In this section we present additional examples for our high-dimensional
generative modeling experiments. In
Figure

13
we perform
interpolation in the latent space. More precisely we let
X_{N}^{0}
and
X_{N}^{1}
be
two samples from
p_{\textup{prior}}
. We then compute

X_{N}^{\lambda}=(1-\lambda)X_{N}^{0}+\lambda X_{N}^{1}
for different values of

\lambda\in\left[0,1\right]
. For each value of
\lambda\in\left[0,1\right]
we
associate
X_{0}^{\lambda}
which corresponds to the output sample obtained using
the generative model given by DSB with final condition
X_{N}^{\lambda}
. Note that
in order to obtain a deterministic embedding we fix the Gaussian random
variables used in the sampling. One could also have used the deterministic
embedding used by
Song et al., 2021
,
i.e.
a neural ordinary differential
equation that admits the same marginals as the diffusion thus enabling exact
likelihood computation, see
Section

H.3
for details.

J.3
Dataset interpolation

For the dataset interpolation task we keep the same parameters and architecture
as before except that the number of Langevin steps is increased to
50
steps in
the two-dimensional examples and to
30
steps in the EMNIST/MNIST interpolation
task. We also change the reference dynamics which is chosen to be the one
obtained with the DSB where
p_{\textup{prior}}
is a Gaussian. This choice allows us to
speed up the training of DSB in this setting. Animated plots are available at

project webpage
.

EMNIST/MNIST

In order to perform translation between the dataset of handwritten letters
(EMNIST) and handwritten digits (MNIST) we reduce EMNIST to
5
letters so that
it contains as many classes as MNIST (we distinguish upper-case and lower-case
letters), see
Cohen et al., 2017
for the original dataset.

Figure 17:
Iteration 10 of the IPF with
T=1.5
(
30
diffusions
steps). From left to right:
t=0,0.4,1.25,1.5
.

Two dimensional examples

We present dataset interpolation for a number of classical two-dimensional datasets.

Figure 18:
Dataset interpolation (DSB iteration 9). From left to right:
t=0,0.15,0.30,0.5
.
